1     Fields and Galois Theory
1.1     Basic notions
Let F be a field. We always assume that 0 ̸= 1 in F . In F , there exists a smallest field,
called the prime field in F , which is simply the field generated by 1 under the operations
+, −, ×, ÷. It is either Q or Fp (Z/pZ), depending on whether p.1 is zero or not in F . We
say that F is of characteristic 0 or p accordingly.
    It is an easy, but useful fact that a field has a unique maximal ideal: (0). So if F → A
is a ring homomorphism, where F is a field, then it is either injective, or zero. In practice,
we don’t consider the latter case.
    If we have two fields F , K and F is contained in K, we say that K is an extension of
F . Sometimes write K/F is an extension of fields. We can view K as a vector space over
F , so we can talk about the dimension of K over F , called the degree of the extension
K/F , denoted by [K : F ]. This degree could be well infinite. In the case it is finite, we
call that K/F is a finite extension, otherwise, an infinite extension.

Proposition 1.1.1. Let F ⊂ E ⊂ K be a tower of finite extensions, then

                                     [K : F ] = [K : E][E : F ].

If {u1 , . . . , um } is a basis for K/E and {v1 , . . . , vn } is a basis for E/F , then

                                  {ui vj : 1 ≤ i ≤ m; 1 ≤ j ≤ n}

is a basis for K/F .

Proof. This is an easy exercise.

    Suppose F ⊂ K are fields and S is a subset of K. Let

                    F [S] := ∩{A : A is a subring of K containing F ∪ S}

                    F (S) := ∩{A : A is a subfield of K containing F ∪ S}
i.e. the smallest subring (resp. subfield) containing F ∪ S. F (S) is called the subfield of
E obtained from F by adjoining S, or generated by S over F . It is clear that F [S] is an
integral domain with fraction field F (S). When S is a finite set, say S = {u1 , . . . , un },
we write
                      F [S] = F [u1 , . . . , un ], F (S) = F (u1 , . . . , un ).
    In the case S = {u}, F (u) is called a simple extension of F .. Then, explicitly, F [u] =
{P (u) : P ∈ F [X]}, and F (u) is the set of P (u)/Q(u), where P, Q ∈ F [X] and Q(u) ̸= 0.




                                                   3
1.2     Algebraic extension
Let K/F be a field extension and u ∈ K.

Definition 1.2.1. We say that u is algebraic over F if there exists a non-zero polynomial
P ∈ F [X] such that P (u) = 0. Otherwise, u is called transcendental.
   An extension K/F is called algebraic if every element of K is algebraic over F .

Proposition 1.2.2. (i) Consider the ring homomorphism θu : F [X] → F [u] sending
    X to u. Then u is algebraic if and only if ker(θu ) is non-zero.

 (ii) If u is algebraic, ker(θu ) = (P ) for some unique monic polynomial P ∈ F [X]. It is
      called the minimal polynomial of u.

 (iii) If u is algebraic, then F [u] is in fact a field so that F [u] = F (u) and we have

                                           [F [u] : F ] = deg P.

Proof. (i) If u is algebraic, then P (u) = 0 for some P , so that P ∈ ker(θu ). The converse
is also clear.
    (ii) By (i), ker(θu ) is non-zero, then use that F [X] is a PID.
    (iii) Any non-zero prime ideal of F [X] is maximal, so that F [u] ∼    = F [X]/ ker(θu ) is
                                                        n−1
already a field. It is easy to see that {1, u, . . . , u }, where n = deg P , is a basis of F [u]
over F .

Corollary 1.2.3. Let F ⊂ K be fields and u ∈ K. Then u is algebraic over F if and
only if [F (u) : F ] is finite, if and only if F (u) is algebraic over F .

Proof. It follows from Proposition 1.2.2.

Corollary 1.2.4. Let F ⊂ K be fields and u, v ∈ K. If u, v are algebraic, so are u + v,
u.v and u/v if v ̸= 0.

Proof. Consider the extensions F ⊂ F (u) ⊂ F (u, v). The elements listed above all
belong to F (u, v). Since v is algebraic over F , it is also algebraic over F (u), so that
F (u, v) = F (u)(v) is a finite extension over F (u), hence it is a finite extension over F .
The assertion then follows from Corollary 1.2.3.

Corollary 1.2.5. Let F ⊂ E ⊂ K be a tower of extensions where both K/E and E/F
are algebraic. Then K/F is also algebraic.

Proof. Let u ∈ K. Since it is algebraic over E, there exists P ∈ E[X], say P (X) =
an X n + · · · + a0 , such that P (u) = 0. Set E ′ = F (a0 , . . . , an ), then u is in fact algebraic
over E ′ , hence E ′ (u) is a finite extension over E ′ . On the other hand, all ai are algebraic
over F , so E ′ is a finite extension over F by Corollary 1.2.3. Therefore, E ′ (u) is finite
over F . But F (u) ⊂ E ′ (u), we get F (u) is finite over F , and therefore u is algebraic over
F.

                                                  4
Corollary 1.2.6. If F ⊂ E, E ′ ⊂ K, and E/F, E ′ /F are algebraic extensions, then so is
EE ′ /F .

   Here EE ′ is called the compositum of E, E ′ inside K, it is just the smallest subfield
of K containing E, E ′ . (1)

Proof. An element γ ∈ EE ′ can be written of the form
                             u1 v1 + · · · + un vn
                        γ=                            , ui , u′i ∈ E, vi , vi′ ∈ E ′ .
                             u′1 v1′ + · · · + u′m vm
                                                    ′


The result follows from Corollary 1.2.4.

       Conversely, here is a general method to construct field extensions.

Lemma 1.2.7. Let F be a field and P ∈ F [X] of degree n. Then there is a simple
extension K = F (u) such that P (u) = 0 and [K : F ] ≤ n.

Proof. Choose an irreducible factor of P , say Q. Then F [X]/(Q(X)) is a field extension
of F . Its degree is that of Q, hence ≤ n.

       Next is a general lemma which will be used frequently later.

Lemma 1.2.8. Let F (u)/F be a simple extension with u algebraic of minimal polynomial
P . If E/F is an extension and v ∈ E satisfies P (v) = 0, then there exists a unique
F -embedding ι : F (u) → E such that ι(u) = v.

Proof. The uniqueness is clear. Prove the existence. We know that F (u) = F [u] ∼      =
F [X]/(P ). Now, the universal property of F [X] implies a ring homomorphism F [X] →
F [v] ⊂ E, sending X to v. Since P (v) = 0, this morphism factors through F [X]/(P ). We
thus get a morphism of rings F [u] → E between two fields.


1.3       Splitting fields and normal extensions
Definition 1.3.1. If K/F be an extension and P ∈ F [X], then we say P splits over K
if P factors as
                    P (X) = c(X − u1 ) · · · (X − un ), c ∈ F, ui ∈ K.
                                                                  √
    For example, P (X) = X 2 − 3 ∈ Q[X] splits over the field Q[ 3].

Definition 1.3.2. Let P ∈ F [X] and K be an extension of F . We say that K is a
splitting field of P if K is a minimal field over which P splits.

Theorem 1.3.3. (Existence) Let F be a field and P ∈ F [X] with deg(P ) = n. Then there
is a field K containing F such that K is a splitting field of P over F and [K : F ] ≤ n!.
 (1)
   Attention: when we talk about this, we need to precise the larger field in which we are taking
compositum.


                                                     5
Proof. We do induction on n, and we allow F . If P splits over F , then we take K = F .
This proves the case n = 1. Suppose n > 1, and the result is true for all polynomials of
degree < n.
    By Lemma 1.2.7, there is a finite extension F1 over F of degree ≤ n such that P (u) = 0
for some u ∈ F1 . Namely, viewing P as an element in F1 [X], it decomposes as

                                   P (X) = (X − u)Q(X).

Clearly deg(Q) = n − 1. By the inductive hypothesis applied to Q ∈ F1 [X] (and to the
new field F1 ), there is a splitting field K of Q over F1 [X] of degree ≤ (n − 1)!. Then it is
clear that K is a splitting field of P over F and has degree ≤ n!.

Proposition 1.3.4. (Uniqueness of splitting field). Let α : F → F ′ be an isomorphism
of fields and P ∈ F [X]. If K and K ′ are splitting fields of F of P ∈ F [X], and of
αP ∈ F ′ [X] respectively, then there is a field isomorphism τ : K → K ′ extending α.

Proof. We do induction on [K : F ] =: n (not deg P !); note that a priori we don’t assume
[K ′ : F ] = n. If n = 1, this is ok. Suppose that n > 1 and the result is true for all
splitting fields which are extensions of dimension strictly less than n.
    We may assume that P does not split over F . Choose an irreducible factor P1 of P
(it could be P ) with m = deg P1 > 1 (possible because P does not split). Then, since P ,
hence also P1 , splits over K, P1 has a root in K, say u. But αP1 also has a root in K ′ ,
                                                                             β
say v. Lemma 1.2.8 says that there exists a field embedding K ⊃ F (u) ,→ F (v) ⊂ K ′ ; β
is also surjective, hence is an isomorphism extending α. Using inductive hypothesis, we
get an F -isomorphism K → K ′ extending β.

Algebraic closure
Definition 1.3.5. A field F is said to be algebraically closed if it has no proper algebraic
extension.

Proposition 1.3.6. Let F be a field. The following statements are equivalent.

  (i) F is algebraically closed.

 (ii) Every non-constant P ∈ F [X] factors as a (finite) product of linear factors in F [X].

(iii) Every non-constant P ∈ F [X] has a root in F .

Proof. (i)=⇒(ii) Let P ∈ F [X] and choose an irreducible factor of P , say P ′ . consider
K := F [X]/(P ′ ), then K is a finite extension of F of degree deg P ′ . But F is algebraically
closed, so K = F and deg P ′ = 1 by Proposition 1.2.2(iii), i.e. P ′ = X −u for some u ∈ F .
This being true for every irreducible factor of P , we get the assertion.
    (ii)=⇒(iii) Trivial.
    (iii)=⇒(i) Otherwise, let K be a non-trivial algebraic extension of F . By choosing
u ∈ K\F , we may assume K = F (u) is a simple extension. Let P be the minimal

                                              6
polynomial of u. By assumption, P has a root in F , but this contradicts the fact that P
is irreducible over F .

      The fundamental theorem of algebra says:

Theorem 1.3.7. The field C of complex numbers is algebraically closed.

Definition 1.3.8. An extension K of F is said to be an algebraic closure of F if K is
algebraic over F and K is algebraically closed.

   Remark that C is not an algebraic closure of Q, since C contains numbers, like e, π
which are not algebraic over Q. We denote by Q the algebraic closure contained in C.

Theorem 1.3.9. If F is a field, then F has an algebraic closure and any two algebraic
closures of F are F -isomorphic.

Proof. (Artin) We first construct an extension E of F in which every polynomial in F [X]
of degree ≥ 1 has a root. Let S = {Pλ : λ ∈ Λ} be the set of all monic irreducible
polynomials in F [X]. For each Pλ ∈ S, introduce new variables yλ,1 , . . . , yλ,dλ where
dλ = deg Pλ . Let R = F [yλ,i ] be the polynomial ring over F generated by yλ,i for all
λ ∈ Λ and all i ≤ deg Pλ . Write
                              dλ
                              Y               λ −1
                                             dX
(1)                       Pλ − (X − yλ,i ) =       rλ,j X j ∈ R[X]
                                i=1                j=0

with rλ,j ∈ F [yλ,i ]. Let I be the ideal in R generated by the rλ,j . We claim that I ⊂ R is a
proper ideal of R. If not, I is the unit ideal, then there exists finite {rλ,j } which generate
1. So only a finite number of polynomials are involved, say {Pλ : λ ∈ Λ1 } where Λ1 is a
finite subset of Λ, and let K be a finite extension in which every Pλ splits, using Lemma
1.2.7. Numerate the roots of Pλ as uλ,1 , . . . , uλ,dλ . Now evaluate yλ,i at uλ,i (for λ ∈ Λ1 )
and set yλ,i = 0 (or any value) otherwise, we deduce rλ,j (uλ,j ) = 0 from (1). Since 1 can
be generated by these {rλ,j : λ ∈ Λ1 , 0 ≤ j ≤ dλ − 1}, we get 0 = 1 in K, contradiction.
    The claim implies that I is contained in a maximal ideal m in R (this uses Zorn’s
lemma). Set K := R/m, which is an extension of F .
    It is clear that K is algebraic over F , because K is generated by yλ,i ( mod m) and
every yλ,i ( mod m) is an algebraic element over F , with Pλ being its minimal polynomial
by (1).
    It is left to prove that K itself is algebraically closed. Let K ′ /K be an algebraic
extension and x ∈ K ′ . Then x is also algebraic over F . By construction, its minimal
polynomial splits over K, thus x ∈ K.

      Now we show that algebraic closures are unique up to (non-canonical) isomorphisms.

Lemma 1.3.10. (Extension lemma) Let F be a field and L an algebraically closed field.
Let α : F ,→ L be an embedding. If K is an algebraic extension of F , then there exists an
extension of α to an embedding K ,→ L.

                                               7
   In particular, if K is algebraic closure of F , then get K ,→ L. If moreover, L is
algebraic over F , then K ∼
                          = L.

Proof. Let S be the set of (E, τ ), where F ⊂ E ⊂ K and τ : E ,→ L extending α. If
(E, τ ), (E ′ , τ ′ ) ∈ S, then we say (E, τ ) ≤ (E ′ , τ ′ ) iff E ⊂ E ′ and τ ′ |E = τ . We get a
partial order on S, and one checks that any chain (totally ordered subset) has an upper
bound. By Zorn’s lemma, there exists a maximal element in S, say (E, τ ). We must
have E = K. Otherwise, there is u ∈ K\E, and since u is algebraic over E, we get an
extension of τ : E(u) ,→ L, a contradiction.

   We cite the theorem of Artin-Schreier.

Theorem 1.3.11. If F is a field which is of finite index in F and [F : F ] > 1. Then F
                                                                              √
is a real closed field: [F : F ] = 2 and −1 is not a square in F , and F = F ( −1).

   To study splitting field, it is often convenient to choose an algebraic closure F /F .

Normal extensions
Let F be a field and fix an algebraic closure F . All algebraic extensions of F considered
below will be taken in F .

Definition 1.3.12. An algebraic extension K/F is called normal if for every irreducible
polynomial P ∈ F [X], if it has one root in K, then it splits over K.

Remark 1.3.13. Here it is necessary to consider irreducible polynomials.

    Let u, v ∈ F , we say that u and v are conjugates over F if they have the same minimal
polynomial over F . We thus have the following: F ⊂ K is normal if and only if K is
closed under conjugates, i.e. if u ∈ K, then all conjugates of u also belong to K.
    We show that splitting fields are normal extensions.

Theorem 1.3.14. Let K/F be an algebraic extension. The following statements are
equivalent.

  (i) K is a normal extension of F .

 (ii) K is a splitting field over F of a family of polynomials {Pi ∈ F [X] : i ∈ I}. (In
      other words, K is the minimal field of F over which all Pi splits.)

(iii) Every F -embedding ι : K ,→ F satisfies ι(K) = K.

Proof. (i)=⇒(ii) We choose a set S = {ui : i ∈ I} such that K = F (S); this is always
possible. Let Pi be the minimal polynomial of ui over F for each i ∈ I. Since ui is a root
of Pi and K/F is normal, all roots of Pi are contained in K. Thus each Pi splits over K.
Because K is generated by the roots of Pi , K is a splitting field of {Pi : i ∈ I}.


                                                8
    (ii)=⇒(iii) By assumption, K is generated by the roots of {Pi : i ∈ I}. The F -
embedding ι : E ,→ F is determined by the images of roots of the Pi ’s. It is clear that if
ui is a root of Pi , then ι(ui ) is also a root of Pi , hence ι(ui ) ∈ K.
    (iii)=⇒(i) Let Q be an (arbitray) irreducible polynomial in F [X] with a root u ∈ K.
We need show that Q splits in K. Let v ∈ F be another root of Q. We then get an
F -embedding F (u) ,→ F sending u to v by Lemma 1.2.7. This can be extended to an
F -embedding ι : K ,→ F . By assumption ι(K) = K, in particular v = ι(u) ∈ K.

Corollary 1.3.15. (i) Let F ⊂ E ⊂ K be a tower of algebraic (not necessarily finite)
    extensions. If K/F is normal, then K/E is normal (but E/F not necessarily).

 (ii) If E/F and E ′ /F are normal extensions, then so is EE ′ /F .

Proof. Exercise.

Remark 1.3.16. In general, even if K/E and E/F are both normal, K/F need not be
                                   √             √
normal. For example, F = Q, E = Q( 2) and K = Q( 4 2).


1.4        Separable and purely inseparable extensions
Definition 1.4.1. A polynomial P ∈ F [X] is called separable if it has no multiple roots.
Otherwise it is called inseparable.

   For P ∈ F [X], we have the notion of ‘derivate’: if P = nk=0 ak X k , then
                                                          P

                                                        n
                                                        X
                                               P ′ :=         kak X k−1 .
                                                        k=1


Lemma 1.4.2. Let P ∈ F [X]. Then P has multiple roots in F if and only if (P, P ′ ) ̸= 1.
When P is irreducible, the latter condition is equivalent to P ′ = 0.

Proof. Write P (X) = nk=1 (X − ak ) with ak ∈ F . Then
                     Q

                              n
                              X
                       P′ =         (X − a1 ) · · · (X − ai−1 )(X − ai+1 ) · · · (X − an ).
                              i=1


If it has a multiple root, say a1 = a2 , then X − a1 still divides P ′ , so (P, P ′ ) ̸= 1.(2)
Conversely, if P has no multiple roots, then P ′ (ai ) ̸= 0 for any 1 ≤ i ≤ n, i.e. (P, P ′ ) =
1.

Corollary 1.4.3. If char(F ) = 0, then any irreducible polynomial has only simple roots
in F .

       For an irreducible polynomial P ∈ F [X], the following statements are equivalent:
 (2)
       Strictly speaking, here we only get (P, P ′ ) ̸= 1 in F [X], but we can deduce (P, P ′ ) ̸= 1 in F [X].


                                                          9
 (1) P is inseparable;

 (2) P ′ = 0;

 (3) char(F ) = p, and there exist P1 ∈ F [X] such that P (X) = P1 (X p ) (since kak = 0
     for all 0 ≤ k ≤ d, so that either ak = 0 or p|k).

    We can continue the procedure in (3): if P1 is again inseparable, we get P2 such that
P1 (X) = P2 (X p ), etc. The degree of P being finite, we finally get a separable polynomial
Pe ∈ F [X] such that
                                                     e
                                     P (X) = Pe (X p ).
If we write n = deg P , ns = deg Pe , then n = pe ns . We call ns the separable degree of P ,
and pe the inseparable degree.

Definition 1.4.4. (i) Let K/F be an algebraic extension. An element u ∈ K is called
     separable, if its minimal polynomial is separable. Otherwise, u is called inseparable.

 (ii) The extension K/F is called separable, if every element of K is separable. Other-
      wise, K/F is called inseparable extension.

Example 1.4.5. Consider the function field F = Fp (t), where p is a prime number. Show
that the polynomial P (X) = X p − t ∈ F [X] is irreducible and inseparable.

Lemma 1.4.6. Assume char(F ) = p and let u ∈ F . Then u is separable if and only if
F (u) = F (up ).

Proof. =⇒ we have F ′ := F (up ) ⊂ F (u). Over F (up ), the polynomial X p − up annihilates
u, so the minimal polynomial of u over F ′ divides X p − up = (X − u)p (as char(F ) = p).
But u is separable over F ′ , so its minimal polynomial has no multiple root, hence is just
X − u, i.e. u ∈ F ′ .
    ⇐= Assume that F (u) = F (up ). We show that u is separable, i.e. its minimal
polynomial P is separable. If not, we write P = P1 (X p ) for some P1 , and set n1 := n/p.
Since up has minimal polynomial P1 , we have [F (up ) : F ] = n1 . But [F (u) : F ] = n, a
contradiction.

Proposition 1.4.7. Assume char(F ) = p and let K/F be a finite extension degree d.
The following statement are equivalent:

  (i) K/F is a separable extension;

 (ii) We have K = F K p . (Note that K p := {xp : x ∈ K} is a sub-field of K, but not
      necessarily contains F .)

(iii) There exists a basis {e1 , . . . , ed } of K/F such that {ep1 , . . . , epd } are still a basis.

(iii)’ For any basis {f1 , . . . , fd } of K/F , {f1p , . . . , fdp } is still a basis.

                                                      10
(iii)” For any elements f1 , . . . , fr of K which are linearly independent over F , {f1p , . . . , frp }
       are still linearly independent over F .
                                                                                                          ∞
Remark 1.4.8. Proposition 1.4.7 is not true for infinite extensions, e.g. K = Fp (t1/p ).

Proof. We first show that (iii), (iii)’, (iii)” are equivalent.
   (iii)⇐⇒(iii)’ there is an invertible matrix M ∈ Mn (F ) such that
                                                        
                                      f1                e1
                                   ..               . 
                                   .  = M ·  ..  .
                                           fd                  ed

Taking p-power, get
                                        f1p
                                                  p 
                                                     e1
                                       ..    (p)    . 
                                       .  = M ·  ..  .
                                                   

                                          fdp                    epd
Here M (p) denotes (apij ) if M = (aij ); since char(F ) = p, we have det M (p) = (det M )p ̸= 0.
In particular, M (p) is still invertible, hence {fip } is a basis.
    (iii)’⇐⇒(iii)” It suffices to extend the set {f1 , . . . , fr } to a basis {f1 , . . . , fd }.
    Now consider other implications.
    (i)=⇒(ii) For any u ∈ K, F (u) = F (up ), hence F K p = K.
    (ii)=⇒(iii) Let {e1 , . . . , ed } be a basis of K/F , i.e. K = F e1 + F e2 + · · · + F ed . Passing
to the p-th power and noting that char(K) = p, we get

                                        K p = F p ep1 + · · · + F p epd

hence(3)
                                       F K p = F ep1 + · · · + F epd .
But [F K p : F ] = [K : F ] = d, so ep1 , . . . , epd must be a basis.
     (iii)”=⇒(i) Fix u ∈ K, we need to show u is separable over F . Take the canonical
basis of F (u) over F which gives a basis of F (up ) over F by passing to the p-th power by
(iii)”. In particular, [F (up ) : F ] = [F (u) : F ], hence F (up ) = F (u) and we conclude by
Lemma 1.4.6.

Corollary 1.4.9. Assume char(F ) = p and let u ∈ F . Then u is separable over F if and
only if F (u)/F is a separable extension.

Proof. ⇐= is obvious.
   =⇒ Write K = F (u). It is direct to see that K p = F (up ). On the other hand, we
have K = F (up ) by Lemma 1.4.6 (as u is separable). Thus the equality K = F K p holds
and we conclude by Proposition 1.4.7.
 (3)
       Note that [F K p : F ] is finite, so F K p is equal to the minimal subring containing F and K p , using
Proposition 1.2.2(iii). Denote L = F ep1 + · · · + F epd . Then L contains F and K p , and L is a ring as
epi epj = (ei ej )p ∈ K p ⊂ L. Thus F K p ⊆ L; the inclusion ⊇ is obvious.


                                                      11
Corollary 1.4.10. Let F ⊂ E ⊂ K be a tower of algebraic extensions. Then K/F is
separable if and only if both K/E and E/F are separable.

Proof. If they are finite extensions, use (ii): K = EK p = F E p K p = F K p . The general
case can be reduced to this. Precisely let u ∈ K and P (X) = k ak X k ∈ E[X] the mini-
                                                                   P

mal polynomial. Replacing E by F (a0 , . . . , an ); clearly E is separable over F . Moreover
u is separable over E, we are thus reduced to the case of finite extensions.

Corollary 1.4.11. Let F ⊂ E, E ′ ⊂ F and assume E/F, E ′ /F are separable extensions,
then so is EE ′ /F .

Proof. Again we reduced the proof to the case of finite extensions. We need to check
F (EE ′ )p contains both E and E ′ , this is true since F E p = E and F E ′p = E ′ .

Definition 1.4.12. Let F ⊂ K be an algebraic extension. Let Ks be the set of all
separable elements in K. Then Ks is an intermediate field of K/F (by Corollary 1.4.9
and Corollary 1.4.11), called maximal separable extension of F in K. When it is finite,
[K : F ]s := [Ks : F ] is called the separable degree.
   When K = F , we write F sep := Ks , called a separable closure of F .

Purely inseparable extensions
                                                                            m
Definition 1.4.13. An element u ∈ F is purely inseparable over F if up ∈ F for some
m ∈ N. By convention, if u ∈ F , then u is purely inseparable: take m = 0.
   An algebraic extension K/F is purely inseparable extension if every element of K is
purely inseparable over F .

Corollary 1.4.14.      (i) If K/F is a finite purely inseparable extension, then [K : F ] is
    a power of p.

 (ii) If K/E and E/F are both purely inseparable extensions, so is K/F .

(iii) A compositum of purely inseparable extensions is still purely inseparable.

Proof. Left as an exercise.

Proposition 1.4.15. Let K/F be an algebraic extension, with Ks the maximal separable
subextension in K. Then K/Ks is purely inseparable.

Definition 1.4.16. We define the inseparable degree of K/F to be [K : Ks ], denoted by
[K : F ]i , which makes
                            [K : F ] = [K : F ]s [K : F ]i .

    The link between separable extensions and the number of embeddings from K to F
is as follows. Let K/F be an algebraic extension. Write HomF (K, F ) for the set of
F -embeddings K ,→ F .


                                             12
Proposition 1.4.17. Let K/F be a finite extension. Then
                                  [K : F ]s = |HomF (K, F )|.
Proof. Let Ks be the maximal separable subextension. We first observe that if τ : K ,→ F
is an F -embedding, then τ is determined by its restriction to Ks . In fact, K/Ks is purely
                                   m
inseparable, i.e. for any u ∈ K, up ∈ Ks for some m ≥ 0, hence if
                                          m                     m
                                v := τ (up ) = (τ (u))p ∈ F .
                                                                                     m
But any element in F has a unique pm -th power root, namely the equation X p − v = 0
                                                         −m
has a unique solution in F for any v ∈ F , denoted by v p . We must have
                                                       −m
                                         τ (u) = v p        .
As a consequence we get
                               HomF (K, F ) = HomF (Ks , F ),
i.e. we may assume that K is separable over F .
     We first treat the case when K = F (u) is a simple extension. Then the minimal
polynomial P of u has degree n = [K : F ]. Since P is separable, it has n distinct roots in
F , say u1 = u, u2 , . . . , un . For each i, we get an embedding K ,→ F by sending u to ui . Of
course, these are different, so we get (at least) n embedding. Conversely, any embedding
must send u to some ui , hence we have the equality |HomF (K, F )| = n, proving the
assertion in this case.
     In general, since K/F is finite, K is generated over F by finite elements, then do an
easy induction.

1.5    Galois extensions
Let K/F be an extension, we write AutF (K) for the group of F -automorphisms of K: it
becomes a group, the binary operation being the composition: (σ, τ ) 7→ σ ◦ τ . Note that,
in general, AutF (K) ̸= HomF (K, F ); this is true only when K is normal.
   We have two basic operations.
   1. If H is a subgroup of AutF (K), we can attach the fixed field :
                            K H := {u ∈ K : ∀τ ∈ H, τ (u) = u}.
Obviously K H is an intermediate field of K/F .
   2. If E/F is a subextension of K/F , then we have AutE (K): the group of E-
automorphisms of K. It is a subgroup of AutF (K).
   These operations are both order-reversing:
                                  H1 ⊂ H2 ⇒ K H1 ⊃ K H2
                            E1 ⊂ E2 ⇒ AutE1 (K) ⊃ AutE2 (K).



                                               13
Definition 1.5.1. A Galois extension of F is a normal and separable algebraic extension
of F . If K/F is Galois, we define the Galois group Gal(K/F ) to be AutF (K).(4)

Definition 1.5.2. Let K/F be a separable extension. The Galois closure of K/F (inside
F ) is the smallest Galois extension of F containing K.

Lemma 1.5.3. Let K/F be a finite Galois extension. Then

  (i) |Gal(K/F )| = [K : F ];

 (ii) K Gal(K/F ) = F .

Proof. (1) Since K/F is separable, we have |HomF (K, F )| = [K : F ] by Proposition
1.4.17. But K is normal, every F -embedding K ,→ F has image K, so HomF (K, F ) =
AutF (K) = Gal(K/F ).
    (2) It is clear that F ⊂ K Gal(K/F ) . For any u ∈ K Gal(K/F ) , denote by P its minimal
polynomial in F [X] which is separable. If v ∈ F is a root of P , we get an F -embedding
F (u) ,→ F mapping u to v; it extends to an embedding τ : K ,→ F by Lemma 1.3.10.
Since K is normal, τ belongs to Gal(K/F ). By assumption, u is fixed by Gal(K/F ), i.e.
u = τ (u) = v. Therefore P has only one root in F . Since P has no multiple roots, we
must have deg P = 1 and u ∈ F .

       The next lemma is quite general !

Lemma 1.5.4 (Artin). Let K be a field. Let H be a finite subgroup of Aut(K) and
E = K H . Then K/E is a Galois extension of degree (5)

                                           [K : E] ≤ |H|.

Proof. First, we prove that the extension K/E is Galois. Let u ∈ K, we want to write
down its minimal polynomial over E. Let O be the H-orbit of u, namely O = {τ (u) : τ ∈
H} (without multiplicities), and consider
                                           Y
                                 Pu (X) :=   (X − α).
                                                  α∈O
                                                                   `
More precisely, if H1 denotes the stabilizer of u and if H = i gi H1 is the left coset
                                                Q
decomposition of H over H1 , then Pu (X) = i (X − gi u). Since τ (Pu ) = Pu for any
τ ∈ H, the coefficients of Pi lie in K H = E, i.e. Pu ∈ E[X]. This implies that P , the
minimal polynomial of u over E, divides Pu . Since Pu has no multiple roots, so neither
does P . A conjugate of u is one of α ∈ O, so lies in K, i.e. K/E is normal.
   Next, we prove that [K : E] ≤ |H| =: n. Let v1 , . . . , vn+1 be arbitrary n + 1 elements
of K, we have to show that they are linearly dependent over E. We may assume vi ̸= 0

                                                        15
1.6    The Galois correspondence
The fundamental theorem of Galois theory is the following

Theorem 1.6.1. Let K/F be a finite Galois extension. There is a one-to-one correspon-
dence between the subgroups H of G = Gal(K/F ) and the intermediate fields F ⊂ E ⊂ K.
The correspondence is given by E = K H and H = Gal(K/E).

Proof. By Lemma 1.5.3, we have K Gal(K/E) = E for any E ⊂ K. We need to prove
Gal(K/K H ) = H. Clearly, H ⊂ Gal(K/K H ), both viewed as subgroups of G, so we are
left to prove
                               |Gal(K/K H )| ≤ |H|,
which follows from Artin’s Lemma 1.5.4.

Corollary 1.6.2. Let K/F be a finite Galois extension. If F ⊂ E ⊂ K, then E/F is
Galois if and only if Gal(K/E) is a normal subgroup of Gal(K/F ). When this holds, the
Galois group Gal(E/F ) is isomorphic to Gal(K/F )/Gal(K/E).

Proof. Let G = Gal(K/F ) and H = Gal(K/E). For any τ ∈ G, {τ (x) : x ∈ E} is still an
intermediate field of K/F , denoted by τ (E). One checks that τ Hτ −1 = Gal(K/τ (E)).
    If E/F is normal, then τ (E) = E for any τ , hence τ Hτ −1 = H and H is normal in G.
Conversely, if H is normal in G, we have

                      Gal(K/E) = H = τ Hτ −1 = Gal(K/τ (E)).
                             −1
This implies E = K H = K τ Hτ = τ (E), hence E/F is a normal extension.
   Finally, we define a homomorphism of groups

                          Gal(K/F ) → Gal(E/F ), τ 7→ τ |E .

Its kernel is clearly Gal(K/E). It is surjective because any F -embedding E ,→ F can be
extended to an F -embedding K ,→ F by Lemma 1.3.10 (alternatively, one can argue by
comparing their orders).

Proposition 1.6.3. Let E/F , K/F be two algebraic extensions and assume E/F is finite
Galois.

  (i) The extension EK/K is also finite Galois.

 (ii) There is an isomorphism Gal(EK/K) ∼
                                        = Gal(E/E ∩ K), hence it can be viewed as
      a subgroup of Gal(E/F ).

Remark 1.6.4. As a consequence of Proposition 1.6.3 (ii), we have [E : E ∩ K] =
[EK : K]. However, be attention that this need not be true if E/F is not Galois, e.g.
      √               √
E = Q( 3 2), K = Q(ω 3 2). So correspondingly, the restriction map HomK (EK, F ) →
HomE∩K (E, F ) need not be surjective in general.

                                          16
Proof. (i) Since E/F is normal, E is a splitting field of some polynomial P ∈ F [X].
Hence EK is the splitting field of P over K, and that EK/K is normal. The extension
is separable by Proposition 1.4.7: assume char(F ) = p, then EK = E p K = (EK)p K as
E = F E p.
    (2) Consider the map

                     ι : Gal(EK/K) → Gal(E/(E ∩ K)), σ 7→ σ|E .

This is clearly a group homomorphism. If σ ∈ Gal(EK/K) is such that σ|E = IdE , then
it is identity on EK (as σ fixes K). This proves the injectivity of ι.
     We prove the surjectivity. Let H be the image of ι, which is a subgroup of Gal(E/E ∩
K). By the Galois correspondence, H = Gal(E/E ∩ K) if and only if E H = E ∩ K. The
inclusion E ∩ K ⊃ E H is obvious. Conversely, let u ∈ E H . Then u ∈ EK is fixed by
Gal(EK/K), hence u ∈ K, and u ∈ E ∩ K. So we get the equality E ∩ K = E H and
concludes the proof.

Proposition 1.6.5. Let K/F, K ′ /F be two finite Galois extensions. Then KK ′ /F is still
finite Galois and there is an injection Gal(KK ′ /F ) ,→ Gal(K/F ) × Gal(K ′ /F ).

Proof. It is clear that KK ′ /F is Galois, by Corollary 1.3.15 and Corollary 1.4.11. The
injection is given by σ 7→ (σ|K , σ|K ′ ). It is an isomorphism if and only if K ∩ K ′ = F
comparing the orders (using [KK ′ : F ] = [KK ′ : K][K : F ] = [K ′ : F ][K ′ : F ]).

Definition 1.6.6. A Galois extension K/F is called abelian if Gal(K/F ) is an abelian
group.

Corollary 1.6.7. A compositum of abelian extensions is still abelian.

Remark 1.6.8. The largest abelian extension inside F is denoted by F ab . The structure
of Gal(F ab /F ) is the main object of class field theory.

   The following direct corollary is, however, non trivial.

Corollary 1.6.9. Let K/F be a finite separable extension, then there are only finitely
many subfields with F ⊂ E ⊂ K.

Proof. By replacing K by its Galois closure, which is still finite over F , we may assume K
is Galois: an intermediate field between F and K is also an intermediate field between F
and K Gal . Then use Galois correspondence to conclude, since Gal(K/F ) has only finitely
many subgroups.

Primitive element theorem
Theorem 1.6.10. If K/F is a finite separable extension, then K = F (u) for some u ∈ K,
i.e. K is a simple extension of F .


                                            17
Proof. If F is a finite field (hence so is K), then it follows from the fact that K × is a
cyclic group.
   Suppose F is infinite for the rest of the proof. We know that there are only finitely
many subfields F ⊂ E ⊂ K. Write K = F (u1 , . . . , un ) for suitable ui ∈ K. An induction
shows that we may assume n = 2.
   For each r ∈ F , F (u1 + ru2 ) is an intermediate field of K/F . The above observation,
using that F is infinite, shows that there exist r ̸= s ∈ F such that

                                   F (u1 + ru2 ) = F (u1 + su2 ).

But then (r − s)u2 ∈ F (u1 + ru2 ), which implies that u2 , and also u1 , lie in F (u1 + ru2 ).
Thus K(u1 , u2 ) = F (u1 + ru2 ) is a simple extension.

Remark 1.6.11. Theorem 1.6.10 is false if K/F is not separable. Example: take
F = Fp (x, y), K = Fp (x1/p , y 1/p ), then [K : F ] = p2 , and Fr := F (x1/p + ry 1/p ) is
an intermediate field of K/F for any r ∈ F of degree p. Thus Fr ⊊ K for any r ∈ F ,
and consequently Fr ̸= Fr′ if r ̸= r′ .

Examples of Galois correspondence

                             {r, a | r3 = 1, a2 = 1, ara = r−1 }.

1.7        Galois groups of polynomials
Definition 1.7.1. Let f ∈ F [X] be a separable polynomial and K be its splitting field.
The Galois group Gal(K/F ) is called the Galois group of f and denoted by Gf .
       In most of the time, we will assume f ∈ F [X] is moreover irreducible.
Lemma 1.7.2. Let f ∈ F [X] be a separable irreducible polynomial of degree n. Then Gf
is isomorphic to a transitive subgroup of the symmetric group Sn . Moreover, the order
o(Gf ) is divided by n.
   Recall that a subgroup H < Sn is called transitive if for all i, j, there exists σ ∈ H
such that σ(i) = j (this does not mean (ij) ∈ H).
Proof. Let α1 , . . . , αn be the distinct roots of f . Let Sn be the group of permutations of
the set T := {α1 , . . . , αn }. Since σ ∈ Gf is determined by the images σ(αi ), which still
lie in T , σ defines a permutation on T , i.e. σ ∈ Sn . For any 1 ≤ i, j ≤ n, there exists an
                        ∼
F -isomorphism K −      → K sending αi to αj , so Gf is transitive. The last statement follows
from the facts that o(Gf ) = [K : F ] = [K : F (α1 )][F (α1 ) : F ] (where K is the splitting
field of f ) and [F (α1 ) : F ] = n.
       Let α1 , . . . , αn ∈ F be the roots of f . Set
                               Y                                         Y
                    ∆(f ) =         (αi − αj ), D(f ) = ∆(f )2 =                 (αi − αj )2 .
                           1≤i<j≤n                                     1≤i<j≤n

Here D(f ) is called the discriminant of f . Note that ∆(f ) ̸= 0 as f is separable.
Example 1.7.3. We have
                 D(X 2 + bX + c) = b2 − 4c, D(X 3 + px + q) = −4p3 − 27q 2 .(6)
       Let σ ∈ Sn ; it naturally acts on the set {α1 , . . . , αn }. It is easy to see that
                                          σ∆(f ) = sign(σ)∆(f ),
where sign(σ) ∈ {±1} denotes the signature of σ. Hence σD(f ) = D(f ). This implies
that D(f ) ∈ F [X], since the Galois group Gf is in particular a subgroup of Sn . Also we
see that ∆(f ) is fixed by σ if and only if sign(σ) = 1, if and only if σ ∈ An . Therefore,
we get the following:
 (6)
       In general, D(X 3 + bX 2 + cX + d) = b2 c2 − 4c3 − 4b3 d − 27d2 + 18bcd


                                                     19
Lemma 1.7.4. Assume char(F ) ̸= 2. Then

                       Gf ⊂ An ⇐⇒ ∆(f ) ∈ F × ⇐⇒ D(f ) ∈ F ×2 .

   From now on, we assume char(F ) ̸= 2.

1.7.1   deg f = 3
We know that Gf is a subgroup of S3 and 3|o(Gf ), so o(Gf ) ∈ {3, 6} and there are two
possibilities for Gf : A3 (the unique subgroup of order 3) and S3 . Lemma 1.7.4 implies
that Gf = A3 if and only if D(f ) has a square root in F × .

Example 1.7.5. Take f = X 3 − 3X + 1 ∈ Q[X]. It is irreducible, with discriminant
−4(−3)3 − 27 = 81 = 92 , so Gf = A3 . But for f = X 3 + 3X + 1 ∈ Q[X], we have
Gf = S3 .

1.7.2   deg f = 4
We first list the subgroups of S4 , up to conjugation (except for 1 and S4 itself):

   - S2 , A3 or S3 : order 2, 3 or 6 respectively, not transitive;

   - {Id, (12)(34)}: order 2, not transitive;

   - ⟨(12), (34)⟩: not normal, not transitive;

   - ⟨(1234)⟩ = {Id, (1234), (13)(24), (1432)}: order 4, cyclic group, transitive, not nor-
     mal;

   - V4 := {Id, (12)(34), (13)(24), (14)(23)}: order 4, normal abelian subgroup, transi-
     tive;

   - ⟨(1234), (13)⟩ ∼
                    = D4 (dihedral group): order 8 and Sylow 2-subgroup, transitive;

   - A4 ; normal subgroup, order 12, transitive.

In summary, transitive subgroups of S4 whose order is divided by 4 are as follows:

   - C4 , conjugates of ⟨(1234)⟩;

   - V4 = {Id, (12)(34), (13)(24), (14)(23)};

   - D4 , conjugates of ⟨(1234), (13)⟩;

   - A4 ;

   - S4 .



                                             20
Moreover, if H contains a transposition (ij), then H = D4 , A4 , S4 .
   The subgroup V4 will play a crucial role. Let α1 , α2 , α3 , α4 be the roots of f . Consider
the following elements
                                   γ1 = α1 α2 + α3 α4
                                          γ2 = α1 α3 + α2 α4
                                          γ3 = α1 α4 + α2 α3 .
They are pairwise distinct, e.g. γ1 − γ2 = (α1 − α4 )(α2 − α3 ) ̸= 0. It is clear that S4
permutes the set {γ1 , γ2 , γ3 }.

Lemma 1.7.6. The stabilizer of each γi is a Sylow 2-subgroup, e.g.

                                      Stab(γ2 ) = ⟨(1234), (13)⟩.

The group V4 fixes all γ1 , γ2 , γ3 . The fixed field of Gf ∩V4 is F (γ1 , γ2 , γ3 ), and F (γ1 , γ2 , γ3 )
is Galois over F with Galois group Gf /(Gf ∩ V4 ).

Proof. Obvious.

    We consider the polynomial

                               g(X) := (X − γ1 )(X − γ2 )(X − γ3 ),

which has coefficients in F ; it is called the cubic resolvent of f .

Lemma 1.7.7. If f = X 4 + bX 3 + cX 2 + dX + e, then

                          g = X 3 − cX 2 + (bd − 4e)X − b2 e + 4ce − d2 .

Moreover, f and g have the same discriminants.

   In particular, when b = c = 0, g = X 3 − 4eX − d2 . When b = d = 0, g = X 3 − cX 2 −
4eX + 4ce.

Theorem 1.7.8. Let M := F (γ1 , γ2 , γ3 ) be the splitting field of g over F and m := [M :
F ]. Then

    • if m = 1, then Gf = V4 ;

                                                     21
      • if m = 2, then Gf is isomorphic to C4 or D4 ; moreover, Gf is isomorphic to D4 if
        and only if f is irreducible in M [X];

      • if m = 3, then Gf = A4 ;

      • if m = 6, then Gf = S4 .
Proof. This is because that [M : F ] = |Gal(M/F )| = |Gf /Gf ∩ V4 |. It is easy to check
that [C4 : C4 ∩ V4 ] = 2 and (since V4 ⊂ D4 )

                         [D4 : V4 ] = 2, [A4 : V4 ] = 3, [S4 : V4 ] = 6.

This proves all the statements except the last one in the case m = 2.
   Assume m = 2. The extension K/M is Galois with Galois group Gf ∩ V4 , which has
order 2 (resp. 4) if Gf is isomorphic to C4 (resp. D4 ). If f is irreducible in M [X], then
Gf ∩V4 acts transitively on the four roots of f , so has order 4. Conversely, if f is reducible
in M [X], then f must decompose as f1 f2 , with deg fi = 2. Therefore Gf ∩ V4 can not act
transitively on {αi }, so Gf ∩ V4 is not transitive, equivalently Gf = C4 .
Proposition 1.7.9. Assume that char(F ) ̸= 2. Let f = X 4 + bX 3 + cX 2 + dX + e ∈ F [X]
be a separable irreducible polynomial. Assume m = [M : F ] = 2, and let γ be the
unique root of the cubic resolvent of f . Then Gf is isomorphic to D4 if and only if either
D(f )(b2 − 4c + 4γ) ∈ F × \F ×2 , or b2 − 4c + 4γ = 0 and D(f )(γ 2 − 4e) ∈ F × \F ×2 .

                        (1234)∆(f ) = −∆(f ), (13)∆(f ) = −∆(f ).

                                                22
1.8     Examples
We recall some facts about the irreducibility of polynomials in Q[X]. The first one is
Gauss’s lemma.
Proposition 1.8.1. Let f (X) = an X n + an−1 X n−1 + · · · + a0 ∈ Z[X] be primitive, i.e.
GCD{ai } = 1. Then f (X) is irreducible in Z[X] if and only if it is irreducible in Q[X].
   For example, f is primitive if it is monic.
Proposition 1.8.2 (Eisenstein’s criterion). Let f = an X n + an−1 X n−1 + · · · + a0 ∈ Z[X].
Suppose that there is a prime p such that
   – p does not divide an (ok if an = 1);

   – p divides an−1 , . . . , a0 ;

   – p2 does not divide a0 .
Then f is irreducible in Q[X].
   We also have the following simple fact.
Lemma 1.8.3. Suppose r ∈ Q is a root of f (X) = an X n + an−1 X n−1 + · · · + a0 ∈ Z[X].
Let r = c/d with (c, d) = 1. Then c|a0 and d|an . In particular, if an = 1, we get r ∈ Z.
   This is easy to prove. In general, it is not enough for the irreducibility of f . But when
deg f = 3, f is reducible if and only if f has a root in Q, so Lemma 1.8.3 is applicable.
Example 1.8.4. Let f = X 3 − 8X + 4 ∈ Q[X]. It is irreducible, because the only possible
roots are ±1, ±2, ±4, and we check that this is not the case.
Remark 1.8.5. Another way to show irreducibility of monic polynomial f ∈ Z[X] is to
show f (mod p) is irreducible in Fp [X] for some prime p.
    For example, f (X) = X 4 − X 2 − 1 is irreducible mod 3, so f is itself irreducible.
Indeed, it is easy to see that f (0), f (1), f (2) ̸= 0 in F3 , and that f (mod p) is not divisible
by X 2 + 1, X 2 + X + 2 or X 2 + 2X + 2 in F3 [X] (these are the only irreducible polynomials
of degree 2 in F3 [X]).
   Next, we give some examples of irreducible polynomials and study their Galois groups.
Example 1.8.6. Consider f (X) = X 4 + 4X 2 + 2 ∈ Q[X]. It is irreducible by Eisenstein’s
criterion. Its cubic resolvent is

                 g(X) = X 3 − 4X 2 − 8X + 32 = (X − 4)(X 2 − 8).

b = 0, c = 4, γ = 4 so b2 − 4c + 4γ = 0, but D(f )(γ 2 − 4e) = (322 × 2) × 8 ∈ (Q× )2 .

1.9     Polynomials with Galois group Sp over Q
Theorem 1.9.1. If p is a prime number and f ∈ Q[x] an irreducible polynomial of degree
p with exactly two non-real roots in C, then the Galois group of f over Q is isomorphic
to the symmetric group Sp .

   Recall Cauchy’s theorem: if G is a finite group, p is a prime and p|o(G), then G has
an element of order p. (7)

Proof. Let K be the splitting field of f and Gf its Galois group. By Cauchy’s theorem,
there is an element σ of order p, thus G contains a p-cycle (as p is a prime !).
    Let τ : C → C denote the complex conjugation a + bi 7→ a − bi. The restriction τ |K
defines an element of Gal(K/Q), which is not trivial. Moreover, it just exchanges the two
non-real roots and fixes the other real roots, so it is a 2-cycle (i.e. a transposition). We
then conclude by Lemma 1.9.2 below.

Lemma 1.9.2. If p is a prime number, Sp is generated by any transposition and any
p-cycle.

Proof. We may assume the transposition is τ = (12) (up to renumbering). Also we may
write the p-cycle σ as (1i2 · · · ip ). If ir = 2 then σ r−1 maps 1 to 2, and is still a p-
cycle (here we use that p is a prime). So we may assume σ = (12j3 · · · jp ), and after
 (7)
    In fact, by Sylow’s theorem, G has a subgroup H of order pn , where pn ||o(G); since a p-group is
solvable, H has subgroups of order pr for any r < n.


                                                  25
renumbering {j3 , . . . , jp } we may assume σ = (123 · · · p) (without changing τ ). Namely
we are left to prove that Sp is generated by (12) and (123 · · · p). For this, one checks that
for i = 1, . . . , p − 2,
                                    σ i (12)σ −i = (i + 1 i + 2),
and that these transpositions generate Sp .

Remark 1.9.3. In Lemma 1.9.2, it is crucial to work with Sp for a prime number p. For
example, (13) and (1234) generate D4 inside S4 .

Example 1.9.4. The polynomial f (x) = x5 − 5x + 2 has Galois group S5 . First, it is
irreducible, since putting x = y − 2, f (y − 2) = (y − 2)5 − 5(y − 2) + 2 with constant
coefficient being −32 + 10 + 2 = −20, so that we can apply Eisenstein’s criterion. Next,
we check that f has 3 real roots, using f ′ (x) = 15x4 − 15 = 0 has 2 real roots ±1.(8)



Dedekind’s theorem
Theorem 1.9.5. (Dedekind) Let f (X) ∈ Z[X] be a monic irreducible polynomial of degree
m, and let p be a prime number such that (f mod p) has only simple roots.(9) Suppose
         Q
that f = i fi with fi irreducible of degree mi in Fp [X]. Then Gf contains an element
whose cycle decomposition is of type

                                           m = m1 + · · · + mr .

Proof. See Jacobson, Basic Algebra I, §4.16.

Example 1.9.6. Consider f (X) = X 5 − X − 1 ∈ Z[X]. It is irreducible as it is modulo
3. Modulo 2, this factors as (X 2 + X + 1)(X 3 + X 2 + 1). Hence Gf contains (12345) and
a cycle of the form σ = (ab)(cde). We check that σ 3 = (ab)3 = (ab) is a transposition.
Therefore Gf = S5 by Lemma 1.9.2.


1.10         Finite fields
A field F is called finite if |F | is finite. They are also called Galois fields. A basis example
is Fp := Z/(p) where p is a prime number; it has p elements.

Lemma 1.10.1. A finite field has a positive characteristic, say p. Its cardinality is then
a power of p.

Proof. The first assertion is obvious. Since F is an Fp -vector space, its cardinality equals
to pn where n = [F : Fp ].
 (8)
       we have f (+∞) > 0, f (1) < 0, f (−1) > 0, f (−∞) < 0.
 (9)
       This is equivalent to that D(f ) is not divisible by p.




                                                     26
Theorem 1.10.2. For every finite p-power q, there exists a finite field of cardinality q.
Moreover, such a field is unique up to isomorphism.

Proof. Given q = pm , let F/Fp be the splitting field of the polynomial X q − X ∈ Fp [X].
Since (u + v)q = uq + v q in F , the roots of X q − X in F form a subfield. On the other
hand, X q − X is separable as (X q − X)′ = −1 is prime to X q − X (see Lemma 1.4.2).
Therefore, it has q distinct roots in F . By construction, F is exactly generated by the
roots, hence F = {u ∈ F : uq = u} and has cardinality q.
    Next we show any finite field of cardinality q, say F ′ , is isomorphic to F . Since
|F ′× | = q − 1, we have uq−1 = 1 for any u ∈ F ′× , hence uq = u for any u ∈ F ′ . This shows
that F ′ is a splitting field of X q − X. We then conclude by the uniqueness of splitting
fields (see Proposition 1.3.4).

    We denote by Fq the unique finite field of cardinality q. To give a more explicit
structure of Fq , especially for the multiplicative structure, we need another construction
of Fq . Write q = pn . Take an irreducible polynomial f ∈ Fp [X] of degree n, and let α be a
root of f in Fp . Since Fp [α] is a field extension over Fp of degree n, it must be isomorphic
to Fq (for any such f !) by Theorem 1.10.2. As a consequence, any element in Fq can be
uniquely written as
                             ad−1 αn−1 + · · · + a1 α + a0 , ai ∈ Fp
and the addition and multiplication in Fq can be made explicit via f .
  Being a splitting field of a separable polynomial, Fq is a Galois extension over Fp .
More generally, we have the following result.

Theorem 1.10.3. (i) For m, n ∈ N, there exists a field embedding Fpm ,→ Fpn if and only
if m|n.
    (ii) The group Gal(Fpn /Fp ) is isomorphic to Z/nZ, and is generated by x 7→ xp , which
is called Frobenius.
    (iii) An algebraic closure of Fp is isomorphic to limn Fpn .
                                                      −→
Proof. (i) The condition is clearly sufficient. Conversely, if Fpm ,→ Fpn , then Fpn becomes
a vector space over Fpm . If d denotes the dimension, then pn = (pm )d , in particular m|n.
    (ii) We know that |Gal(Fpn /Fp )| = n = [Fpn : Fp ]. On the other hand, σ : x 7→ xp
is an element of Gal(Fpn /Fp ), so it suffices to prove that σ has order n. If d denotes the
                     d
order of σ, then xp = x for any x ∈ Fpn , so Fpn ,→ Fpd and n|d by (i). But, we also have
d|n (as σ n = Id), hence d = n.
    (iii) This is because any irreducible polynomial splits in Fp .

Remark 1.10.4. As an analog of Theorem 1.10.3(ii), for any p-power q and any n ≥ 1,
Fqn /Fq is Galois of group Z/nZ with a generator given by x 7→ xq .

   The following result proves the primitive element theorem 1.6.10 in finite field case.

Proposition 1.10.5. Let F be a finite field, then F × forms a cyclic group.

                                             27
      More generally, we prove the following result:

Proposition 1.10.6. If F is a field, and G is a finite subgroup of F × , then G is cyclic.

Proof. First note that G is an abelian group, hence is a finite direct product of cyclic
groups:
                               G∼= H1 × H2 × · · · × Hr ,
where Hi = ⟨xi ⟩ is a cyclic group of order mi .
    We claim that (mi , mj ) ̸= 1 for any i ̸= j; this will imply that G is cyclic. Suppose
this is not true, and let k be the LCM (least common multiple) of all mi . Then k <
o(G) = m1 · · · mr . However, every element of G satisfies f (x) = xk − 1 ∈ F [x], while f
has at most k roots in F (even in F ). Contradiction.

Counting irreducible polynomials of given degree
Let q be a finite p-power. Denote by Sq (n) the set of monic, irreducible polynomials in
Fq [X] of degree n and by Nq (n) its cardinality. Note the following facts:

        f |gn for any f ∈ Sq (d).

      – Any root of gn generates a subfield of Fqn , hence its minimal polynomial lies in Sq (d)
        with d|n.

                                                28
1.11     Cyclotomic extensions
Let ξn be a primitive n-th root of unity in C, i.e. ξnn = 1 and ξnk ̸= 1 if k < n. Recall the
following basic facts:
  (i) the set of n-th roots of unity is just {ζnk : 0 ≤ k ≤ n − 1};

 (ii) the root ζnk is primitive if and only if (k, n) = 1; hence the number of primitive n-th
                                                                              Q
      roots of 1 is ϕ(n), the Euler’s function. Explicitly we have ϕ(n) = n p|n (1 − 1/p).
Lemma 1.11.1. The extension Q(ξn )/Q is Galois.
Proof. We only need to show Q(ξn )/Q is normal. This is clear, since a conjugate of ξn is
again a (primitive) n-th root of unity, hence is a power of ξn and contained in Q(ξn ).
   We want to determine the degree [Q(ξn ) : Q], which amounts to determining the
degree of the minimal polynomial of ξn over Q. Clearly, X n − 1 annihilates ξn , but it is
not minimal. It is natural de define
                                          Y
                         Φn (X) :=                   (X − ξ).
                                     ξ: primitive n-th root of 1

By Galois correspondence, Φn has coefficients in Q, because it is fixed by Gal(Q(ξn )/Q)
by construction.
   For example, we have Φ1 = X − 1, Φ2 = X + 1, Φ3 = X 2 + X + 1, Φ4 = X 2 + 1,
Φ5 = X 4 + X 3 + X 2 + X + 1, Φ6 = X 2 − X + 1, etc. By definition, we have
                                             Y
                                  Xn − 1 =       Φd .
                                                    d|n

   Let P denote the minimal polynomial of ξn over Q. Then P |Φn . Our goal is to show
P = Φn .
Lemma 1.11.2. Let p be a prime not dividing n. If u is a root of P (X), then so is up .
Proof. We have P |(X n − 1); so we may decompose in Q[X]
                                  X n − 1 = P (X) · Q(X).
Gauss lemma implies that P, Q ∈ Z[X] (being monic). Assume that up is not a root of P .
Then up is a root of Q. Equivalently, u is a root of Q(X p ) (as up is a root of X n − 1). But
P , being irreducible, is the minimal polynomial of u, hence P (X) divides Q(X p ). Taking
reduction mod p, we see that P (X) divides Q(X p ) in Fp [X]. On the other hand, since
ap = a for a ∈ Fp we have
                                                     p
                                      Q(X p ) = Q(X) ,
so Q(X p ) has the same roots as Q(X) (although with different multiplicities). This implies
that any root of P is also a root of Q. However, we know by construction
                                 P · Q = X n − 1 ∈ Fp [X],
a contradiction with the separability of X n − 1 (as (n, p) = 1).

                                               29
Theorem 1.11.3. We have P = Φn ; in particular Φn is irreducible. The Galois group
Gal(Q(ξn )/Q) is isomorphic to (Z/nZ)× .

Proof. We show P = Φn , i.e. Φn |P . It amounts to showing that every primitive n-th root
of unity is a root of P . Fix such a ξ and write ξ = ξnk , with (k, n) = 1. By decomposing k
as a product of primes pi , with (pi , n) = 1, we see that ξnk is a root of P by Lemma 1.11.2,
.
    For σ ∈ Gal(Q(ξn )/Q), it sends ξn to ξnk with (k, n) = 1. Then σ 7→ (k mod n) gives
the desired map to (Z/nZ)× .

   Since Q(ξn ) is an abelian extension over Q, any subfield is also a Galois extension
of Q. On the other hand, for any field extension F of Q, F (ξn )/F is also Galois with
Gal(F (ξn )/F ) being a subgroup of (Z/nZ)× .

Remark 1.11.4. An important theorem, named after Kronecker-Weber, says that Qab
(the maximal abelian extension of Q contained in C) is obtained by adjoining to Q all
n-th roots of unity for all n ∈ N.

    We recall the group structure of (Z/nZ)× . If n = pα1 1 · · · pαr r is the prime decomposi-
tion, then
                       (Z/nZ)× ∼ = (Z/pα1 1 Z)× × · · · × (Z/pαr r Z)× ,
For a prime p ≥ 3, (Z/pα Z)× is a cyclic group isomorphic to

                                  Z/(p − 1)Z × Z/pα−1 Z.

For p = 2, (Z/2α Z)× is not cyclic when α ≥ 3 and is isomorphic to Z/2Z × Z/2α−2 Z.
   We have the following beautiful result.

Theorem 1.11.5. Let q = pd . Let n ≥ 1 be prime to p. Then the polynomial Φn (X) is
irreducible in Fq [X] if and only if the class of q in the group (Z/nZ)× is a generator.

Proof. Left as an exercise.


1.12     Solvable groups and radical extensions
Let G be a finite group.

Definition 1.12.1. We say that G is solvable if G has a series of subgroups

                              G = G0 ⊃ G1 ⊃ · · · ⊃ Gn = {e}

such that where Gi is normal in Gi−1 and Gi−1 /Gi is an abelian group.

Example 1.12.2. The symmetric groups S3 , S4 are solvable, but S5 is not.

   We recall the following standard facts about solvable groups:


                                              30
  (i) If G is solvable and H < G is a subgroup, then H is solvable.

 (ii) If G is solvable and H < G is a normal subgroup, then G/H is solvable.

(iii) If 1 → G1 → G → G2 → 1 is an exact sequence, then G is solvable if and only if
      G1 , G2 are solvable.

 (iv) If G is solvable, then G has a normal subgroup H of prime index (i.e. [G : H] is a
      prime). Indeed, let H be a maximal normal subgroup of G, then the quotient group
      G/H is simple and also solvable, so it is abelian and of prime order (being simple).

   Let F be a field of characteristic 0.

Proposition 1.12.3. Let a ∈ F . If K is the splitting field of X n − a over F then the
Galois group Gal(K/F ) is solvable.

Proof. Let α be a root of X n −a. Then the roots of X n −a are exactly {αξni ; 0 ≤ i ≤ n−1},
where ξn is a (fixed) primitive root of unity. Thus, the splitting field K is equal to F (α, ξn ).
   Letting E = F (ξn ) we obtain a tower of extensions

                                      F (α, ξn )


                          F (α)                         E = F (ξn )


                                         F

It is clear that E/F is a Galois extension and the group Gal(E/F ) is a subgroup of
(Z/nZ)× , hence is abelian. It suffices to prove Gal(K/E) is also abelian. If σ ∈ Gal(K/E)
sends u to uξnk and τ sends u to uξnl , then στ and τ σ both send u to uξnk+l , since ξn ∈ E.
This proves the claim and that sending σ to k induces an embedding Gal(K/E) ,→
Z/nZ.


Definition 1.12.4. A finite extension K/F is said to be radical if there exist intermediate
fields
                            F = K0 ⊂ K1 ⊂ · · · ⊂ Kn = K
such that for each i, Ki = Ki−1 (ui ) with ui ∈ Ki satisfying um
                                                               i ∈ Ki−1 for some mi ≥ 0.
                                                                i




   Roughly, a radical extension of F is an extension of F which can be obtained by
adjoining a sequence of m-th roots of elements. Note that a radical extension need not
be Galois (see Proposition 1.12.3).
   Below are some basic properties about radical extensions.



                                                   31
  (i) If F ⊂ E ⊂ K is a tower of field extensions and K/F is radical, then K/E is radical
      (but E/F need not be radical).
       Example. Consider F = Q and K = Q(ξ7 ) which is clearly a radical extension of
       F . Since Gal(Q(ξ7 )/Q) = Z/6Z, the Galois correspondence implies that Q(ξ7 ) has
       a subfield E which has degree 3 over Q. Note that E/Q is Galois, and there is no
       proper intermediate field of E/Q.
       We claim that E/Q is not radical. Indeed, if E = Q(u) with um ∈ Q and we choose
       m to be minimal, then m ≥ 3; moreover, if τ denotes a generator of Gal(E/Q) ∼     =
                                                                           (10)
       Z/3Z, then τ (u) = uξm where ξm is a primitive m-th root of unity.       Since E/Q
       is Galois, we deduce that E contains ξm , hence E = Q(ξm ). But this is not possible
       because there is no solution for the equation ϕ(m) = 3.

 (ii) If K/E and E/F are finite radical extensions, then K/F is radical.

 (iii) If E/F, E ′ /F are radical, then EE ′ /F is also radical.

Proposition 1.12.5. Let E/F be a (finite) radical extension, and K/F be the Galois
closure of E. Then K/F is also radical and Gal(K/F ) is solvable.

Proof. The first assertion follows from the property (iii) above, as K is the compositum of
the conjugates of E. By an easy induction, the second assertion follows from Proposition
1.12.3 (which treats the case of a simple radical extension).

Lemma 1.12.6. Let K/F be a Galois extension with [K : F ] = p is prime. Assume that
F contains ξp , primitive p-th root of unity. Then K/F is a simple radical extension, i.e.
there exists u ∈ K such that up ∈ F and F (u) = K.

Proof. By assumption Gal(K/F ) is a cyclic group of order p, say generated by τ . We
will construct a non-zero element u ∈ K satisfying τ (u) = ξp−1 u. Then τ (u) ̸= u implies
  / F and τ (up ) = (ξp−1 u)p = up implies up ∈ F . Since [K : F ] is prime, there is no
u ∈
proper intermediate field of K/F , so we must have K = F (u).
    By linear independence of characters(11) , we may choose v ∈ K such that u :=
Pp−1 i i
  i=0 ξp τ (v) is nonzero. Then (as ξp ∈ F is fixed by τ )
                              Pp−1 i i+1
                                         (v) = ξp−1 ( p−1  i+1 i+1
                                                                   (v)) = ξp−1 u
                                                     P
                    τ (u) =    i=0 ξp τ               i=0 ξp τ


as required.
(10)
      Proof: if τ (u) = uξd for some d < m, then we would have τ (ud ) = (uξd )d = ud , so ud ∈ Q which
contradicts the choice of m.
 (11)
      Let G be an abelian group and L be a field. Let χ1 , . . . , χn be distinct characters G → L× . Then
they are linearly independent: if c1 , . . . , cn ∈ L satisfy

                                  c1 χ1 (g) + · · · + cn χn (g) = 0, ∀g ∈ G

then ci = 0 for 1 ≤ i ≤ n.


                                                     32
Theorem 1.12.7. Let K/F be a finite Galois extension such that Gal(K/F ) is solvable.
Then K is contained in a radical extension of F .
Proof. We prove by induction on n := [K : F ] (the base field F is allowed to change).
The case n = 1 is trivial.
   Since Gal(K/F ) is solvable, it has a normal subgroup H of prime index. We fix such a
prime p and adjoin the p-th roots of unity to K and F . It is clear that [K(ξp ) : F (ξp )] ≤
[K : F ].
   Case 1. If [K(ξp ) : F (ξp )] < n, then by inductive hypothesis K(ξp ) is contained in a
radical extension L/F (ξp ), as illustrated by the diagram below:

                                   K        K(ξp )         L

                                                      radical
                                    F       F (ξp )

Since F (ξp )/F is radical, L/F is also radical.
   Case 2. If [K(ξp ) : F (ξp )] = n, then Gal(K(ξp )/F (ξp )) ∼ = Gal(K/F ) and we may
                                                             H
view H as a subgroup of Gal(K(ξp )/F (ξp )). Let E = K(ξp ) which is a Galois extension
over F (ξp ) of degree p. Lemma 1.12.6 implies that E/F (ξp ) is a simple radical extension.
Since [K(ξp ) : E] = np < n, by induction K(ξp ) is contained in a radical extension L/E.
As before, L/F is also radical and the proof is complete.


1.13     Solvability of algebraic equations
Let F be a field of characteristic 0.
Definition 1.13.1. We say that f ∈ F [X] is solvable by radicals over F if f splits in
some radical extension of F , i.e. the splitting field of f is contained in some radical
extension of F . Equivalently, the roots of f can be obtained by the algebraic operations of
+, −, ×, ÷ and taking m-th roots.
Remark 1.13.2. In general, for a polynomial which is solvable by radicals, its splitting
field need not be a radical extension.
Theorem 1.13.3. Let f ∈ F [X]. Then f is solvable by radicals if and only if Gf is
solvable.
Proof. Let Kf /F be the splitting field of f . Assume that Kf is contained in a radical
extension K; we may assume K/F is Galois using Proposition 1.12.5. Then Gal(K/F )
is solvable by Proposition 1.12.5 again. Being a quotient group of Gal(K/F ), Gf is also
solvable. The converse follows from Theorem 1.12.7.

   Therefore, we have a necessary and sufficient condition for a polynomial to be solvable
by radicals: its Galois group is a solvable group. Since Sn is not solvable for n ≥ 5, we
obtain the following important result.

                                             33
Corollary 1.13.4. The general equation of degree n can not be solved by radicals for
n ≥ 5.


1.14      Applications: constructibility
We are allowed to do two basic types of operations:

 (a) Given two points on the plane, we can construct the line joining them.

 (b) Given a point A, and a radius r > 0, we can draw the circle with centre A and
     radius r.

   In this way, starting from points O = (0, 0) and (1, 0), we get new points, called
constructible points, by

   • intersecting two lines

   • intersecting a line with a circle

   • intersecting two circles.

   Remark that we can do the following, less obvious, operations:

 (a’) Given a segment S joining A and B, can construct a circle with diameter AB.
       Proof : Choose r large enough and construct two circles around respectively A
       and B of radius r. Denote the two intersections by C and C ′ . Then the line CC ′
       intersects with AB at the mid-point of AB, then the circle follows. Note that CC ′
       is perpendicular to AB, i.e. CC ′ ⊥ AB.

(b’) Given any line ℓ and any point P , we can construct a line passing P and perpen-
     dicular to ℓ; if P is not on ℓ, then we can construct a line passing P and parallel to
     ℓ.
       Proof : Choose r large enough such that the circle around P of radius r intersects
       with ℓ at A and B. Construct C, C ′ as in (a’); then the line CC ′ passes P and
       is perpendicular to AB. Now assume P ∈  / ℓ. It suffices to construct ℓ′ such that
       P ∈ ℓ′ and ℓ′ ⊥ CC ′ ; then ℓ′ ∥ ℓ.

    Using (b’), we easily see that a point (a, b) is constructible, if and only if (a, 0) and
(0, b) are both constructible. So it suffices to study the constructible points of the form
(a, 0). For this purpose, we make the following definition.

Definition 1.14.1. A real number c ∈ R is said to be constructible if and only if the point
(c, 0) is constructible. We let

                              C := {c ∈ R : c is constructible}.

                                             34
       The first fact is the following.
Proposition 1.14.2. C is a subfield of R (hence contains Q).
Proof. Of course, if c ∈ C, then −c ∈ C by doing a circle of radius c. Let a, b ∈ C, we
want a + b ∈ C: we may assume a, b > 0, then construct a circle around (a, 0) of radius b,
which will intersect with the x-axis at (a + b, 0).
   Next we show a−1 ∈ C: construct the line ℓ passing (a, 0) and (0, 1), and construct
the line parallel to ℓ and passing (1, 0), the intersection with y-axis gives (0, 1/a), in this
way we get (1/a, 0).
   Finally we show ab ∈ C in a similar way. Construct first the line ℓ which passes (1, 0)
and (0, a), and do the line parallel to ℓ and passing (b, 0), the intersection with y-axis
gives (0, ab).
       Another observation is
                                                √
Lemma 1.14.3. If c ∈ C and c > 0, then              c ∈ C.
Proof. Construct the circle D with diameter the segment joining (0, 0) and (1 + c, 0). The
line parallel to y-axis passing (1, 0) will intersect with D on some point (1, c′ ) (choose the
                                                         √
one in the first quadrant). Then we check that c′ = c.
Theorem 1.14.4. Let K/F (where F, K ⊂ R) be a normal extension of degree 2n for
some n ∈ N. Then F ⊂ C implies K ⊂ C.
Proof. We do induction on n. If n = 1, then K = F (u) for some u. The minimal
polynomial of u is X 2 + bX + c (degree 2). By changing u by u + b/2, we may assume
u2 ∈ F . Lemma 1.14.3 implies that u ∈ C.
    Suppose that n > 1 and the result is true for all m < n. Let G = Gal(K/F ), whose
order is 2n . By a standard result from group theory, G has a normal subgroup H of index
2.(12) Let K ′ = K H . Then [K ′ : F ] = 2 and we saw that K ′ ⊂ C. Again by the inductive
hypothesis, we get K ⊂ C.
Corollary 1.14.5. Let Q ⊂ F ⊂ R with F/Q normal and [F : Q] = 2n . Then F ⊂ C.
Remark 1.14.6. The condition that F/Q being “normal” in Corollary 1.14.5 is neces-
sary. To see this, let f ∈ Q[X] be an irreducible polynomial of degree 4 with Gf ∼
                                                                                 = S4 . Let
αi (1 ≤ i ≤ 4) be the four roots of f . Then [Q(αi ) : Q] = 4. If Q(αi ) ⊂ C for all i, then
Kf ⊂ C too. But Kf /Q contains a subfield of degree 3 over Q, which cannot be contained
in C (by Theorem 1.14.8 below). Contradiction.

    Our next task is to show the converse of Corollary 1.14.5: if c ∈ C, then [Q(c) : Q] = 2n
for some n ∈ N. For this, we need to analyse the three types of intersections.
    Let F be a subfield of R. The plane of F means the subset F × F of the plane R2 .
By a line in F , we mean a line joining two points P, Q of the plane of F . By a circle in
F , we mean a circle whose centre lies in the plane of F and the radius lies in F .
(12)
    Proof: the center of G is non-trivial; by induction G/Z(G) has a normal subgroup of index 2 whose
preimage gives the required subgroup H ◁ G.


                                                 35
Lemma 1.14.7. (1) If ℓ1 and ℓ2 are distinct non-parallel lines in F , then ℓ1 ∩ ℓ2 is a
point in F × F .
    (2) If ℓ is a line in F and D is a circle in F with ℓ ∩ D ̸= ∅, then ℓ ∩ D consists of
one or two points in the plane of F (u) for some u ∈ R with u2 ∈ F (the case u ∈ F is
allowed).
    (3) If D1 and D2 are distinct circles in F and D1 ∩ D2 ̸= ∅, then D1 ∩ D2 consists of
one or two points in the plane of F (u) for some u ∈ R with u2 ∈ F .

Proof. Recall that a line in F has equation ax + by + c = 0 with a, b, c ∈ F and a, b not
both 0, and a circle in F has equation

                            x2 + y 2 + ax + by + c = 0, a, b, c ∈ F.

One easily deduces the results by solving the equations ℓ = 0 and D = 0.

Theorem 1.14.8. If c ∈ C, i.e. c is a constructible number, then c is algebraic over Q
and [Q(c) : Q] = 2n for some n ≥ 0.

Proof. By assumption, there is a finite sequence of ruler and compass constructions giving
the point (c, 0). At the first stage, we produce a point (a0 , b0 ) from intersections of the
form (1)-(3) using the subfield F0 = Q. By Lemma 1.14.7, there is an element u0 ∈ R
with u20 ∈ F0 such that F0 (a0 , b0 ) ∈ F0 (u0 ); here u0 ∈ F is allowed. Letting F1 = F0 (u0 ),
we continue to produce a point (a1 , b1 ) with F1 as the base field. We then get u1 ∈ R
with u21 ∈ F1 such that F1 (a1 , b1 ) ⊂ F1 (u1 ). Continuing this procedure, we get a sequence
of fields Fk such that Fk ⊂ Fk+1 and [Fk+1 : Fk ] = 1 or 2. Now the procedure stops after
finitely many steps, and Q(c) ⊂ Fn . Since [Fn : Q] = 2n , [Q(c) : Q] is also a power of
2.

       The above discussion can be generalized as follows. Choose a subfield F ⊂ R and let

                          CF := {c ∈ R : c is constructible from F }.

By a similar discussion as above, especially by Theorem 1.14.4, we get the following result.

Theorem 1.14.9. If K ⊂ R is a normal extension of F and [K : F ] = 2n for some n ≥ 0,
then F ⊂ CF . Conversely, for any c ∈ CF , c is algebraic over F and [Q(c) : Q] = 2n for
some n ≥ 0.

  Below we will apply the above results to the three classical problems in Greek math-
ematics, namely, squaring the circle, doubling the cube and trisecting an angle.
  1. Squaring the circle: construct a square whose area is equal to that of a circle.
                               √
  This amounts to constructing π. As π is not algebraic,(13) this is not possible by
Theorem 1.14.8.
(13)
   This was proved by Lindemann in 1882. His proof is based on the transcendence of e proved by
Hermite in 1873 and Euler’s identity eiπ + 1 = 0.


                                              36
   2. Doubling the cube: given the length of an edge of a cube, construct a second
cube having double the volume of the first.
                                 √            √
   This amounts to constructing 3 2. Since [Q( 3 2) : Q] = 3, this is not possible by
Theorem 1.14.8.
   3. Trisecting an arbitrary angle: construct θ/3 for a given angle θ.
   Here, we say an angle θ is constructible if we can construct two intersecting lines with
angle θ, equivalently, if the point (cos(θ), sin(θ)) is constructible.

Lemma 1.14.10. Let θ ∈ R. The following statements are equivalent:

  (i) The angle θ is constructible.

 (ii) The number cos(θ) is constructible.

(iii) The number sin(θ) is constructible.

Proof. This is obvious.

   As a consequence, if θ, θ′ are two constructible angles, then the angle mθ + nθ′ is also
constructible for m, n ∈ Z, using trigonometric identities.

Example 1.14.11. If θ = π/3 = 60◦ , since cos(θ) = 1/2, θ is a constructible angle. Now
let α = θ/3 = π/9. Then we have

                           1/2 = cos(3α) = 4 cos3 (α) − 3 cos(α),

so that cos(α) satisfies the equation

One checks that f is irreducible, hence [Q(cos(α)) : Q)] = 3 and α is not constructible.

                                              37
   4. Constucting regular polynons. Next we turn to the problem of constructing a
regular polygon with n sides where n ∈ N (i.e. a polygon whose sides have equal length).
For each n ∈ N, let θn be the angle with radian measure 2π/n. We have the following
observation:
    Fact: The problem of constructing a regular polygon with n sides is equivalent to that
of constructing the angle θn , for we may inscribe the regular polygon in the unit circle.
   By Lemma 1.14.10, this amonts to constructing the number cos(θn ). A more important
observation is the following theorem.

Theorem 1.14.13. Let ξn be a primitive n-th root of unity. A regular polygon with n
sides is constructible if and only if [Q(ξn ) : Q] is a power of 2.

Proof. We may take ξn = eiθn . The Euler formula says

                     ξn = cos(θn ) + i sin(θn ), ξn−1 = cos(θn ) − i sin(θn )

which give the relation
                                     ξn + ξn−1 = 2 cos(θn ).
In particular cos(θn ) ∈ Q(ξn ) and [Q(ξn ) : Q(cos(θn ))] = 1 or 2, because

                                     ξn2 + 1 = 2 cos(θn )ξn .

    Assume cos(θn ) is constructible, then [Q(cos(θn )) : Q] is a power of 2, so is [Q(ξn ) : Q].
    Conversely, assume [Q(ξn ) : Q] is a power of 2. Then, []Q(cos(θn )) : Q] is also a power
of 2. Since Q(ξn )/Q is an abelian Galois extension, Q(cos(θn )) is also a Galois extension
of Q. Hence Q(cos(θn )) ⊂ C and cos(θn ) is constructible.

    Combined with results in §1.11, we deduce the following sufficient and necessary con-
dition for a regular polygon with n sides to be constructible.

Corollary 1.14.14. A regular polygon with n sides is constructible if and only if n has
prime decomposition
                                  n = 2k p1 · · · ps
where k, s ≥ 0, and pi is a Fermat prime for each i.
                                                                                m
   Recall that a Fermat prime is a prime number p of the form 1 + 22 for some m, like
p = 3, 5, 17, ... This is equivalent to requiring p = 1 + 2m for some m by the next Lemma.

Lemma 1.14.15. If 1 + 2m is a prime, then m is a 2-power.

Proof. For odd m, we have (x + y)|(xm + y m ). Thus if m ≥ 3 is odd, then 3|(1 + 2m ) and
so 1 + 2m can not be a prime. If m = kl where k is even and l ≥ 3 is odd, then similarly
(1 + 2k )|(1 + 2m ). Therefore m can not have any odd prime factor.


                                               38
1.15     Infinite Galois theory
Topological groups
Definition 1.15.1. A group G equipped with a topology is a topological group if the maps

                       G × G → G, (g, h) 7→ gh;       G → G, g 7→ g −1

are both continuous.

Example 1.15.2. • (R, +) and (R× , ×) are topological groups.
   • If G is a finite group, it becomes a topological group when equipped with the discrete
topology.

    It follows from the definition that left multiplication La : G → G, g 7→ ag is continuous,
since it is the composition of
                                   g7→(a,g)        (g,h)7→gh
                                 G −→ G × G −→ G.

Moreover, La is a homeomorphism with inverse La−1 .

Lemma 1.15.3. Let G be a topological group and H be a subgroup of G.

  (i) Equipped with the subspace topology, H is a topological goup.

 (ii) The closure of H is still a subgroup. Moreover, if H is normal, then so is its closure.

(iii) G is Hausdorff if and only if {e} is closed in G.

Proof. Exercise.

Lemma 1.15.4. Let H ⊂ G be a subgroup.

  (i) If H is open, then H is also closed.

 (ii) If H is closed of finite index, then H is open. The converse also holds when G is
      compact.

Proof. (i) Let S be a set of representatives of cosets G/H, then G\H = ∪σ∈S\{e} σH. Note
that each σH is open, and any union of open sets is still open, hence H is closed.
    (ii) If [G : H] is finite, then S is finite and H is open because it is the complementary
of a finite union of closed sets, which is again closed. When G is compact and H is open,
the coset decomposition in (i) gives a disjoint open cover of G, hence the cover must be
finite.

   As a consequence, topological groups are nice topological spaces: topological properties
can be just tested around the identity!



                                              39
   Let {Gi | i ∈ I} be a family of groups indexed by a directed (14) partially ordered set
I and suppose we have a family of morphisms

                                        pij : Gj → Gi , ∀i, j ∈ I, i ≤ j,

called transition maps, with the following properties:

       • pii is the identity map;

       • pik = pij ◦ pjk for all i ≤ j ≤ k.

We call the data (Gi , Gij ) an inverse system of groups indexed by I.
    The inverse limit lim Gi is defined to be a group G with group homomorphisms pi :
                         ←−
G → Gi such that pij ◦ pj = pi , ∀i, j ∈ I, i ≤ j, satisfying the following universal property:
if (H, fi ) is another solution such that pij ◦ fj = fi , then there is a unique homomorphism
θ : H → G such that pi ◦ θ = fi for all i ∈ I.
    From the universal property it is easy to see that the inverse limit, if exists, is unique
up to a unique isomorphism. In the category of groups, the inverse limit indeed exists
and has the following explicit description
                                         Y
                         lim Gi = (gi ) ∈      Gi | pij (gj ) = gi ∀i ≤ j .
                         ←−
                                                    i∈I
                                              Q
