                     ←−
    Any product of Hausdorff topological spaces is Hausdorff and any subspace of a Haus-
dorff space is Hausdorff. The result follows.
    Recall that a space X is called totally disconnected if for every x ∈ X the connected
component containing x is {x} itself.(15) We know that any product of totally disconnected
spaces is totally disconnected and any subspace of a totally disconnected space is totally
disconnected. Clearly, each Gi is disconnected, thus lim Gi is totally disconnected.
                                                      ←−
Theorem 1.15.8. If the transition morphisms Gi → Gj are all surjective, then the
morphisms G → Gi are also surjective.

Proof. Exercise.

Krull topology
Let K/F be a (possibly infinite) Galois extension. For any intermediate field E, K/E is
still Galois, whose group Gal(K/E) is a subgroup of Gal(K/F ). Set G = Gal(K/F ).

Remark 1.15.9. For any F -embedding τ : K → K, it is automatically an automorphism.
Indeed, let x ∈ K and take a finite Galois subextension E/F which contains x. Then
τ |E : E → K must have image equal to E, in particular, x ∈ Im(τ ).

Definition 1.15.10. We take

                         N = {Gal(K/E) : E/F is finite and Galois}

as a neighbourhood basis (16) of IdK ∈ G. For any σ ∈ G, let σN be its neighbourhood
basis. The topology thus defined is called the Krull topology on G.

       It is direct to check that G becomes a topological group under the Krull topology.

Remark 1.15.11. (i) Equally, we can take

                                N ′ = {Gal(K/E) : E/F is finite}
(15)
    With the subspace topology from R, Q is totally disconnected but not discrete.
(16)
    A neighbourhood basis for a point x in a topological space X is a family of neighbourhoods N such
that for any open set U containing x, there exists N ∈ N such that N ⊂ U.



                                                 41
as a neighbourhood basis of IdK . In fact, if E/F is finite and if E ′ denotes its Galois closure,
then E ′ /F is finite and Galois. Since Gal(K/E) contains a finite index subgroup Gal(K/E ′ )
which is open, it is open too.
    (ii) For any intermediate field E, Gal(K/E) is closed. In fact, Let F ⊂ E ′ ⊂ E be all finite
subextensions of F , then E is the compositum of all such E ′ , and
                                                \
                                   Gal(K/E) =     Gal(K/E ′ )
                                                   E′

is the intersection of closed subsets (as Gal(K/E ′ ) is open).

Lemma 1.15.12. For any intermediate field E which is finite Galois over F , the map

                                  G → Gal(E/F ), σ 7→ σ|E

is a continuous surjection. Here Gal(E/F ) is equipped with the discrete topology.

Proof. For any ι : E → E, we can extend it to an F -isomorphism K → K, see Lemma
1.3.10 (and Remark 1.15.9). Hence the morphism is surjective. The kernel of the mor-
phism is Gal(K/E) which is open, so it is continuous.

    We index all finite Galois extensions E/F contained in K by I, in such a way that
E ≤ E ′ iff E ⊆ E ′ . Then I is a partially order set and is directed as E, E ′ ≤ EE ′ . This
is an inverse system, so we can define the inverse limit limE Gal(E/F ). Using Lemma
                                                            ←−
1.15.12, we obtain a continuous morphism of topological groups

                                     ι : G → lim Gal(E/F ).
                                             ←−
                                               E

Proposition 1.15.13. The map ι is an homeomorphism.

Proof. If σ ∈ G is such that σ|E = IdE for all finite Galois subextensions E/F , then σ
must be identity on K since every element of K is contained in some finite subextension
E. This proves the injectivity of ι.
    Conversely, if (σE )E ∈ limE Gal(E/F ), then we define an F -automorphism σ of K as
                            ←−
follows: for x ∈ K, choose E large enough containing x and let σ(x) := σE (x) ∈ E ⊂ K.
This is well defined: if we choose another E ′ , then, since EE ′ /F is also finite and Galois,
we have
                                 σE ′ (x) = σEE ′ (x) = σE (x).
   Since ι is continuous, it suffices to prove that ι is open. Let E ′ /F be a finite Galois
extension, so that H = Gal(K/E ′ ) is open in G for the Krull topology. We need to show
that it is also open for the inverse limit topology on limE Gal(E/F ), which is clear because
                                                       ←−
                           Y
                               Gal(E/F ) × {IdE ′ } ∩ G = Gal(K/E ′ ).
                                                   
                         E̸=E ′

This proves the openness of ι.

                                                42
Corollary 1.15.14. Under the Krull topology, G is compact Hausdorff and totally dis-
connected.

Proof. This follows directly from Proposition 1.15.7 and Proposition 1.15.13.

Proposition 1.15.15. (1) For any Galois extension K/F , K Gal(K/F ) = F .
  (2) Let H be any subgroup of G, then Gal(K/K H ) is the closure of H.

Proof. (1) Every element in K/F lies in a finite Galois extension of F , so this follows
from the finite case.
   (2) Since Gal(K/K H ) is closed and contains H, it contains H. Now take σ ∈ / H, we
want to show that σ moves some element in K H , i.e. σ ∈ / Gal(K/K H ). Since σ ∈  / H,
there exists an open set Gal(K/E) (where E/F is finite Galois) such that

                                    σGal(K/E) ∩ H = ∅.

Consider ϕ : G → Gal(E/F ) which is surjective with kernel Gal(K/E); let ϕ(H) be the
image of H. The assumption implies that ϕ(σ) = σ|E ∈         / ϕ(H). Therefore, by Galois
                                                    ϕ(H)
correspondence in finite case, i.e. ϕ(H) = Gal(E/E       ), σ moves some element in E ϕ(H) .
But one checks that E ϕ(H) ⊂ K H , the result follows.

   We deduce the main theorem of infinite Galois theory.

Theorem 1.15.16. Let K/F be Galois with Galois group G. There is one-to-one bijection
between the set of closed subgroups of G and the set of intermediate fields between K and
F , given by
                               H 7→ K H , E 7→ Gal(K/E).
Moreover

  (i) a closed subgroup H is normal if and only if K H is Galois over F and in this case,
      Gal(K H /F ) ∼
                   = G/H.

 (ii) H is open if and only if K H is finite over F , and in this case [G : H] = [K H : F ].

Proof. The one-to-one correspondence is easily derived from the Proposition 1.15.15.
    (i) The same proof of Corollary 1.6.2 is also valid here.
    (ii) If H is open, then it contains Gal(K/E) for some finite Galois extension E/F . We
have K H ⊂ K Gal(K/E) = E where the equality follows from Proposition 1.15.15. Since
E/F is finite, K H /F is also finite. Conversely, since H is assumed to be closed, we have
Gal(K/K H ) = H by Proposition 1.15.15 again. If K H /F is finite, then H is open by
definition.
    To see the equality [G : H] = [E : F ] where E = K H , we note that when H is
normal the assertion follows from (i). In general, let E ′ be the Galois closure of E and
H ′ = Gal(K/E ′ ) < H, then [G : H ′ ] = [E ′ : F ] as just noted. Since [H : H ′ ] = [E ′ : E]
by (i) applied to K/E, the result follows.

by Frobenius. Since Fp = lim Fpn is an algebraic closure of Fp , we get
                          −→

Remark 1.15.19. In a profinite group, a (normal) subgroup of finite index need not be
closed. In our situation, there exists H < G = Gal(K/F ) of finite index such that H = G,
hence K H = F = K G . This does not contradict Artin’s Lemma (since |H| = +∞).
                             √ √ √                                                  √
    Example: Let K = Q( 2, 3, 5, ...) be the extension obtained by adjoining p to
Q, for all prime numbers p. If pn denotes the n-th prime number, then
                                                                  n

       G := Gal(K/Q) ∼     Gal(Q( p1 , . . . , pn )/Q) ∼       Z/2Z ∼

This group admits a subgroup H :=        n≥1 Z/2Z which is proper and dense (hence not
closed). By taking the pre-image of a proper subgroup of finite index in the quotient group
G/H, we obtain a (proper) subgroup of finite index in G which is not closed (because it
contains H).


1.16     Norm and trace
Let K/F be a finite extension.

Definition 1.16.1. For any α ∈ K, let mα : K → K be the F -linear map defined by
mα (x) = αx. Set
                             NK/F (α) = det(mα ) ∈ F
                                  TrK/F (α) := Tr(mα ) ∈ F.

   By linear algebra, we have the following facts.

   - NK/F (−) is multiplicative: NK/F (αβ) = NK/F (α)NK/F (β). If a ∈ F , then NK/F (aα) =
     a[K:F ] NK/F (α).

   - TrK/F (−) is additive and F -linear: TrK/F (α + β) = TrK/F (α) + TrK/F (β); if a ∈ F
     then TrK/F (aα) = aTrK/F (α).

   - In particular, α ∈ F implies NK/F (α) = α[K:F ] and TrK/F (α) = [K : F ]α.

Corollary 1.16.2. The trace map TrK/F : K → F is either surjective or identically zero.

                                             44
Lemma 1.16.3 (Transitivity). Let F ⊂ E ⊂ K be a tower of finite extensions. Then

                       NE/F ◦ NK/E = NK/F , TrE/F ◦ TrK/E = TrK/F .

Proof. An easy exercise.

      We have the following explicit description.

Lemma 1.16.4. Let K = F (α) be a simple extension of F . Let P be the minimal
polynomial, say P (X) = X n + an−1 X n−1 + · · · + a0 . Then

                          NF (α)/F (α) = (−1)n a0 , TrF (α)/F = −an−1 .

Proof. Use the F -basis {1, α, ..., αn−1 } to represent the linear transformation mα .

Proposition 1.16.5. If K/F is finite extension, then

Proof. Consider the tower F ⊂ F (α) ⊂ K. We saw that

(6)                                NK/F (α) = NF (α)/F (α)[K:F (α)] .

                        HomF (K, F ) → HomF (F (α), F ), σ 7→ σ|F (α) .

Corollary 1.16.6. Let K/F be a finite extension. Then K/F is separable iff TrK/F is
surjective iff TrK/F is non-zero.

                                                   45
Proof. If K/F is inseparable, then F has positive characteristic, say p. Proposition 1.16.5
implies that TrK/F is identically zero.
     If K/F is separable, we need to prove that TrK/F is non-zero. By Proposition 1.16.5,
                                      P
it is enough to find some α such that σ σ(α) ̸= 0. We conclude by the next theorem.
Theorem 1.16.7 (Dedekind). Let χ1 , ..., χn be distinct characters G → L× . They are
linearly independent: if c1 , ..., cn ∈ L satisfy

                             c1 χ1 (g) + · · · + cn χn (g) = 0, ∀g ∈ G

then ci = 0 for 1 ≤ i ≤ n.
Proof. The proof is analogue to the technique used in Artin’s lemma 1.5.4. The assump-
tion that χi are distinct is necessary.
Example 1.16.8. Let K/F be an extension of finite fields. Then NK/F and TrK/F are
both surjective.

1.17     Galois cohomology
Let G be a finite group. A G-module is an abelian group A together with an action of G,
i.e. a map G × A → A, (g, a) 7→ g(a) such that
 (a) (gg ′ )a = g(g ′ a);

 (b) 1a = a;

 (c) g(a + a′ ) = ga + ga′ .
   A morphism between two G-modules A1 , A2 is a group homomorphism f : A1 → A2
which is G-equivariant, i.e. f (ga1 ) = gf (a1 ) for any g ∈ G and a1 ∈ A1 .
   Let Z[G] denote the group algebra of G. Precisely,
                                         X
                                 Z[G] =          ag g, ag ∈ Z
                                                g∈G

which is a free Z-module with a natural basis indexed by elements of G. The multiplication
in Z[G] is given by             X         X          X
                               (    ag g)(   bh h) =    ck k
                                   g∈G         h∈G            k∈G


                                                  46
           P
where ck =   gh=k ag bh . It is direct to check that a G-module is the same as a (left)
Z[G]-module.
Example 1.17.1. Let K be a Galois extension of F with galois group G. Then (K, +)
and (K × , ×) are G-modules.
   Given a G-module A, we define cohomology groups H n (G, A) for n ≥ 0 as follows.
   • If n = 0, C 0 (G, A) := A; if n ≥ 1,

                                    C n (G, A) := {functions f : Gn → A}.

   • Define dn : C n (G, A) → C n+1 (G, A) as follows:
      (dn )f (g1 , . . . , gn+1 ) = g1 f (g2 , . . . , gn+1 ) + ni=1 (−1)i f (g1 , . . . , gi gi+1 , . . . , gn+1 )
                                                               P

                                    +(−1)n+1 f (g1 , . . . , gn ).

   • One checks dn ◦ dn−1 = 0, so we obtain a complex

                         0 → C 0 (G, A) → C 1 (G, A) → · · · → C n (G, A) → · · · .

Definition 1.17.2. For n ≥ 0, define Z n (G, A) := Ker(dn ) and call its elements n-
cocycles, B n (G, A) := Im(dn−1 ) and call its elements n-coboundaries; define the cohomol-
ogy groups
                                                  Z n (G, A)
                                   H n (G, A) := n           .
                                                  B (G, A)
Example 1.17.3. For n = 0, H 0 (G, A) = Ker(d0 ), where d0 : A → C 1 (G, A) is given by
a 7→ (g 7→ ga − a). It is clear that H 0 (G, A) = AG .
Example 1.17.4. For n = 1, d1 : C 1 (G, A) → C 2 (G, A) is given by

                           (d1 f )(g1 , g2 ) = g1 f (g2 ) + (−1)f (g1 g2 ) + f (g1 ).

Thus Z 1 (G, A) is exactly the set of so-called crossed homomorphisms, i.e. f : G → A
satisfying
                               f (g1 g2 ) = f (g1 ) + g1 · f (g2 ).
By Example 1.17.3, B 1 (G, A) consists of those crossed homomorphisms of the form ga − a
for a ∈ A, called principal crossed homomorphisms. We then have
                                              {crossed homomorphisms}
                       H 1 (G, A) :=                                        .
                                         {principal crossed homomorphisms }
Remark 1.17.5. When the action of G on A is trivial, i.e. g · a = a for any g ∈ G
and a ∈ A, a crossed homomorphism is just a usual group homomorphism. Moreover, as
ga − a = 0, we have Z 1 (G, A) = 0 and so

                                         H 1 (G, A) = Hom(G, A),

where Hom(G, A) denotes the set of group homomorphisms from G to A.

                                                        47
Remark 1.17.6. Note that if f is a crossed homomorphism, then we always have f (1) = 0
as f (1) = f (1 · 1) = f (1) + f (1).
    Assume that G = ⟨σ⟩ is a cyclic group of order n. Then a crossed homomorphism
f : G → A is determined by α := f (σ) and α satisfies

(7)                          (1 + σ + · · · + σ n−1 )α = f (σ n ) = f (1) = 0.

Conversely, if α ∈ A satisfies (7), then the formula

                                    f (σ i ) := (1 + σ + · · · + σ i−1 )α

defines a crossed homomorphism f : G → A. Thus there is a one-to-one correspondence
between the set of crossed homomorphisms and α ∈ A satisfying (7).

Theorem 1.17.7. Let K be a Galois extension of F with Galois group G. Then H 1 (G, K × ) =
0 and H 1 (G, K) = 0.

Proof. Let f be a crossed homomorphism G → K × . In multiplicative notation, this
means

Theorem 1.17.8 (Hilbert’s Theorem 90). (17) Let K be a finite extension of F with cyclic
Galois group G = ⟨σ⟩.
   (i) If α ∈ K × with NK/F (α) = 1 then α = σ(β)/β for some β ∈ K × .
   (ii) If TrK/F (α) = 0, then α = σ(β) − β for some β ∈ K.

f (σ) = α. Then apply Theorem 1.17.7. (i) is proved in a similar way.

1.18    Kummer theory
Recall that we have considered the following situation: K is the splitting field of X n −
a for some a ∈ F ; we know that Gal(K/F ) is solvable, with both Gal(F (ζn )/F ) and
Gal(K/F (ζn )) being abelian. Now we study in detail Galois extensions of type K/F (ζn )
in a more general context.
    In this subsection, we fix a field F and n ∈ N satisfying the following condition:

                        F contains a primitive n-th root of unity.

This is equivalent to requiring that F contains n distinct roots of unity. Remark that this
implies that p does not divide n when char(F ) = p.
   Denote by µn the subgroup of n-th roots of unity in F . Note that µn is in fact cyclic.

Lemma 1.18.1. Let a ∈ F × and m be the order of a in F × /(F × )n . Then every irreducible
factor of X n − a ∈ F [X] has the form X m − b for some b ∈ F × .

Proof. Let α ∈ F be a root of P (X) = X n − a. It suffices to prove that the minimal
polynomial of α, say Q, has the form X m − b.
    By definition of m, am ∈ (F × )n , so there exists b ∈ F × such that am = bn . Since
α = a, we get αnm = bn , i.e. αm /b ∈ µn ⊂ F × , so αm ∈ F × . As a consequence, Q
 n

divides X m − b for some b ∈ F × . In particular d := deg Q ≤ m.
    We need to prove m = d. Since P = n−1               i
                                          Q
                                            i=0 (X − αξn ), there exists a subset P
                                                                                  S of Z/nZ
                  Q             i                                              d      i
such that Q = i∈S (X − αξn ) (thus |S| = d). Expanding it, we see that α ξn i∈S ∈ F .
Since ξn ∈ F , we deduce αd ∈ F × , so ad ∈ (F × )n (as αn = a). But m is the order of a in
F × /(F × )n , so m|d and finally m = d.

Remark 1.18.2. In Lemma 1.18.1, we see that any irreducible factor of X n − a has
degree dividing n. This is not true if F does not contain µn ; for example, X 3 − 1 ∈ Q[X]
has an irreducible factor of degree 2.

Lemma 1.18.7. If K/F is a finite Galois extension with Galois group G = C1 × · · · Cr ,
(18)
                                                             Q
  then K = K1 · · · Kr , where Kj is the fixed field of Hj := i̸=j Cj × {1}.

Proof. By induction, we only need prove it for r = 2. Observe that K1 ∩ K2 = F : let
x ∈ K1 ∩ K2 , then x is fixed by H1 and H2 , hence by G, hence belongs to F . Since K1 /F
and K2 /F are Galois extensions,(19) we know by Proposition 1.6.3(ii)

                     Gal(K1 K2 /K2 ) = Gal(K1 /(K1 ∩ K2 )) = Gal(K1 /F ).

In particular, we have

           [K1 K2 : F ] = [K1 K2 : K2 ][K2 : F ] = [K1 : F ][K2 : F ] = |C1 ||C2 | = |G|,

hence K1 K2 = K.

Remark 1.18.8. The proof of Theorem 1.18.6 gives the following: If K/F is a cyclic
                                                               √

Reformulation using Galois cohomology
We have defined H n (G, A) for a G-module A, with H 0 (G, A) = AG . We admit the
following fact from homological algebra: if 0 → A1 → A → A2 → 0 is a short exact
sequence of G-modules, then there is a long exact sequence
                                       ∂
            0 → AG    G   G    1            1          1
                 1 → A → A2 → H (G, A1 ) → H (G, A) → H (G, A2 ) → · · ·


where ∂ is called the connecting morphism.
   Let K/F be a finite Galois extension with G := Gal(K/F ). We have the following
exact sequence of G-modules:
                                                    x7→xn
                                  1 → µn → K × −→ (K × )n → 1

where µn carries the trivial G-action. We obtain a long exact sequence

               1 → µn → F × → (K × )n ∩ F × → H 1 (G, µn ) → H 1 (G, K × ) = 0

where the last equality follows from Hilbert’s Theorem 90. Moreover, note that H 1 (G, µn ) ∼
                                                                                            =
Hom(G, µn ), see Remark 1.17.5. Therefore we obtain an isomorphism of groups, called
Kummer isomorphism

                           ((K × )n ∩ F × )/(F × )n ∼= Hom(G, µn )

1.19     Artin-Schreier extensions
Let F be a field of characteristic p.

Lemma 1.19.1. For any a ∈ F , the polynomial X p − X − a ∈ F [X] is either irreducible
or completely reducible (i.e. every irreducible factor has degree 1).

Proof. Write f = X p − X − a. First observe that if α ∈ F is a root of f , then α + j (for
j ∈ Fp ) is also a root of f . Hence f decomposes in F [X] as
                                        Y
                                    f=     (X − (α + j)).
                                        j∈Fp


In particular, if f (X) has one root in F , then f is completely reducible over F .
   We assume f is reducible in F [X], say f = gh, with 1 ≤ d := deg g ≤ p − 1. Then g
has the form                            Y
                                   g=       (X − (α + j))
                                        j∈S
                                                                         P
for some subset S ⊂ Fp } with |S| = d. Expanding the expression we get j∈S (α + j) =
      P
dα + j∈S j ∈ F , and so dα ∈ F . As 1 < d < p − 1, d ̸= 0 in F , so α ∈ F . As explained
in the first paragraph, this implies that f is completely reducible.

Proposition 1.19.2. A field extension K/F is cyclic of degree p if and only if K = F (α)
where α is a root of X p − X − a ∈ F [X] and a is not of the form cp − c for any c ∈ F .

Proof. ⇐= The condition that a ̸= cp − c for any c ∈ F means that X p − X − a ∈ F [X]
is irreducible. As in the proof of Lemma 1.19.1, the conjugates of α are just α + j for
j ∈ Fp . Since these elements are distinct, F (α) is a Galois extension over F of degree p.
    =⇒ Assume K/F is cyclic of degree p, say Gal(K/F ) = ⟨σ⟩. Since TrK/F (1) = p = 0,
Hilbert Theorem 90 implies that 1 = σ(α) − α for some α ∈ K, i.e. σ(α) = α + 1.
Consequently, σ i (α) = α + i for 1 ≤ i ≤ p − 1, and in this way we get all the conjugates



                                               52
of α (p conjugates in total). This shows [F (α) : F ] = p, thus K = F (α). Finally, since
Gal(K/F ) = ⟨σ⟩ and

                        σ(αp − α) = (α + 1)p − (α + 1) = αp − α,

by Galois correspondence we deduce a := αp − α ∈ F , so the minimal polynomial divides
X p − X − a. They must be equal comparing their degrees.

Definition 1.19.3. An Artin-Schreier extension is a finite Galois extension whose group
is an elementary abelian p-group.

Theorem 1.19.4. An extension is Artin-Schreier if and only if K = F (α1 , . . . , αr ) where
αj is a root of X p − X − aj for suitable aj ∈ F .

Proof. Analogue to the proof of Theorem 1.18.6.

Reformulation via Galois cohomology
Let F be as above, and K/F be a finite Galois extension with G = Gal(K/F ). It is clear
the map f : x 7→ xp − x is a homomorphism of G-modules (K, +) → (K, +); let P(K)
denote the image of f . Consider the short exact sequence of G-modules
                                               f
                                0 → Fp → K → P(K) → 0

where P(K) := {xp − x|x ∈ K} ⊂ K is an additive subgroup of K.
  We obtain a long exact sequence

                0 → Fp → F → P(K) ∩ F → H 1 (G, Fp ) → H 1 (G, K) = 0

where the last equality follows from Hilbert Theorem 90. Note that H 1 (G, Fp ) = Hom(G, Fp ),
we deduce an isomorphism

(10)                   (P(K) ∩ F )/P(F ) ∼
                                         = Hom(G, Fp ),       a 7→ θa

where the homomorphism θa : G → Fp is given by θa (σ) = σ(α) − α by choosing a root α
of X p − X − a. It is direct to check that θa is well-defined (i.e. independent of the choice
of α) and is indeed a group homomorphism.
    Let Hθ < G be the kernel of θ ∈ Hom(G, Fp ) and Kθ = K Hθ , then Kθ = F (α) if θ
corresponds to a ∈ (P(K) ∩ F )/P(F ) and α is a root of X p − X − a.
    The isomorphism (10) also implies a pairing

                           G × (P(K) ∩ F )/P(F ) −→ Fp
                                  (σ, a)         7−→ θa (σ)

which is perfect when K/F is Artin-Schreier.


                                             53
1.20      Transcendental extensions
Let F be a field and Ω/F an extension. Given α1 , . . . , αn ∈ Ω, there exists an F -
homomorphism
                        ι : F [X1 , . . . , Xn ] → Ω, Xi 7→ αi .

Definition 1.20.1. The αi are said to be algebraically independent over F if ι is injective;
otherwise they are algebraically dependent over F .

    That is, they are algebraically dependent over F if there exists a non-zero polynomial
f (X1 , . . . , Xn ) such that f (α1 , . . . , αn ) = 0.
    An infinite set A ⊂ Ω is said to be algebraically independent over F if every finite
subset of A is algebraically independent; otherwise, it is algebraically dependent over F .

Lemma 1.20.2. Let A ⊂ Ω and β ∈ Ω. Suppose that A is algebraically independent over
F , but that A ∪ {β} is not. Then β is algebraic over F (A).

Proof. The assumption means that there exist α1 , . . . , αn ∈ A and a non-zero polynomial
f (X1 , . . . , Xn , Y ) ∈ F [X1 , . . . , Xn , Y ] such that f (α1 , . . . , αn , β) = 0. Then we can write

                                f = gm Y m + gm−1 Y m−1 + · · · + g0 .

Since A is algebraically independent, the gi ’s (for 1 ≤ i ≤ m) are not all zero, from which
the result follows.

Definition 1.20.3. A transcendence basis for Ω over F is an algebraically independent
set A ⊂ Ω such that Ω is algebraic over F (A).

Theorem 1.20.4. Every algebraically independent subset of Ω is contained in a transcen-
dence basis for Ω over F . In particular, transcendence bases exist.
    Conversely, if A ⊂ Ω is a subset such that Ω is algebraic over F (A), then any maximal
algebraically independent subset of A is a transcendence basis.

Proof. Let S be the set of algebraically independent subsets of Ω containing the given
one. We partially order it by inclusion. Let T be a totally ordered subset of S and let
B = ∪A∈T A. We see that B ∈ S, i.e. B is still algebraically independent, because every
algebraic relation only involves finitely many elements. So, by Zorn’s lemma, there exists
a maximal algebraically independent subset A in S and Lemma 1.20.2 shows that Ω is
algebraic over F (A). The last assertion is clear.

   Similar to the fact that two bases of a finite dimensional F -vector space have the same
cardinality, it can be shown that any two (possibly infinite) transcendence bases for Ω
have the same cardinality. We first prove a key lemma.

Lemma 1.20.5. (The exchange property) Let A := {α1 , . . . , αn } be a subset of Ω. Let β ∈
Ω be an element which is algebraic over F (α1 , . . . , αn ) but not algebraic over F (α1 , . . . , αn−1 ).
The following statements hold.

                                                    54
    (i) αn is algebraic over F (A′ ), where A′ := {α1 , . . . , αn−1 , β}.
    (ii) If A is algebraically independent, then so is A′ .
    (iii) If A is a transcendence basis of Ω, then so is A′ .

Proof. (i) Since β is algebraic over F (α1 , . . . , αn ), there exist ui ∈ F (α1 , . . . , αn ), not all
0, such that
                                ur β r + · · · + u1 β + u0 = 0.
We may further assume ui ∈ F [α1 , . . . , αn ]. Write ui = fi (α1 , . . . , αn ) for fi ∈ F [X1 , . . . , Xn ]
and put f = ri=0 fi Y i , we get a non-zero polynomial f (X1 , . . . , Xn , Y ) ∈ F [X1 , . . . , Xn , Y ]
           P

such that
                        f (α1 , . . . , αn , Y ) ̸= 0, f (α1 , . . . , αn , β) = 0.
Now write f as a polynomial in Xn ,
                                         d
                                         X
(11)                               f=          gi (X1 , . . . , Xn−1 , Y )Xni
                                         i=0

and note that at least one of the polynomials gi (α1 , . . . , αn−1 , Y ) ̸= 0, say gi0 ; this is
because f (α1 , . . . , αn , Y ) ̸= 0. Since β is not algebraic over F (α1 , . . . , αn−1 ), we have
gi0 (α1 , . . . , αn−1 , β) ̸= 0 (recall the definition !). Therefore f (α1 , . . . , αn−1 , Xn , β) ̸= 0 (as
a polynomial in Xn ) by (11), and gives an algebraic relation of αn over F (α1 , . . . , αn−1 , β).
     (ii) Assume that A′ is algebraically dependent. Then, since A′ \{β} is algebraically
independent, by Lemma 1.20.2, β is algebraic over F (α1 , . . . , αn−1 ) which contradicts the
assumption.
     (iii) We need to show that Ω is algebraic over F (A′ ). It suffices to show that F (A) is
algebraic over F (A′ ), and again reduces to showing αn is algebraic over F (A′ ), which is
true by (i).

Theorem 1.20.6. If A and B are two transcendence bases for Ω/F , then A and B have
the same cardinality. The cardinality is called the transcendence degree of Ω/F .

Example 1.20.7. The transcendence degree of Q(π, e) over Q is either 1 or 2; the precise
answer is unknown because it is not known whether π and e are algebraically independent.

    We recall some basic facts about the cardinality of a set A, measuring the size of A.
It is denoted by |A| or #A.

  (i) When A is finite, |A| denotes the number of its elements.

 (ii) Two sets A, B are said to have the same cardinality if there exists a bijection between
      A and B. We write |A| ≤ |B| if there is an injective map of A into B. The
      Cantor-Schröder-Bernstein theorem says that |A| = |B| if and only if |A| ≤ |B| and
      |B| ≤ |A|.



                                                       55
 (iii) Assume Axiom of choice, the cardinalities of infinite sets are denoted by

                                             ℵ0 < ℵ1 < ℵ2 < · · ·

        For each ordinal α, ℵα+1 is the least cardinal number strictly greater than ℵα .

 (iv) The cardinality of the natural numbers is denoted ℵ0 , while the cardinality of the
      real numbers by “c”. The diagonal argument shows that ℵ0 < c. The continuum
      hypothesis(20)(21) says that c = ℵ1 .
                                             ˙
  (v) If B is infinite and |B| ≥ |A|, then |A∪B| = |A| + |B| = |B|.

 (vi) If A ̸= ∅ and B is infinite and |B| ≥ |A|, then |A × B| = |B|. It suffices to treat the
      case |A| = |B|, i.e. to prove |B × B| = |B|. (Exercise.)

(vii) If |A| is infinite, let S = {B ⊂ A, B finite}. Then |S| = |A|. Proof: S is a disjoint
      union of Sn , where Sn = {B ⊂ A : |B| = n}. It is clear that |Sn | ≤ |An | = |A|
      (since A is infinite). Hence |S| ≤ |A × N| = |A|.(22)

(viii) If B is infinite and if f : A → B is such that for any b ∈ B, f −1 (b) is a finite set, then
       |A| ≤ |B|. Indeed, consider Bn ⊂ B, the subset of b ∈ B such that |f −1 (b)| = n.
       Then
                                       f : An := f −1 (Bn ) → Bn
        is an n-to-1 map, so |An | ≤ |(Bn )n | ≤ |B n | = |B| (as B is infinite). Therefore
        A ,→ B × N as in (vii), hence the result.

Proof of Theorem 1.20.6. First treat the case when one of A, B is finite, and we must
show that the other is also finite and |A| = |B|. We may assume A is finite and write
A = {α1 , . . . , αn }. We do induction on n. If n = 0, i.e. A = ∅, then Ω is algebraic over
F , hence B is also empty. Suppose n ≥ 1 and the result is true for n − 1 case. Consider
A ∩ B. If it is not empty, then Ω has a transcendence basis over F (A ∩ B) of cardinal

                                           A\(A ∩ B) < n,

and by inductive hypothesis, B\(A ∩ B) has the same cardinality, hence |A| = |B|.
    Assume A ∩ B = ∅. Put K = F (α1 , . . . , αn−1 ). Since Ω is not algebraic over K,
there must exist some β ∈ B which is not algebraic over K. Since β is algebraic over
F (α1 , . . . , αn ), Lemma 1.20.5 shows that A′ := {α1 , . . . , αn−1 , β} is also a transcendence
basis. But A′ ∩ B ̸= ∅, the above argument then implies that |B| = |A′ | = |A|.
    Now we prove the case when A and B are both infinite. It suffices to prove the
inequality |A| ≥ |B| by symmetry. For any x ∈ A, it is algebraic over F (B), hence
 (20)
      It is independent of the ZF axiomatization of set theory.
 (21)
      The generalized continuum hypothesis (GCH) states that for every infinite set A, there are no cardi-
nals strictly between |A| and 2|A| .
 (22)
      Here for each n, we choose an injection ιn : Sn → A, then S → A × N, s 7→ (ιn (s), n) if s ∈ Sn , is
injective.


                                                   56
algebraic over F (Sx ) for some finite subset Sx ⊂ B. Put S = ∪x∈A Sx ⊂ B. Then
|S| ≤ |A| by property (viii) (which implies |∪˙ x∈A Sx | ≤ A). On the other hand, any
element of A is algebraic over F (S) and Ω is algebraic over F (A), so is algebraic over
F (S). Hence S ⊂ B must be a maximal algebraically independent subset, so S = B and
|B| ≤ |A|.

Proposition 1.20.8. Let F ⊂ E ⊂ K be field extensions. Then

                        trdeg(K/F ) = trdeg(K/E) + trdeg(E/F ).

                                                                            ˙ is a
Proof. If A, B are respectively a transcendence basis of E/F and K/E, then A∪B
transcendence basis for K/F .

Proposition 1.20.9. Any two algebraically closed fields with the same transcendence
degree over F are F -isomorphic.

Proof. Choose transcendence bases A and A′ for the two fields Ω and Ω′ . By assumption,
there exists a bijection A → A′ , which extends uniquely to an F -isomorphism F [A] →
F [A′ ], and hence to an an F -isomorphism of the fields F (A) → F (A′ ). Identifying F (A)
and F (A′ ), Ω and Ω′ are algebraic closures of the same field, hence are isomorphic.


Proposition 1.20.10. Let F be an infinite field. We have the following statements.

  (i) |F [X]| = |F |.

 (ii) If K/F is an algebraic extension, then |K| = |F |.

(iii) If K = F (A), where A = {αi } is an arbitrary set of indeterminates, then |F (A)| =
      max(|A|, |F |).

Proof. (i) Let F [X]n denote the subset of polynomials of degree n. Then F [X]n is bijective
to F n , so that |F [X]n | = |F | since F is infinite. Therefore there exists an injective map
F [X] ,→ F × N and the result follows.
    (ii) We may assume K is an algebraic closure of F . Then K is a disjoint union of
finite sets, each of which is the set of roots of a monic irreducible polynomial. We get a
map K → F [X] (with image being the set of monic irreducible polynomials), with finite
fibers. So |K| ≤ |F [X]| = |F |, giving the result.
    (iii) Since F (A) embeds into F [A] × F [A], we have F (A) = F [A]. If A is finite, then
we may do induction, hence reduce to show |F [X]| = |F |, which is proved in (i). For
infinite A, we have                        [
                             F [A] =                F [A′ ] ,→ H × F,
                                    A′ ⊂A, A′ finite

where H = {A′ ⊂ A, A′ finite}. Since |H| = |A|, we get the result.



                                                57
Corollary 1.20.11. Let F be an infinite field and K/F be any extension. Then

                              |K| = max(|F |, trdeg(K/F )).

Proof. Let A be a set of transcendence basis of K over F , then K is an algebraic extension
of F (A). So |K| = |F (A)| = max(|F |, |A|) by Proposition 1.20.10.

Example 1.20.12. (i) We know |C| = |R| = c > |Q|. Hence trdeg(R/Q) = |R|.
    (ii) There is an isomorphism of fields C ∼
                                             = Qp , where Qp is the field of p-adic numbers.
To see this, we only need to check trdeg(Qp /Q) = |R|. By Corollary 1.20.11, it is enough
to show |Qp | = |R|. This follows from the construction of Qp .

Remark 1.20.13. If we have an integral domain R containing a field F , then we define
the transcendence degree of R over F to be that of Frac(R). In commutative algebra, when
R is noetherian, this is equal to the Krull dimension of R.

Definition 1.20.14. An extension Ω/F is called purely transcendental if it has a tran-
scendence basis A such that Ω = F (A).

   In general, subextensions of a purely transcendental extension need not be purely
transcendental. However, this is true when the transcendence degree is 1.

Theorem 1.20.15 (Lüroth). Let X be an indeterminante. If F ⊂ K ⊂ F (X), then
K = F (α) for some α ∈ F (X). In particular, every nontrivial extension of F contained
in F (X) is purely transcendental over F .




                                            58
2      Module theory
2.1     Modules and homomorphisms
We fix a ring R with unit.

Definition 2.1.1. A left R-module is an abelian group (M, +) equipped with a map

                                   R × M → M, (r, m) 7→ rm

called scalar multiplication, satisfying
    • r(r′ m) = (rr′ )m
    • r(m + m′ ) = rm + rm′
    • (r + r′ )m = rm + r′ m
    • 1m = m
for all r, r′ ∈ R and m, m′ ∈ M .

    If the scalar multiplication is written on the right (m, r) 7→ mr, we get the notion of
right modules, e.g. m(rr′ ) = (mr)r′ . (23)

Example 2.1.2. (1) For R = Z, a Z-module is just an abelian group.
   (2) For R = F a field, an R-modules is an F -vector space.
   (3) If F is a field, then an F [X]-module M is an F -module with an additional action
of X on M which is F -linear. In other words, an F [X]-module is an F -vector space M
combined with a linear map from M to M .
   (4) Let R = Mn (F ) where F is a field, and let V = F n (viewed as column vectors).
Then R acts on V by matrix multiplication, making V a left R-module. If we view V as
row vectors, then it becomes a right R-module.
   (5) If M is a left R-module and S → R is a ring homomorphism, then M becomes an
S-module.

   If S is another ring, an (R, S)-bimodule is an abelian group which is a left R-module
and a right S-module such that

                                 r(ms) = (rm)s, ∀r ∈ R, s ∈ S.

Example 2.1.3. (1) Every ring R is an (R, R)-bimodule.
    (2) When R is commutative, every left R-module is naturally an (R, R)-bimodule by
setting rmr′ := rr′ m.

Definition 2.1.4. A subset N ⊂ M is said a left sub-R-module of M if N is a commu-
tative subgroup of M and rn ∈ N if n ∈ N .
    Given a submodule N of M , we can form the quotient module M/N := {x + M }.
(23)
    A left R-module is the same thing as a right Rop -module, where Rop denotes the opposite ring: as a
set Rop = R, and with the same addition law, but a ∗ b := b · a.


                                                  59
Example 2.1.5. R itself is a left (or right) R-module; left (right) submodules of R are
called left (right) ideals.

   The set of submodules of a given module M , together with the two binary operations
+ and ∩,(24) forms a lattice which satisfies the modular law: Given submodules U , N1 ,
N2 of M such that N1 ⊂ N2 , then the following two submodules are equal:

                                   (N1 + U ) ∩ N2 = N1 + (U ∩ N2 ).

  If {mi } is a family of elements in M , there exists a minimal submodule of N containing
{mi }, called the submodule generated by them, denoted by ⟨mi ⟩.

Definition 2.1.6. A map ϕ : M → M ′ between two R-modules is called a homomorphism
if
   • ϕ is a homomorphism for the underlying abelian groups;
   • ϕ(rm) = rϕ(m) for r ∈ R and m ∈ M .

   Let ker(ϕ) := {m ∈ M |ϕ(m) = 0} be the kernel of ϕ, im(ϕ) := {m′ ∈ M ′ |∃m ∈
M, m′ = ϕ(m)} be the image of ϕ. One checks that ker(ϕ) is a submodule of M and
im(ϕ) is a submodule of M ′ . Recall the following facts:

 (1) if ϕ : M → M ′ is a homomorphism, then M/ ker(ϕ) ∼
                                                      = im(ϕ).

 (2) if ϕ is surjective then M/ ker(ϕ) ∼
                                       = M ′.

 (3) if M1 , M2 are submodules of M , then

                                      (M1 + M2 )/M1 ∼
                                                    = M2 /(M1 ∩ M2 ).

 (4) Consider HomR (M, M ′ ) which is an additive group:

                                         (ϕ + ϕ′ )(m) = ϕ(m) + ϕ′ (m).

         But note that in general there is no R-module structure on HomR (M, M ′ ).

 (5) When M = M ′ , we get EndR (M ), which becomes a ring: if ϕ : M → M and
     ϕ′ : M → M , then the composition ϕ ◦ ϕ′ is still an R-module homomorphism from
     M to M . Setting multiplication ϕ · ϕ′ := ϕ ◦ ϕ′ defines a multiplication operation
     on EndR (M ), which is in general not commutative ! For example, when M = F n ,
     EndF (M ) = Mn (F ) is not commutative.
(24)
       Arbitrary intersection of submodules is still a submodule

2.2    Free modules
Definition 2.2.1. An R-module is called free if it is isomorphic to a direct sum of copies
of R, i.e.
                                    M∼
                                           M
                                        =       Ri
                                                       i∈I

for some indexing set I, where each Ri is a copy of R. We write R(I) for the direct sum
above (and reserve the notation RI for the direct product).


                                                      61
   An R-module M is free iff it has a basis, i.e. a set {ei : i ∈ I} such that any element
of M is a unique finite linear combination of the ei ’s. Unlike vector spaces over fields,
submodules of free modules need not be free.

Remark 2.2.2. By convention, the zero module is free with ∅ as basis.

   Recall that our fixed ring R is non-zero.

Proposition 2.2.3. If R(I) ∼
                           = R(J) as left R-modules where I is infinite, then |I| = |J|.
                                          ∼
Proof. We fix an isomorphism ι : R(J) − → R(I) . Consider the canonical basis {ei : i ∈ I}
for R(I) and the basis {fj : j ∈ J} for R(J) . Then each ι(fj ) is contained in the sub-R-
         L                                                    S
module i∈Sj Rei for some finite subset Sj ∈ I. Let I0 := j∈J Sj ⊂ I. We must have
I0 = I, because i∈I0 Rei contains the whole R(J) . If J is finite, then I0 = I is also finite
                L

which is impossible. Hence J is infinite, and

                                     |I| ≤ |J × N| = |J|.

Exchanging I and J, we also get |J| ≤ |I|.

   However, Proposition 2.2.3 is no longer true when both I and J are finite.

Example 2.2.4. Let F be a field and V = F (N) which is an infinite dimensional F -vector
                                                             ˙
space. Let R = EndF (V ). Then by choosing a bijection N → N∪N,   we get an isomorphism
                                              ∼
V → V ⊕ V . Applying HomF (V, −), we get R = R ⊕ R.

Definition 2.2.5. A ring R is said to have IBN (Invariant Basis Number) if for any
n, m ∈ N, Rn ∼
             = Rm implies n = m. In this case, we define n to be the rank of Rn .

Proposition 2.2.6.       (i) If R is a field, then R has IBN.

 (ii) If R is commutative, then R has IBN.

(iii) If R is a finite ring, then R has IBN.

Proof. (i) This is a standard fact in linear algebra.
   (ii) Take a maximal ideal m of R. If Rn ∼     = Rm , then module m we get (R/m)n ∼
                                                                                    =
       n
(R/m) and the result follows from (i).
   (iii) If Rn ∼
               = Rm , then |Rn | = |Rm |, hence n = m.

Theorem 2.2.7. Every R-module is a quotient of some free R-module.

Proof. Take a set of generators of M , say {aλ }λ∈I ; it does exist since we can take M itself.
Then by universal property, there is a homomorphism of R-modules R(I) → M , which is
obviously surjective.




                                              62
2.3    Finitely generated modules
Definition 2.3.1. An R-module M is called finitely generated if it is isomorphic to a
quotient of a finite free module Rn , equivalently, if there exists m1 , . . . , mr such that

                                 M = Rm1 + · · · + Rmr .

It is also called a finite R-module.
    An R-module M is called cyclic if it is a quotient of R, i.e. it can be generated by one
element. In other words, a cyclic R-module is of the form R/a for some left ideal a.

Example 2.3.2. The left module V = F n over Mn (F ) is finitely generated. In fact it is
a cyclic module generated by m1 = (1, 0, . . . , 0)T .

   Recall that in a ring R with unity, Zorn’s lemma implies that R admits left maximal
ideals and right maximal ideals. We have an analogue for finitely generated modules.

Proposition 2.3.3. Let M be a non-zero finitely generated R-module. Then every proper
submodule of M is contained in a maximal proper submodule of M .

Proof. By assumption, M has a finite set of generators {mi ; i = 1, . . . , r}. A submodule
of M is proper if and only if it does not contain all the mi . Using this, we see that the
union of an increasing chain of proper submodules of M is still proper. Thus we may
conclude by Zorn’s lemma.

Definition 2.3.4. An R-module M is called simple if M ̸= 0 and M has no submodules
other than M and {0}.

Corollary 2.3.5. Any non-zero finitely generated R-module admits (at least) one simple
quotient module.

Example 2.3.6. (i) As a Z-module, Q is not finitely generated (looking at denominators
of a finite set of generators) and has no maximal proper submodule.
    (ii) Q is not a free Z-module. If it were, then since any r ̸= 0, r′ ̸= 0 are Z-linear
dependent, it must be a cyclic module. But this is not true by (i).

Annihilators
For any m ∈ M , Rm is a cyclic submodule of M . Denote

                              AnnR (m) := {r ∈ R | rm = 0}

which is a left ideal of R; we call it the annihilator of m. Denote

                             AnnR (M ) := {r ∈ R | rM = 0}

and call it the annihilator of M . Note that AnnR (M ) is a two-sided ideal of R.

                                             63
Definition 2.3.7. (1) An element m ∈ M is called torsion if AnnR (m) ̸= 0. The module
M is called torsion if any element of M is torsion; called torsion-free if the only torsion
element in M is zero.
   (2) M is called a faithful module if AnnR (M ) = 0.

Remark 2.3.8. (i) In general, an element m ∈ M and the submodule generated by m can
have different annihilators. For example, if R = Mn (F ), M = F n and m1 = (1, 0, . . . , 0)T
as in Example 2.3.2, then Rm1 = M and
                                
                    AnnR (m1 ) = (aij ) ∈ R | ai1 = 0 ,         Ann(M ) = 0.

    (ii) The above example also shows that there exists a torsion (cyclic) module which is
faithful.

Modules over PID
Let R be a commutative PID (principal ideal domain). Typical examples are R = Z or
R = F [X] where F is a field.

Theorem 2.3.9. Let M be a finitely generated R-module.

  (i) There is a R-module decomposition
                                                 s
                                               M M
                                    M = Rm                  R/(pni i
                                                                       
                                                      i=1

      where p1 , . . . , ps are prime elements and ni ≥ 1. Up to reordering, the ideals (pni i )
      are uniquely determined by M .

 (ii) There is a unique increasing sequence of proper ideals

                                      (d1 ) ⊇ (d2 ) ⊇ · · · ⊇ (dn )

      such that M is isomorphic to the sum of cyclic modules:

                                 M∼
                                          M M
                                    = Rm
                                                           
                                                    R/(di ) .
                                                       i

      The ideals (di ) are called invariant factors of M .

   Remark that a finite abelian group can be viewed as a finitely generated (torsion)
Z-module, so Theorem 2.3.9 implies the structure theorem for finite abelian groups.

Proposition 2.3.10. Let M be a free R-module of rank n and N be a non-zero submodule
of rank m ≤ n. Then N is also free. Moreover, there exists a basis {e1 , . . . , en } for M
and elements a1 |a2 | · · · |am of R such that {a1 e1 , . . . , am em } is a basis for N .


                                               64
2.4     Exact sequences
A sequence of R-module homomorphisms (finite or run to infinity in either direction)
                                                       f0             f1            f2
                     C • : · · · → M −1 → M 0 → M 1 → M 2 → M 3 → · · ·

is called a cochain complex if f i ◦f i−1 = 0 for any i. It is called exact if im(f i ) = ker(f i−1 )
for any i. We set
                                 H i (C • ) := ker(f i )/im(f i−1 ).
Then the complex C • is exact if and only if H i (C • ) = 0 for any i.
   In particular, we have the following basic facts.
                f
 (1) 0 → M → N is exact if and only if f is injective; also written as M ,→ N .
          g
 (2) M → N → 0 is exact if and only if g is surjective, also written as M ↠ N .

 (3) An exact sequence of the form
                                                        f             g
                                        0→M →N →P →0

      is called a short exact sequence, which means that f is injective, g is surjective, and
      ker(g) = im(f ).

 (4) Let f : M → N be a homomorphism of R-modules. Putting

                                             coker(f ) := N/im(f ),

      we obtain an exact sequence
                                                             f
                              0 → ker(f ) → M → N → coker(f ) → 0.

   The next famous result is referred as Snake lemma.

Lemma 2.4.1. Consider a commutative diagram of R-modules with exact rows:
                                                 f               g
                                        A             /B                  /C         /0

                                             α              β                   γ
                                                f′
                                                                g′
                                                                            
                               0       / A′           / B′                / C′

Then there exists an exact sequence
                       f           g              δ                        f¯′            g¯′
              ker(α) → ker(β) → ker(γ) → coker(α) → coker(β) → coker(γ).

Moreover, if f is injective, then f is also injective; if g ′ is surjective, then ḡ ′ is also
surjective.

                                                      65
Proof. We only give the construction of δ. Given c ∈ ker(γ) ⊂ C, we can lift it to b ∈ B as
g is surjective. Since γ(g(b)) = γ(c) = 0, we have g ′ (β(b)) = 0, i.e. β(b) ∈ ker(g ′ ) = im(g ′ ).
Thus there exists a′ ∈ A′ such that β(b) = f ′ (a′ ). We then define

                                δ(c) := image of a′ in coker(α).

The definition does not depend on the choice of b, because different choices would differ
by an element in A, which maps to 0 in coker(α).


2.5     Chain conditions
Definition 2.5.1. An R-module M is said to be noetherian if it satisfies the following
ascending chain condition (ACC): every chain

                                     M1 ⊂ M2 ⊂ M3 ⊂ · · ·

of submodules of M stabilizes, that is, there exists n such that Mn′ = Mn for all n′ ≥ n.

Definition 2.5.2. An R-module M is said to be artinian if it satisfies the following
descending chain condition (DCC): every chain

                                     M1 ⊃ M2 ⊃ M3 ⊃ · · ·

of submodules of M stabilizes, that is, there exists n such that Mn′ = Mn for all n′ ≥ n.

Lemma 2.5.3. The following characterization are equivalent:

 (1) M is noetherian;

 (2) Every non-empty family S of submodules of M contains a maximal element with
     respect to ⊂.

 (3) Every submodule is finitely generated.

Proof. (1)=⇒(2) Take any element M1 in S. If it is already maximal, then we are done;
if not, we can find another element M2 ∈ S such that M1 ⊊ M2 . In this way, we get a
sequence of strictly ascending chain. The noetherian property implies that the sequence
ends after finite steps.
    (2)=⇒(3) Fix a submodule M ′ of M . Consider the set S of all finitely generated
submodules of M ′ . It is non-empty since (0) ∈ S. By (2), S has a maximal element, say
N . We claim that M ′ = N . Otherwise, choose m ∈ M ′ \N , then N + Rm is still finitely
generated, a contradiction to the maximality of N . Hence M ′ = N is finitely generated.
    (3)=⇒(1) Consider an ascending chain M1 ⊂ M2 ⊂ · · · , and let M ′ denote the union
of the chain, which is still a submodule of M . By (3), M ′ is finitely generated, say by
{m1 , . . . , mr }. Then there exists n ≫ 1 such that m1 , . . . , mr ∈ Mn , so that Mn = M ′
and the chain is stable.

                                                66
   Similarly, being artinian is equivalent to that every non-empty family S of submodules
contains a minimal element. However, there is no counterpart (3) for artinian modules.

Definition 2.5.4. A ring R is said to be left noetherian (resp. left artinian) if it is
noetherian (resp. artinian) as a left R-module. Note that left submodules of R are just
left ideals of R.

Remark 2.5.5. There exist rings which are left noetherian but not right noetherian. For
example:
                             nα 0                      o
                        R=             , α ∈ Z, β, γ ∈ Q .
                                 β γ

Example 2.5.6.      (i) Z is noetherian, but not artinian.

 (ii) Consider the submodule Z[1/p]/Z of Q/Z (as a Z-module). It is not noetherian,
      because the ascending chain
                                      1     1
                                     Z ⊂ Z 2 ⊂ ···
                                      p    p
     does not stabilize. But it is artinian, because every descending chain has the form
                                            1     1
                                        Z      ⊃ Z ⊃ ···
                                            n1    n2
     for suitable ni ∈ N satisfying ni+1 |ni , hence stabilizes.

(iii) Modules of finite cardinality are both noetherian and artinian.

Proposition 2.5.7. Let 0 → N → M → P → 0 be a short exact sequence. Then M is
noetherian (resp. artinian) if and only if N and P are both noetherian (resp. artinian).

Proof. We first prove the following claim: let N, M1 , M2 be submodules of M such that

                  M1 ⊂ M2 , N + M1 = N + M2 , N ∩ M1 = N ∩ M2 ,

then M1 = M2 . Let z ∈ M2 . Then z ∈ N + M2 = N + M1 , z = n + m1 . Since M1 ⊂ M2 ,
we also have n ∈ M2 , hence n ∈ N ∩ M2 = N ∩ M1 , i.e. z ∈ M1 .
   Now prove the assertion. Let M1 ⊂ M2 ⊂ · · · be an ascending chain of submodules of
M . Since N is noetherian, the chain

                                 N ∩ M1 ⊂ N ∩ M2 ⊂ · · ·

stabilizes. Since P ∼
                    = M/N is noetherian, the chain (N + M1 )/N ⊂ (N + M2 )/N ⊂ · · ·
stabilizes, hence N + M1 ⊂ N + M2 ⊂ · · · also stabilizes. The claim allows to show that
M1 ⊂ M2 ⊂ · · · stabilizes.

Corollary 2.5.8. Let N, M be submodules of some R-module. If M and N are both
noetherian (resp. artinian), then M + N is noetherian (resp. artinian) as well.

                                              67
Proof. Alternatively, use (M + N )/M ∼  = N/(M ∩ N ), which is a homomorphic image of
N , hence is noetherian (resp. artinian).

Proposition 2.5.9. If R is left noetherian (resp. artinian), then every finitely generated
left R-module is noetherian (resp. artinian).

Proof. This is because M is a quotient of Rn for some n > 0.

Definition 2.5.10. An R-module M is said to be finitely presented if there exists an
exact sequence
                             Rm → Rn → M → 0.

Corollary 2.5.11. If R is left noetherian, then every finitely generated left R-module is
finitely presented.

Remark 2.5.12. We will see later that a left artinian ring is also left noetherian. But
this is wrong, as we already see, for an artinian module.

   We say a left ideal I of R is nil if each element of I is nilpotent.

Proposition 2.5.13. Let R be a left artinian ring, and I ⊂ R be a left ideal. If I is nil,
then it is nilpotent.

Proof. We have a descending chain I ⊃ I 2 ⊃ · · · which must stabilize, say I k = J
whenever k ≫ 0. Suppose that J ̸= 0.
   Since J 2 = J ̸= 0, the set of left ideals of R such that J · a ̸= 0 is nonempty, hence
admits a minimal one a0 . Choose a ∈ a0 such that Ja ̸= 0. Note that Ja is also a left
ideal, and satisfies J · (Ja) = J 2 a = Ja ̸= 0. However, Ja ⊂ Ra ⊂ a0 (since a0 is a left
ideal), we must have Ja = a0 . We can therefore choose y ∈ J such that ya = a, hence
(1 − y)a = 0. But, y is nilpotent, 1 − y is invertible with inverse being i≥0 y i (finite
                                                                             P

sum), so a = 0, contradiction.

Remark 2.5.14. When R is commutative, a nil ideal in a noetherian ring is nilpotent:
write I = (x1 , . . . , xr ).

Proposition 2.5.15. Let R be left noetherian (resp. artinian) ring. Then Mn (R) is also
noetherian (resp. artinian).

Proof. Since Mn (R) is finite free over R, it is a noetherian (resp. artinian) R-module
whenever R is. On the other hand, we have a natural ring morphism R → Mn (R),
sending r to the matrix rIn . This allows to view Mn (R)-modules as R-modules. The
result then follows.




                                            68
2.6    Hilbert basis theorem
We prove the following famous theorem.

Theorem 2.6.1. If R is left noetherian, so is the polynomial ring R[X].

    For a polynomial f = nk=0 ak X k ∈ R[X], with an ̸= 0, we call L(f ) := an the initial
                             P

or leading coefficient of f . For a left ideal a ⊂ R[x] and k ≥ 0, we let

                         Lk (a) := {L(f ) : deg(f ) = k, f ∈ a} ∪ {0}.

Lemma 2.6.2. (1) Lk (a) is a left ideal of R and Lk (a) ⊂ Lk+1 (a) for all k.
   (2) If a′ is another ideal of R such that a ⊂ a′ and Lk (a) = Lk (a′ ), for all k ≥ 0, then
a = a′ .

Proof. (1) If f (X), g(X) ∈ a then f (X) + g(X) and rg(X) (r ∈ R) also in a. The second
inclusion holds because Xf (X) ∈ a.
    (2) Let g(X) ∈ a′ and k = deg g. Since Lk (a) = Lk (a′ ), there exists fk (X) ∈ a such
that g(X) − fk (X) has degree ≤ k − 1. But this new element belongs to a′ since a ⊂ a′ , we
can continue this procedure which terminates after finite steps and shows that g ∈ a.

Proof of Theorem 2.6.1. Let (aj )j≥1 be an ascending chain of left ideals in R[X]. We
consider the family of left ideals of R:

                                    {Li (aj ), i ≥ 0, j ≥ 1}.

Note the following facts:

   • if i is fixed, the chain {Li (aj ), j ∈ N} is ascending;

   • if j is fixed, the chain {Li (aj ), i ∈ N} is also ascending.

We will prove that there exists k ≫ 0 such that Li (aj ) = Li (ak ) for all j ≥ k and all
i ≥ 0. Hence aj = ak for j ≥ k by Lemma 2.6.2.
    Let Lp (aq ) be a maximal element of the family {Li (aj ), i ≥ 0, j ≥ 1}, that is Lp (aq ) =
Li (aj ) if i ≥ p and j ≥ q. For 0 ≤ i ≤ p − 1 fixed, the chain Li (aj ) stabilizes, there exists
q(i) sufficiently large such that Li (aj(i) ) = Li (aj ) for all j ≥ q(i). If we put

                               k := max{q, q(0), . . . , q(p − 1)},

then Li (aj ) = Li (ak ) for all i ≥ 0 and j ≥ k, as desired.

Corollary 2.6.3. If R is left noetherian, so is R[X1 , . . . , Xn ]. More generally, if S is a
finitely generated R-algebra, then S is left noetherian.




                                               69
2.7    Schur’s lemma
Recall that an R-module is simple if M ̸= 0 and M has no submodules other than M and
{0}

Example 2.7.1.      (i) M is simple if and only if M ∼
                                                     = R/a for some maximal left ideal.

 (ii) Over a field, a simple R-module is just a vector space of dimension 1.

(iii) We view M := Rn as a left module over Mn (R). Then submodules of M are of the
      form an for some left ideal a (Exercise). If R = D is a division ring (for example,
      R = F is a field), then M is a simple module.

Lemma 2.7.2 (Schur’s Lemma). Let M1 , M2 be simple R-modules and φ : M1 → M2
be a homomorphism. Then either φ = 0 or φ is an isomorphism. In particular, the
endomorphism ring of a simple R-module is a division ring.

Proof. Easy but important.

Corollary 2.7.3. Let M be a simple module, then EndR (M ⊕n ) ∼
                                                             = Mn (D) where D :=
EndR (M ) is a division ring by Schur’s lemma.

Proof. We construct a morphism EndR (M ⊕n ) → Mn (D): if φ ∈ HomR (M ⊕n , M ⊕n ), we
get an element in EndR (M ) ∼
                            = D:
                                       ι           φ      prj
                                     k
                             αjk : M → M ⊕n → M ⊕n → M,

then we send φ to (αjk )j,k ∈ Mn (D). One checks that the ring structure of EndR (M ⊕n )
corresponds to matrix multiplication in Mn (D). (Left as Exercise.)

  More generally, if M = M1⊕n1 ⊕ · · · ⊕ Mr⊕nr , where M1 , . . . , Mr are distinct simple
R-modules, then
                                 r
                               ∼
                                 Y
                     EndR (M ) =   Mni (Di ), Di := EndR (Mi ).

2.8    The Jordan-Hölder theorem
Let M be a left R-module. A normal series of M is a finite chain of submodules

(12)                       M = M0 ⊇ M1 ⊇ · · · ⊇ Mr = (0).

If Mi ⊋ Mi+1 , we say the normal series has no repetitions. A refinement of a normal
series (12) is a new normal series obtained by inserting additional terms in (12).

Definition 2.8.1. (1) A composition series of M is a normal series such that Mi ⊋ Mi+1
for all i and Mi /Mi+1 is a simple module. The corresponding set (with multiplicities)

                             {M0 /M1 , M1 /M2 , . . . , Mr−1 /Mr }

                                              70
is called the set of composition factors (or Jordan-Hölder factors) and denoted by JH(M ).
    (2) Two composition series are called equivalent if r = s and their composition factors
are isomorphic up to permutation, taking into account of multiplicities.

   Note that, not every module has a composition series. For example, Z (over itself)
has no composition series.

Theorem 2.8.2. (1) (Jordan) If an R-module M has one composition series of length r,
then every composition series of M has the same length r. Moreover, every normal series
without repetitions can be refined to a composition series.
    (2) (Hölder) If an R-module M has a composition series, then any two composition
series are equivalent.

Proof. (1) The theorem is true for r = 1. We proceed by induction on r, assuming the
result holds for modules having a composition series of length ≤ r − 1. Let

(13)                        M = M0 ⊋ M1 ⊋ · · · ⊋ Mr = (0),

be a composition series of length r. Fix another composition series

                            M = N0 ⊋ N1 ⊋ · · · ⊋ Ns = (0);

we need to show s = r.
   By (13), M1 has a composition series of length r − 1. If N1 ⊆ M1 , then we must have
N1 = M1 , as M/N1 is simple by definition while M1 /N1 is a proper submodule of M/N1 .
Therefore r − 1 = s − 1 by the inductive hypothesis, hence r = s. So we may assume
N1 ⊈ M1 . Since M/M1 is simple, we get

                                     M = M1 + N1 .

From the isomorphisms

                          = M1 /M1 ∩ N1 , M/M1 ∼
                     M/N1 ∼                    = N1 /M1 ∩ N1 ,

we see that M1 /(M1 ∩ N1 ) and N1 /(M1 ∩ N1 ) are both simple. Consider the diagram

                            M1
         M = M1 + N1 ⊋         ⊇ M1 ∩ N1 ⊇ M1 ∩ N2 ⊇ · · · ⊇ M1 ∩ Ns = (0).
                            N1

We first observe that the series M1 ∩ N1 ⊇ · · · ⊇ M1 ∩ Ns = (0), although has repetitions,
has all subquotients either 0 or simple modules: this is because

                            M1 ∩ Ni /M1 ∩ Ni+1 ,→ Ni /Ni+1 .

In particular, M1 ∩ N1 admits one composition series (by deleting the repetitions). If
the length is ℓ, then ℓ + 1 = r − 1 (by induction), hence ℓ = r − 2. Hence, N1 has one

                                            71
composition series of length r − 1, and by the inductive hypothesis again, any composition
series of N1 has length r − 1, so s − 1 = r − 1 and s = r.
    (2) We know that any two composition series have the same length:

                           (∗) M = M0 ⊋ M1 ⊋ · · · ⊋ Mr = (0)

                          (∗∗) M = N0 ⊋ N1 ⊋ · · · ⊋ Nr = (0).
We proceed by induction on r. The theorem being trivial if r = 1, we assume r ≥ 2 and
the result holds for modules of length < r. If M1 = N1 , then we have two composition
series of M1 which are equivalent by induction, and the two series of M are also equivalent.
    Assume M1 ̸= N1 , so that M = M1 + N1 . By taking a fixed composition series for
M1 ∩ N1 , we obtain as above two composition series of M :

                 (∗′ ) M = M1 + N1 ⊋ M1 ⊋ M1 ∩ N1 ⊋ · · · ⊋ Mr = (0)

                 (∗∗′ ) M = M1 + N1 ⊋ N1 ⊋ M1 ∩ N1 ⊋ · · · ⊋ Mr = (0).
They are indeed composition series because M1 /M1 ∩ N1 and N1 /M1 /N1 are both simple.
The induction hypothesis implies that the two composition series of M1 ∩N1 are equivalent.
We conclude by noting that the extra terms are just

                   {M/M1 , M1 /(M1 ∩ N1 )} = {M/N1 , N1 /(M1 ∩ N1 )}.

Hence (∗) ∼ (∗′ ) ∼ (∗∗′ ) ∼ (∗∗).
Example 2.8.3. Consider C12 = Z/12Z. It has several composition series:

                                      C1 < C2 < C6 < C12

                                      C1 < C2 < C4 < C12
                                      C1 < C3 < C6 < C12
but they all have the same Jordan-Hölder factors: C2 , C2 , C3 .
   However, note that C4 and C2 × C2 have the same Jordan-Hölder factors, but they are
not isomorphic.
Theorem 2.8.4. An R-module M has a composition series if and only if M satisfies both
chain conditions, i.e. M is noetherian and artinian.
Proof. =⇒ is clear.
   ⇐= Let M0 = M . If M0 ̸= (0), then let M1 be a maximal proper submodule of M
(use ascending chain condition!). If M1 ̸= (0), we similarly define M2 and continue if
M2 ̸= (0). In this way, we get

                                     M = M0 ⊋ M1 ⊋ · · · ⊋ .

The descending chain condition assures the sequence stabilizes, hence Mr = 0 for some
r ≫ 0. We get a composition series of length r since Mi /Mi+1 is simple.

                                               72
Corollary 2.8.5. Consider a short exact sequence 0 → M1 → M → M2 → 0. Then M
has a composition series if and only if both M1 and M2 have one. Moreover, JH(M ) =
JH(M1 ) ∪ JH(M2 ).

Proof. Because the analogous statement hold for noetherian (resp. artinian) modules.

Definition 2.8.6. If M has a composition series of length r, then we define the length of
M to be r:
                                     ℓ(M ) = r.
When M has no composition series, we set ℓ(M ) = ∞.

Proposition 2.8.7. (1) If 0 → M1 → M → M2 → 0 is exact, then ℓ(M ) = ℓ(M1 ) +
ℓ(M2 ).
   (2) If M1 , M2 are two submodules of M , then

                      ℓ(M1 ) + ℓ(M2 ) = ℓ(M1 + M2 ) + ℓ(M1 ∩ M2 ).

Proof. Obvious.

Corollary 2.8.8. Let D be a division ring. Then, up to isomorphism, Dn is the unique
simple Mn (D)-module.

Proof. Write R = Mn (D). By Example 2.7.1(iii), Dn is a simple R-module.
   Let M be a simple R-module. Then M is a quotient of R. We know that R has a
composition series R = ni=1 Dn , with composition factors {Dn }. So M is isomorphic to
                       L

Dn by Jordan-Hölder theorem.


2.9    Semi-simple modules
Definition 2.9.1. A module M is said to be semi-simple if it is a direct sum of simple
modules.

   We view the zero module 0 as a direct sum over ∅, so it is semi-simple.

Remark 2.9.2. A direct product of simple modules is in general not semi-simple. For
example, a simple Z-module has the form Z/pZ for some prime p. Thus, any semi-simple
                                                Q
Z-module is torsion. However, the direct product p prime Z/pZ is not torsion (it contains
Z as a submodule), so it is not semi-simple.

Proposition 2.9.3. The following are equivalent for an R-module M .

 (1) M is semi-simple.

 (2) For every submodule N ⊂ M , there exists another submodule N ′ such that M =
     N ⊕ N ′ . (N ′ is usually called a complement of N .)

 (3) M is equal to the sum of its simple submodules.

                                           73
                                  L
Proof. (1)=⇒(2). Write M =            i∈I Si . We may assume N ⊊ M . Let

                                            M

module by P . For any i ∈ I, P ∩ Si is either 0 or Si because Si is simple. If P ∩ Si = 0,
then N ∩ ( j∈J∪{i} Sj ) = 0, (25) a contradiction to the choice of J. Hence P ∩ Si = Si for
            L

non-zero cyclic submodule of N ′ . Then T contains a maximal proper submodule, say T1 ,
as T is finitely generated. Now T1 has a complement in M , it also has a complement in
T by Lemma 2.9.4 below, say T = T1 ⊕ T2 . Since T2 ∼   = T /T1 (and T1 is a maximal proper
submodule in T ), T2 is simple, thus T2 ⊂ N ∩ T , which is a contradiction since the sum
is direct. This shows that T = 0 and so N = M , as required.

Lemma 2.9.4. If N, N ′ are submodules of M such that M = N ⊕ N ′ , then

                                        P = N ⊕ (N ′ ∩ P )

for any N ⊂ P ⊂ M .

Proof. Let x ∈ P ⊂ M = N ⊕ N ′ , we may write x = y + y ′ with y ∈ N , y ′ ∈ N ′ . Since
y ∈ N ⊂ P , we have y ′ ∈ P , so y ′ ∈ N ′ ∩ P . This proves P = N + (N ′ ∩ P ); this is clearly
a direct sum.

       The proof above in fact gives more:

                                            P
then xi ̸= 0 by assumption, hence xi = x − j xj showing that N ∩ P ̸= 0, contradiction.

                                             L
Remark 2.9.6. Although N is isomorphic to I\J Si (in Corollary 2.9.5), it is not always
true that N is equal to ⊕K Si for suitable K ⊂ I. (As an example, consider the subspace
inside M = F ⊕ F :
                                  N = {(x, x) : x ∈ F },
where F is a field.)

Definition 2.9.7. We say two simple R-modules have the same type if they are isomor-
phic. A semi-simple module is called isotypic if it is a sum of simple modules all of the
same type; also called σ-isotypic if the type is σ.
                                               L
    If M is a semi-simple module, then M = σ Mσ , namely M decomposes as the direct
sum of its σ-isotypic summands for distinct σ. In general, for any R-module M , we define
its socle to be the sum of all its simple submodules. Hence soc(M ) is always a semi-simple
module, but it could be zero; it is nonzero for a module satisfying the descending chain
condition; this is extremely important when studying modular representation theory. It is
easy to see that if M semi-simple and σ-isotypic, then so are its submodules and quotient
modules.
                             L                        Q
Corollary 2.9.8. If M = σ Mσ , then End(M ) = σ End(Mσ ).


2.10     Indecomposable modules
Definition 2.10.1. An R-module M is called decomposable if there exists a decomposition
M = M1 ⊕ M2 with Mi both nonzero; otherwise we say M is indecomposable.
  A module M is called strongly indecomposable if M ̸= 0 and End(M ) is local.

   We recall the definition of a local ring:

Definition 2.10.2. Let A be a (unital) ring. It is called local if the set of non-units of
A is an (two-sided) ideal (hence automatically maximal ideal).

   By a unit in A we mean an element which has both a left inverse and a right inverse
(which must be equal).

Lemma 2.10.3. The following statements are equivalent:

                                               75
     (i) A has a unique maximal left ideal;

 (i’) A has a unique maximal right ideal;

 (ii) A/rad(A) where rad(A) denotes the Jacobson ideal of A;

(iii) A is local.
Proof. By definition, rad(A) is defined to be the intersection of all maximal left ideals in
A. We will prove later on that it is also equal to the intersection of all maximal right
ideals in A, thus rad(A) is a (two-sided) ideal.
    (i) ⇒ (ii) Admitting the above fact, J(A) is the unique maximal left ideal in A. We
claim that this implies A/J(A) is a division ring. More generally, we claim that a ring
which only admits two left ideals, 0 and D iteself, is a division ring. Indeed, if a ∈ D\{0},
then D · a = D (being a nonzero left ideal of D), thus there exists b ∈ D\{0} such that
ba = 1. By the same reason there exists a′ ∈ D\{0} such that a′ = 1. We deduce that b
is a unit with inverse a = a′ , so a is also a unit.
    (i’) ⇒ (ii) Similar.
    (ii) ⇒ (i) + (i’) Clear.
    (i) + (i’) ⇒ (iii) If x ∈ A is a non-unit, then it does not have a left inverse (or a right
inverse), so must be contained in rad(A) which is the unique maximal left ideal (resp.
right ideal). Thus rad(R) is equal to the set of non-units in A and A is local.
    (iii) ⇒ (i) + (i’) Clear.
Definition 2.10.4. Let A be a (unital) ring. An element e ∈ A is called an idempotent
if e2 = e. In this case, 1 − e is also an idempotent since (1 − e)2 = 1 − 2e + e2 = 1 − s,
and we have e(1 − e) = 0.
  If A is local, it contains no idempotents except 0 and 1. Indeed, if e is such that
 2
e = e, then e(1 − e) = 0 so e, 1 − e are non-units; hence e, 1 − e ∈ m, and also 1 ∈ m.
Remark 2.10.5. There exists indecomposable modules but not simple. Usually, two sim-
ple modules can have “extensions”. For example, Z/p2 Z is indecomposable but not simple;
in fact it is a non-split extension of the simple module Z/pZ by itself.
    Another example: Z itself is an indecomposable module of infinite length. Indeed, any
submodule of Z is an ideal, of the form nZ. If Z = nZ + mZ, then (n, m) = 1; but they
always have non-zero intersection, i.e. the sum is not a direct sum.
Proposition 2.10.6. An R-module M is indecomposable if and only if the only idempo-
tents in EndR (M ) are 0 and 1. In particular, strongly indecomposable implies indecom-
posable.
Proof. If M = M1 ⊕ · · · ⊕ Mn where Mj ̸= 0 and n > 1, then the projections ej : M → Mj
is an idempotents and ej ̸= 0, 1. Conversely, suppose EndR (M ) contains an idempotent
e ̸= 0, 1. Then M = eM ⊕ (1 − e)M where eM, (1 − e)M ⊂ M are both nonzero. To
see this, it is enough to check the sum is direct: if ex = (1 − e)y, then ex = e(ex) =
e(1 − e)y = 0.

                                              76
Lemma 2.10.7 (Fitting). Let M be an R-module, which is both noetherian and artinian.
For every f ∈ EndR (M ), there exists a canonical decomposition
                                      M = im(f ∞ ) ⊕ ker(f ∞ )
such that each summand is f -stable, and
   (1) f |im(f ∞ ) is an isomorphism
   (2) f |ker(f ∞ ) is nilpotent.
Proof. Consider the chains
                                 im(f ) ⊃ im(f 2 ) ⊃ im(f 3 ) ⊃ · · · ,
                                ker(f ) ⊂ ker(f 2 ) ⊂ ker(f 3 ) ⊂ · · · .
By our hypothesis, they are stable and we denote their limit by im(f ∞ ) and ker(f ∞ ),
respectively.
     Let us show im(f ∞ ) ∩ ker(f ∞ ) = 0. Take n ≫ 0 so that ker(f n ) = ker(f ∞ ) and
im(f n ) = im(f ∞ ). Write x ∈ im(f n ) as x = f n (yn ) for some yn ∈ M . If x ∈ ker(f n ), then
f n (x) = f 2n (yn ) = 0, so yn ∈ ker(f 2n ) = ker(f ∞ ) = ker(f n ), thus x = 0.
     Next we show M = im(f ∞ )+ker(f ∞ ), which will imply the direct sum decomposition.
Retain the assumption that n ≫ 0 so that everything stabilizes at n. Given x ∈ M , choose
y ∈ M with f n (x) = f 2n (y) (always possible). Then
                                       x = x − f n (y) + f n (y)
from which we get M = im(f ∞ ) + ker(f ∞ ).
Theorem 2.10.8. Let M be an indecomposable module satisfying both chain conditions.
Then any endomorphism of M is either nilpotent or an automorphism. In particular, M
is strongly indecomposable.
Proof. By Fitting’s lemma, we have either M = im(f ∞ ) or M = ker(f ∞ ), hence the first
assertion. Next, we need to show End(M ) is local, i.e. the ideal I of all non-automorphisms
is an ideal; this follows from Lemma 2.10.9 below.
Lemma 2.10.9. If for any x ∈ A, it is either nilpotent or invertible, then A is a local
ring with the maximal ideal consisting of all nilpotent elements.
Proof. Let m = {x ∈ A : x nilpotent}. We need to check that m is a two-sided ideal of
A. Let x ∈ m and r ∈ A arbitrary, to show xr, rx ∈ m, it is equivalent to show they are
not invertible. We may assume x ̸= 0 and, since x is nilpotent, let n ≥ 2 be minimal
such that xn = 0 and xn−1 ̸= 0. Since xn−1 (xr) = 0 and (rx)xn−1 = 0, we get rx, xr are
not invertible. Finally let x, y ∈ m, we need to check x + y ∈ m: if not, x + y =: u is
invertible, then x = u − y is also invertible: [ i≥0 (u−1 y)i ] · u−1 (resp. u−1 · i≥0 (yu−1 )i )
                                                P                                 P

provides the left inverse (resp. right inverse), as u−1 y (resp. yu−1 ) is nilpotent.(26) This
gives a contradiction with x ∈ m.
(26)
    here note the subtlety: since A is not necessarily commutative, that y is nilpotent does not imply
that u−1 y is nilpotent in general. However, we certainly have u−1 y is not invertible, hence nilpotent by
assumption


                                                   77
2.11    Krull-Schmidt theorem
Lemma 2.11.1. Let M, N be R-modules such that M ̸= 0 and N is indecomposable. Let
f : M → N , g : N → M such that gf : M → M is an isomorphism. Then f and g are
both isomorphisms.

Proof. Since gf is an isomorphism, g is surjective and f is injective. We need to show
that f is surjective and g is injective. We first assume gf = IdM . Consider im(f ) and
ker(g), both are submodules of N . It is easy to check that N = imf ⊕ ker(g):

   - if x ∈ im(f ) ∩ ker(g) then x = f (y) and 0 = g(x) = gf (y) = y, hence x = 0.

   - for any x ∈ N , let y = g(x) ∈ M , then x = (x − f (y)) + f (y) ∈ ker(g) + im(f ).

But N is indecomposable, so either ker g = 0 and imf = N , or ker g = N and imf = 0
(but in this case gf = 0, i.e. M = 0). In general case, let h : M → M be the inverse
of f g, then (gf )h = g(f h) = IdM , so that g, f h are both isomorphisms by the argument
above.

Theorem 2.11.2. Let M ̸= 0 be an R-module that is noetherian and artinian. There
exists a decomposition
                        M = M1 ⊕ · · · ⊕ Mr , r ∈ Z≥1
as a direct sum of indecomposable submodules. Moreover, the integer r is unique and the
indecomposable summands Mi are unique up to isomorphism and permutation.

Proof. We do induction on ℓ(M ), which is finite by Theorem 2.8.4. If M is already
indecomposable, we are done. If M = M1 ⊕ M2 with M1 , M2 ̸= 0, then ℓ(M1 ), ℓ(M2 ) <
ℓ(M ), and by induction we have decompositions for M1 , M2 , taking direct sums gives one
for M .
    Suppose M = M1 ⊕ · · · ⊕ Mr and M = N1 ⊕ · · · ⊕ Ns are two decompositions. We shall
argue by induction on max{r, s}. Without loss of generality, we assume s ≥ r. Fixing
M1 , we want to find some 1 ≤ j ≤ s such that M1 ∼ = Nj . For j = 1, . . . , s, set

                      uj : Nj ,→ M ↠ M1 ,         vj : M1 ,→ M ↠ Nj .
                               Ps
Then uj vj ∈ EndR (M1 ), and     j=1 uj vj = IdM1 . In fact, we have

                      s
                      X                           s
                                                  M
                             uj vj : M1 ,→ M →          Nj → M ↠ M1 ,
                       j=1                        j=1


and the composition of the middle two morphisms is just IdM . Since M1 is indecomposable
and satisfies both chain conditions, EndR (M1 ) is a local ring by Theorem 2.10.8. This
implies that at least one of uj vj is an isomorphism: otherwise IdM1 would be also in the
maximal ideal. Therefore by Lemma 2.11.1, uj , vj define isomorphisms M1 ∼  = Nj .


                                             78
    Up to permutation, we may assume j = 1. The next step is to “cancel out” M1 and
                       pr
N1 . Since u1 : N1 → M →1 M1 is an isomorphism, and pr1 ( i≥2 Mi ) = 0, we have
                                                         L

                                 N1 ∩      Mi = 0.
