                                L
It follows easily that M = N1 ⊕( i≥2 Mi ), from which we obtain that M/N1 is isomorphic
         L               L
to both 2≤i≤r Mi and 2≤j≤s Nj , and we can conclude by induction.

Remark 2.11.3. The Krull-Schmidt theorem fails if M is only assumed to be noetherian
or artinian.


2.12     Tensor product of modules
We first recall the notion of bimodules. Let R, S be two rings with unit.

Definition 2.12.1. An (R, S)-bimodule is an additive group which is a left R-module and
a right S-module such that

                             r(ms) = (rm)s, ∀r ∈ R, s ∈ S.

   Homomorphisms between (R, S)-bimodules are just homomorphisms which are simul-
taneously homomorphisms between left R-modules and right S-modules.

Example 2.12.2.        • Every ring R is an (R, R)-bimodule.

   • If R is commutative, then every left R-module is naturally an (R, R)-bimodule:
     rmr′ := rr′ m.

    Convention: R M denotes left R-module, and MS for right S-module, R MS for (R, S)-
bimodule. It is clear that HomR (R MS , R N ) is a left S-module, while HomS (R MS , NS ) is
a right R-module.

Definition 2.12.3. Consider MR , R N and an additive group A. A map

                                     ϕ:M ×N →A

is called a balanced product if it satisfies:
    (i) ϕ(x + x′ , y) = ϕ(x, y) + ϕ(x′ + y)
    (ii) ϕ(x, y + y ′ ) = ϕ(x, y) + ϕ(x, y ′ )
    (iii) ϕ(xr, y) = ϕ(x, ry)
for any x, x′ ∈ M , y, y ′ ∈ N and r ∈ R. The set of balanced products over R from M × N
to A is denoted by Bil(M, N ; A). It forms an additive group.

Remark 2.12.4. (1) Every ring R is an (R, R)-bimodule. So the ring multiplication
(r, r′ ) 7→ rr′ is an R-balanced product R × R → R.
     (2) We have a natural identification Bil(M, N ; A) = HomR (M, HomZ (N, A)), here
HomZ (N, A) is viewed as a right R-module.

                                            79
Definition 2.12.5. The tensor product of M and N over R is an abelian group M ⊗R N
together with a balanced product

                                     ⊗ : M × N → M ⊗R N

which is universal in the following sense: for every abelian group A and every balanced
product ϕ : M × N → A, there is a unique group homomorphism ϕ̃ : M ⊗R N → A such
that
                                            ⊗ /
                                  M ×N          M ⊗R N
                                                             ϕ̃
                                                       & 
                                               ϕ
                                                        A
Remark 2.12.6. We have a natural isomorphism

                      HomZ (M ⊗R N, A) ∼
                                       = HomR (M, HomZ (N, A)).
Proposition 2.12.7. Tensor products of M and N , if they exist, are unique up to a
unique isomorphism.
Proof. It follows from the definition of M ⊗R N : in fact follows from the universal property.
This is a general fact: If T , T ′ are two tensor products, then we let T ′ = A and use the
universal property of T , to get a morphism T → T ′ ; on the other hand let T = A and
use the universal property of T ′ to get T ′ → T . They have to be inverse to each other by
uniqueness: indeed, the uniqueness implies that if α ◦ ⊗ = ⊗, then α = Id.
Theorem 2.12.8. Tensor product M ⊗R N exists.
Proof. Consider the free Z-module F with the set M × N as its basis, i.e F is a free
module with basis e(x,y) for every (x, y) ∈ M × N . We will define M ⊗R N as a quotient
of F . Define I to be the submodule generated by elements

                                (x + x′ , y) − (x, y) − (x′ , y)

                                (x, y + y ′ ) − (x, y) − (x, y ′ )
                                       (xr, y) − (x, ry).
Put M ⊗R N := F/I and ⊗ : M × N → M ⊗R N is given by (x, y) 7→ (image of e(x,y) ).
Then this gives the tensor product: for this we check the universal property. For any
balanced product ϕ : M × N → A, we get a morphism of Z-modules

                               ϕ̃ : F → A, e(x,y) 7→ ϕ((x, y))

then by construction I ⊂ ker ϕ̃, that is, ϕ̃ induces a morphism M ⊗R N → A.
Corollary 2.12.9. Every element of M ⊗R N can be written (non-uniquely) as a finite
sum                        X
                              xi ⊗ yi , xi ∈ M, yi ∈ N,
                                 i

where x ⊗ y denotes the image of (x, y) under ⊗.

                                               80
Example 2.12.10. When R = k is a field, we have the usual notion of tensor products
of k-vector spaces V ⊗k W ; this is again a k-vector space. Moreover, if {vi }, {wj } are
basis of V , W respectively, then {vi ⊗ wj } is a basis of V ⊗ W .

Remark 2.12.11. Attention on M × N (direct sum; abelian group structure) and the
map ⊗ : M × N → M ⊗R N (this map is not a group homomorphism).

Lemma 2.12.12. If M is an (R, S)-bimodule, and N is (S, T )-bimodule, then M ⊗R N
is naturally an (R, T )-bimodule. The module structure is given by

                                   r(x ⊗ y) = (rx) ⊗ y

                                   (x ⊗ y)t = x ⊗ (yt).

Proof. Obvious check.

Lemma 2.12.13. Let φ : R MS → R MS′ and ψ : S NT → S NT′ be homomorphisms. There
is a unique homomorphism of (R, T )-bimodules such that

                 φ ⊗ ψ : M ⊗S N → M ′ ⊗S N ′ , x ⊗ y 7→ φ(x) ⊗ ψ(y).

Such hommorphisms behave well under composition: (φ ⊗ ψ) ◦ (α ⊗ β) = (φα) ⊗ (ψβ).

Proof. First define a balanced product M × N → M ′ ⊗R N ′ as the composition
                                                   ⊗
                           M × N → M ′ × N ′ → M ′ ⊗R N ′ .

Then use the universal property of M ⊗R N .

Lemma 2.12.14. (1) We have a natural isomorphism M ⊗R R ∼         = M . Here we view R
                                                 ∼
as an (R, R)-bimodule. similarly we have R ⊗R M = M .
   (2) We have (M ⊗R M ′ ) ⊗S M ′′ ∼= M ⊗R (M ′ ⊗S M ′′ ), for all MR , R MS′ , S M ′′ .
   (3) When R is commutative, then there are canonical isomorphism M ⊗R N ∼       = N ⊗R M
between R-modules, sending x ⊗ y to y ⊗ x.

Proof. An easy exercise.

                                       = HomR (N, HomS (M, P )).


2.13     Tensor product of algebras
In this part, R is a commutative algebra.

Definition 2.13.1. An (associative) R-algebra is a ring A (with unit) which is equipped
with an R-module structure, such that the multiplication A × A → A is balanced, in
particular:
                          x(ry) = (xr)y = (rx)y = r(xy).
A homomorphism between R-algebras is a ring homomorphism which is also R-linear.

   Equivalently, the R-algebra structure is equivalent to give a ring homomorphism R →
A with image in the center of A.
   Let A and B be two R-algebras. Since A and B are both R-modules, we can form
A ⊗R B the tensor product; this is also an R-module. This can further be made to an
R-algebra: define first the product on elements of the form a ⊗ b by

                              (a ⊗ b)(a′ ⊗ b′ ) := (aa′ ) ⊗ (bb′ )

and extend by linearity to all of A ⊗ B. This makes A ⊗ B to be an R-algebra, with
identity element 1A ⊗ 1B . If A and B are both commutative, so is A ⊗R B.
    In the case A = B, by universal property of tensor product, we get a homomorphism
µ : A ⊗R A → A between R-modules, sending x ⊗ y to xy.

Linear disjointness
Now we consider cases where A, B are both fields. Let F be field, and F a fixed algebraic
closure of F . Let E, K be two extensions of F contained in F . In general E ⊗F K need
not be a field, even need not be a domain. For example, C ⊗R C is not a domain. Let
a = 1 ⊗ i, b = i ⊗ 1, then a2 = b2 = −(1 ⊗ 1), so that (a + b)(a − b) = 0. But a, b are
linearly independent over R.

                                              82
Definition 2.13.2. We say that E and K are linearly disjoint over F if the following
condition is satisfied: whenever x1 , . . . , xm are linearly independent elements of E over
F , they are also linearly independent over K.

Proposition 2.13.3. The following are equivelent:

  (i) E and K are linearly disjoint over F ;

 (ii) the natural map m : E ⊗F K → E.K is injective;

(iii) E ⊗F K is a field.

Proof. (i)⇐⇒(ii) By definition of linear disjointness.
   (ii)=⇒(iii) We claim that m is an isomorphism. The image of m is the subring E[K]
generated by E and K. But, by Proposition 1.2.2 it is a field. Namely, any element in
E[K] is contained in E[S] for some finite subset S of K. So (iii) follows from (ii).
   (iii)=⇒(ii) Trivial.

Remark 2.13.4. If E and K are linearly disjoint over F , then E ∩ K = F . Indeed, take
any x ∈ E ∩ K and consider {1, x} which are linearly dependent over K. Hence they are
also linearly dependent over F , thus x ∈ F .
                       √            √
Example 2.13.5. Q( 3 2) and Q( 3 2i) are not linearly disjoint over Q, although their
intersection gives Q. Otherwise, K ⊗Q L ∼     = K.L would have degree 6 over Q, while
K ⊗Q L has degree 9 over Q.

Example 2.13.6. If E/F is finite Galois, then E and K are linearly disjoint over E ∩K.
Indeed, we may assume E ∩ K = F . By Proposition 1.6.3, we have [EK : K] = [E : F ],
which is equal to [E ⊗F K : K], hence the result.

Galois descent
Let K/F be a finite Galois extension with Galois group G. Let V be a K-vector space.
We say V has a G-action: if G × V → V is a G-action satisfying the extra condition:

                            g(xv) = g(x)g(v), x ∈ K, v ∈ V.

Proposition 2.13.7 (Galois descent). Let V be a K-vector space with a G-action. Then
we have an isomorphism of K-spaces
                                         ∼

                                       → V, x ⊗ v 7→ xv.

Moreover, the morphism is G-equivariant in the sense that:

                              g(x ⊗ v) = (gx) ⊗ v 7→ g(xv).

3        Ring theory
3.1        Semi-simple rings
Just as in module theory, we start by studying semi-simple rings and simple rings.

Definition 3.1.1. A ring is R called left (resp. right) semi-simple if R is semi-simple as
a left (resp. right) R-module.

Lemma 3.1.2. Let R1 , . . . , Rm be left semi-simple rings, then so is R = R1 × · · · × Rm .

Proof. Obvious.

Example 3.1.3. Let D be a division ring, Mn (D) is a left (and right) semi-simple ring:
since
                             Mn (D) = Dn ⊕ · · · ⊕ Dn
where Dn is simple. Moreover, Dn is the unique simple module over Mn (D) (by Jordan-
Hölder theorem).

is of finite length and has composition series.

Corollary 3.1.7. A ring R is left semi-simple if and only if it is right semi-simple.
Proof. Assume R is left semi-simple. Then it has the form (14) in Wedderburn-Artin
theorem, which is clearly right semi-simple.
Proposition 3.1.8. A ring R is semi-simple if and only if every left (or right) R-module
is semi-simple.
Proof. Every left R-module M is a quotient module of a free R-module. Since R is semi-
simple, R(I) is also semi-simple R-module, so is M by Corollary 2.9.5. The converse is
trivial taking M = R.

3.2       Simple rings
Definition 3.2.1. A ring R is called simple if it has no two-sided ideals except {0} and
itself.
Remark 3.2.2. Be attention that a simple ring need not be a simple (left) module over
itself.
       Examples of simple rings are fields, division rings.
Lemma 3.2.3. For any two-sided ideal a of R, the set Mn (a) is a two-sided ideal of
Mn (R). Moreover, a 7→ Mn (a) gives a bijection between the set of two-sided ideals of R
and the set of two-sided ideals of Mn (R).
Proof. It is easy to check that Mn (a) is a two-sided ideal of Mn (R).
   Conversely: if I ⊂ Mn (R) is a two-sided ideal, we show that I = Mn (a) with
                                   a := {x11 ∈ R : ∃(xij ) ∈ I}.
Indeed, given (xij ) ∈ I, for each (i, j), there exists another (yij ) ∈ I (using permutation
matrices) such that y11 = xij , hence xij ∈ a by definition of a.
Corollary 3.2.4. If D is a division ring, then Mn (D) is a simple ring.
       Remark that simple rings need not be semi-simple. Below is an example.
Example 3.2.5. Let V be a F -vector space of countably infinite dimension and R =
EndF (V ). Then R has a unique proper non-zero two-sided ideal I, (29) consisting of those
k-linear maps with finite rank. So R := R/I is a simple ring. Next we show that it
is not semi-simple. Indeed, we already see that R does not satisfy IBN (invariant basis
number), so R/I does not satisfy IBN.(30) However, semi-simple rings satisfy IBN. (If S
is semi-simple, S has a composition series, so ℓ(S) is finite. If S m ∼
                                                                      = S n , then m = n by
Krull-Schmidt theorem.)
(29)
      Proof: let f ∈
                   / I, hence imf is of infinite dimension. Choose W to be a complement of ker f in V .
                                                                              ∼
Then f |W is injective (as W ∩ ker f = 0) and induces an isomorphism f : W −  → imf ; as a consequence
W is of infinite dimension. Since V, W, im(f ) are all of countably infinite dimension, there exist two
bijections g : V → W and h : imf → V (both viewed as an element in R in obvious way), so that the
composition h ◦ f ◦ g : V → V is an isomorphism.

Weyl algebras
Let F be a field of characteristic 0. The polynomial ring F [x] carries the usual F -
                   d

                                 d

the polynomials in F [x], namely
                                                         d

                                                        dx
Note that this is a non-commutative ring; in fact we have
                                      d        d

                                     dx       dx
                           d
Below we will write y for dx , so that

                                A(F ) = F ⟨x, y : yx − xy = 1⟩.

   By induction, it is to check the following relations

(15)                  yxi − xi y = ixi−1 ,   y i x − xy i = iy i−1 ,   ∀i ≥ 1.

For example, by induction yxi−1 − xi−1 y = (i − 1)xi−2 so

   yxi = (xy + 1)xi−1 = xyxi−1 + xi−1 = x(xi−1 y + (i − 1)xi−2 ) + xi−1 = xi y + ixi−1 .

As a consequence, every element in A(F ) can be written in a unique way as a finite sum
             i j                              i j

  i,j≥0 cij x y , cij ∈ F . In other words, {x y , i, j ≥ 0} forms a basis of A(F ) as a vector
space over F .

Proposition 3.2.6. A(F ) is a simple ring.

Proof. Let J be a non-zero two-sided ideal in A(F ) and fix 0 ̸= f ∈ J. Write f =

Remark 3.2.7. It can be shown that A(F ) is a left (and right) noetherian ring. Indeed,
it is equipped with a good filtration such that the associated graded ring is commutative
and noetherian. On the other hand, one can show that A(F ) is not Artinian (Exercise).

                                               88
    The ring A(F ) has very interesting properties and play an important role in represen-
tation theory. Below we give an example.

Proposition 3.2.8. If M is a left A(F )-module such that dimF M < ∞, then M = 0.
                          d
Proof. Now x and y = dx     are two F -linear operators on M . A well-known fact from
linear algebra says that the trace of the commutator of two linear operators on a finite
dimensional vector space is zero. Thus the trace of yx − xy is 0. But we have yx − xy = 1
on M , so Tr(yx − xy) = dimF M , hence M = 0.

Remark 3.2.9. More generally, for n ≥ 1 the Weyl algebra in n variables is defined as
                                                             ∂         ∂
                          An (F ) := F ⟨x1 , . . . , xn ,       ,...,     ⟩.
                                                            ∂x1       ∂xn
An easy induction on n shows that An (F ) is a simple ring.

Double centralizer theorem for simple rings
Let M be a left R-module and R′ := EndR (M ).
    We can view M as a left R′ -module, via (ϕ, m) 7→ ϕ(m). Let R′′ := EndR′ (M ), then
R′′ ,→ E. There is a natural morphism

(16)                                        λ : R → R′′

sending r to [m 7→ rm]. Indeed, one checks that for all f ∈ R′ and m ∈ M ,

                           f · rm = f (rm) = r(f m) = r(f · m)

by definition of the action of f and the assumption that f is a left R-module homomor-
phism.

Example 3.2.10. Let R = Mn (D) where D is a division algebra. Let M = Dn be the
(unique) simple left R-module. Then EndR (M ) ∼
                                              = Dop by the proof of Proposition 3.1.5
(i.e. every element of EndR (M ) has the form ρd : x 7→ xd for some d ∈ D). Now
                                      ∼
view M as a left Dop -module, then R −
                                     → EndDop (M ), and the isomorphism is given by
A 7→ (lA : x 7→ Ax).

Theorem 3.2.11. (Rieffel) Let R be a simple ring and a be a non-zero left ideal of R,
R′ = EndR (a) and R′′ = EndR′ (a). Then the natural map λ : R → R′′ is an isomorphism.

Proof. The kernel of λ is a two-sided ideal, so λ is injective (as R is simple). Since aR is
a two-sided ideal, we have aR = R and so λ(a)λ(R) = λ(R).
   Claim: λ(a) is a left ideal of R′′ .
    Indeed, let a ∈ a and h ∈ R′′ , it suffices to check that h · λ(a) = λ(h(a)). For any
x ∈ a, the LHS is x 7→ h(ax), while the RHS is x 7→ h(a) · x. They are equal, because h
is a morphism of R′ -modules and ρx : a 7→ ax belongs to R′ , i.e. h(ρx · a) = ρx · h(a).

                                                  89
   The claim implies λ(a) = R′′ λ(a), so

                        R′′ = R′′ λ(R) = R′′ λ(a)λ(R) = λ(a)λ(R) = λ(R),

i.e. λ is surjective.

Proposition 3.2.12. Let R be a simple ring. Then R is semi-simple if and only if it is
artinian. If this is the case, then R ∼
                                      = Mn (D) for some division ring D.

Proof. We already see that semi-simple rings are artinian. Now assume R is simple and
artinian. Let a be a minimal (non-zero) left ideal in R, which exists as R is artinian.
In particular, a is simple and D := R′ = EndR (a) is a division ring by Schur’s lemma.
Rieffel’s theorem says that R ∼
                              = EndD (a), so we are left to show dimD (a) = n is finite, so
that
                             R∼= Mn (EndD (D)) = Mn (Dop ).
   Indeed, if dimD (a) were infinite, then R = EndD (a) would contain a proper two-sided
ideal consisting of D-linear endomorphisms of a which are of finite rank. This is impossible
as R is simple.

   Rieffel’stheorem can be stated as a form of the double centralizer property.

Definition 3.2.13. Let E be a ring and R ⊂ E be a subring. The centralizer of R in E
is given by (or ZE (R))

                              CE (R) = {z ∈ E : rz = zr ∀r ∈ R}.

   Clearly CE (CE (R)) ⊇ R; but the equality does not hold in general.
    Now we specialize to the case E = EndZ (M ): the endomorphism ring of the abelian
group M . There is a natural ring morphism R → E induced by the module structure
R × M → M . In general it need not be injective (in fact it is injective if and only if M
is a faithful R-module); let R̄ be its image in E. We also have R′ ,→ E.

Lemma 3.2.14. We have CE (R̄) = R′ and CE (R′ ) = R′′ .

Proof. Almost by definition: let f : M → M be a homomorphism as abelian groups; it
commutes with R̄ if and only if f is an R-module homomorphism. The second statement
is proved similarly.

   An R-module M is said to have the double centralizer property or to be a balanced
module if CE (CE (R)) = R (equivalently R = R′′ ). Thus Rieffel’s theorem says that a
non-zero left ideal a in a simple ring is balanced and faithful.




                                              90
3.3     Jacobson radicals
The study of the Jacobson radical comes from the study of the semi-simplicity. E. Cartan
defined the radical of a Lie algebra g as the largest solvable ideal of g, and used it to
define and classify finite-dimensional semi-simple Lie algebras. In the associative case,
Wedderburn and Artin developed a parallel theory by defining the radical as the largest
nilpotent ideal, but this approach breaks down for general rings because there might not
be a largest nilpotent ideal. After decades of attempts to generalise the notion, Jacobson
resolved this problem by defining the radical as the intersection of all maximal left ideals.

Definition 3.3.1. We define
                                                          \
                                     rad(R) =                           m
                                                 maximal left ideal m

and call it the left Jacobson radical of R. It is also denoted by J(R).

   We define similarly the right Jacobson radical of R. We will see later that they
coincide.

Lemma 3.3.2. Let y ∈ R, the following are equivalent:

 (1) y ∈ rad(R).

 (2) yM = 0 for every simple left R-module M . (That means, the Jacobson radical
     consists of those ”bad” elements which cannot be detected by the action on semi-
     simple modules.)

 (3) 1 − xy has a left inverse for every x ∈ R.

 (3’) 1 − xyz has an inverse for any x, z ∈ R. Here we mean that it has both a left and
      right inverse; note that the left and right inverse must coincide: ax = 1, xb = 1
      implies a = b.(31)

Proof. (1)=⇒(3) If not, Zorn’s lemma shows that 1 − xy is contained in a left maximal
left ideal m, then 1 ∈ m as y ∈ m.
    (3)=⇒(2) If ym ̸= 0 for some m ∈ M , then M = R(ym) (as M is simple), therefore
m = x(ym) for some x ∈ R. But 1 − xy has a left inverse, so m = 0.
    (2)=⇒(1) Every simple module corresponds to a maximal left ideal: M = R/m.
    (3’)=⇒(3) is trivial. Next we combine (2) and (3) to imply (3’). Fix x, z ∈ R. Since
yM = 0 for any simple M , (yz)M = y(zM ) ⊂ yM = 0, so by (3) 1 − x(yz) has a left
inverse, say u. Rather that to show 1 − xyz has a right inverse, we show that u has a left
inverse (hence u is invertible with inverse 1 − xyz).
(31)
    In general, it could happen that x ∈ R has only left inverses, or even has infinitely many left inverses.
For example, in R = EndF (V ) with V = F (N) , choose {en ; n ≥ 0} a basis of V . Let g ∈ R sending en to
en+1 , and for any v ∈ V , let fv ∈ R sending e0 to v and en+1 to en . Then fv ◦ g = IdV . This also implies
that g does not have right inverse; in fact g is not surjective.


                                                     91
       Indeed, the equality u(1 − xyz) = 1 implies that

                                      u = 1 + uxyz = 1 + (ux)yz,

which has a left inverse again by (3) (as seen in last paragraph).
                                             T
Corollary 3.3.3. (i) We have rad(R) = M simple AnnR (M ).

 (ii) rad(R) is a two-sided ideal.

Proof. (i) If y ∈ rad(R), then yM = 0 for any simple left module M , so that y ∈ Ann(M )
for any simple M . The converse is also clear.
    (ii) Note that AnnR (M ) is a two-sided ideal and apply (i).

Corollary 3.3.4. The left radical and right radical coincide.

Proof. The property in Lemma 3.3.2 is symmetric.

Example 3.3.5.          (1) rad(Z) = 0; rad(Z/12Z) = 6Z/12Z.

 (2) If (R, m) is local, then rad(R) = m.

 (3) If R is semi-simple, then rad(R) = 0. Indeed, decompose R as a1 ⊕ · · · ⊕ an , then
                                        L
     the maximal ideals of R are exactly j̸=i aj and their intersection is zero.

 (4) If R → S is surjective, then f (rad(R)) ⊂ rad(S).

 (5) For any ideal a contained in rad(R), rad(R/a) = rad(R)/a.

 (6) If R is a non-commutative ring, rad(R) is not necessarily equal to the intersection
     of all maximal two-sided ideals of R. For example, if V = F (N) and R = EndF (V ),
     then rad(R) = 0, while it has a unique non-zero two-sided ideal I.(32)
                                                                √
(7)* For R = C[x1 , x2 , · · · , xn ]/I, the Jacobson radical is I, the radical of I. (To get
     this, you need Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz.)

Lemma 3.3.6. (Nakayama’s lemma) Let M be a finitely generated non-zero left R-
module. Then rad(R).M ̸= M .
(32)
    Let {ek , k ∈ N} be a basis of V . For each k, let Ik be the set of linear maps with images contained in
Vk := ⊕i̸=k F ei . This is a right ideal of R; we claim that it is maximal. Let f ∈

                                                         pr            f

Proof. Since M is finitely generated and non-zero, it has a maximal proper submodule
M ′ , so that M/M ′ is simple. By Lemma 3.3.2, a(M/M ′ ) = 0 for all a ∈ rad(R), hence
rad(R).M ⊂ M ′ ⊊ M .
     A second proof. Let x1 , . . . , xn be a set of generators of M , with n taken to be
minimal. Then every element in M has the form ni=1 ri xi where ri ∈ R. Since rad(R) is
                                                   P

                                                      P
Assuming rad(R)M = M , we can represent xi as j aij xj , where aij ∈ rad(R). Letting
A = (aij ), we get x = Ax where x = (x1 , . . . , xn )T , i.e. (1 − A)x = 0. Let A∗ be the
adjoint matrix of 1 − A, i.e. A∗ (1 − A) = det(1 − A), then we obtain det(1 − A)x = 0.
Since det(1 − A) is of the form 1 − a with a ∈ rad(R), it is invertible, hence x = 0.

Remark 3.3.7. For Nakayama’s lemma, we will often use the following statements for a
local ring (R, m). (Check that they are equivalent.)

   • If M is a finitely generated non-zero left R-module, then m · M ̸= M ;

   • If M is a finitely generated non-zero left R-module, and x1 , x2 , · · · , xn ∈ M , their
     images in M/mM generate M/mM as R/m-module, then x1 , x2 , · · · , xn generate
     M as R-module;

   • If N is a finitely generated non-zero left R-module, and f : M → N is a left R-
     module homomorphism and f¯: M/mM → N/mN is surjective, then f is surjective.

A geometric picture: for a vector bundle E → X, and a point P ∈ X, then any basis
of the fiber of E over P can be extended to a basis of sections of E|U → U ⊂ X for
some neighborhood P ∈ U ⊂ X. Here, E|U corresponds to M and the fiber of E over P
corresponds to M/mM .


3.4     Nil ideals and Nilpotent ideals
Definition 3.4.1. Let a be a left (or right) ideal of R. We say

   • a is nil, if every x ∈ a is nilpotent, i.e. xn(x) = 0 for some n ∈ N;


                                                 93
       • a is nilpotent, if an = 0 for some n ∈ N.
   Of course, nilpotent ideals are nil. The converse need not be true however. For
example, R = Z[x1 , x2 , · · · ]/(x21 , x32 , · · · ), a = (x1 , x2 , · · · ) is nil, but not nilpotent.
Remark 3.4.2. (i) A finite sum of nilpotent left ideals of R is still nilpotent. (Check
   this!). But for nil ideals, it is an open question. We have(33)
         Köthe’s conjecture: in any ring, the sum of two nil left ideals is nil.

 (ii) Let x be a nilpotent element, Rx need not be a nil ideal. For example, in M2 (F ),
                                                                  
      x := 00 10 is nilpotent, but in Rx, we have 01 00 00 10 = 00 01 , which is not nilpotent.

 (iii) A nil ideal is nilpotent in the following two cases: R is left artinian (see Proposition
       2.5.13), or R is left noetherian. The result in the noetherian case is named Levitzki’s
       theorem. Note that left artinian ring is always left noetherian.(34)
Proposition 3.4.3. If a is a nil left ideal of R, then a ⊂ rad(R).
Proof. Let y ∈ a, need to show 1 − xy is invertible for any x ∈ R. Since xy ∈ a, xy is
nilpotent, hence the inverse of 1 − xy is given by i≥0 (xy)i .
                                                  P

   Remark that this does not mean that any nilpotent element belongs to rad(R), com-
pare Remark 3.4.2(ii). Also, it is not true that any element in rad(R) is nilpotent even if
we assume that there exists a largest nilpotent ideal or R is commutative. For example,
take R = F [[t]], then rad(R) = tF [[t]] is the maximal ideal, but any non-zero element in
rad(R) is not nil.
   However, in the artinian case, we have
Theorem 3.4.4. Let R be a left artinian ring. Then, the largest left (also, right, two-
sided) nilpotent ideal exists and equals rad(R).
Proof. By the former property, we only have to prove that J = rad(R) is nilpotent; the
proof is analogue to Proposition 2.5.13. We have a descending chain J ⊃ J 2 ⊃ · · · , which
stablizes since R is artinian; say J k = I for k ≫ 0. We want to show I = 0. Suppose it
is not the case: I ̸= 0. Again the descending condition gives a minimal element a0 in the
non-empty set
                              {a : left ideal such that Ia ̸= 0}.
Choose a ∈ a0 such that Ia ̸= 0 (of course we get Ra ⊂ a0 hence Ra = a0 , but this is not
enough to conclude). Since I 2 = I by the construction of I, we have
                                       I(Ia) = I 2 a = Ia ̸= 0,
thus Ia = a0 by minimality.
   In sum, there exists y ∈ I ⊂ J such that a = ya, namely (1 − y)a = 0. However
1 − y ∈ R× since y ∈ J, thus a = 0, a contradiction.
(33)
      A very important conjecture in non-commutative ring theory, posed in 1930’s; see Agata Smoktunow-
icz’s ICM talk (2006).
 (34)
      See Wiki’s term on “nil ideal” or “Levitzki’s theorem”.


                                                  94
Remark 3.4.5. The above theorem explains that why the Jacobson radical is a generalisa-
tion of Wedderburn’s (resp. Artin’s) radical for the finite-dimensional (one-sided artinian)
associative ring to a general setting.
Remark 3.4.6. When R is commutative, the nilradical of R is defined as the intersection
                             T
of all prime ideals of R:     p prime p, which equals the set of nilpotent elements of R.
According to the properties above, it is contained in rad(R) and equals rad(R) if we assume
R is artinian.


3.5    Semi-primitive and semi-primary rings
The notion of Jacobson radical leads to a slight generalisation of semi-simple rings.
Definition 3.5.1. A ring is called semi-primitive if rad(R) = 0. (This property is sym-
metric for left and right.) Sometimes, it is also called Jacobson-semisimple.
   By definition, if a is a two-sided ideal contained in rad(R), then rad(R/a) = rad(R)/a.
As a consequence, R/rad(R) is semi-primitive.
   As examples, semi-simple rings are semi-primitive. Also, EndF (V ) with V = F (N) is
semi-primitive (but not semi-simple by Example 3.2.5).
Theorem 3.5.2. The following are equivalent:
  (i) R is semi-simple.

 (ii) R is semi-primitive and left artinian.
   Remark that since R is not known to be noetherian (although this is indeed the case),
we don’t a priori have a composition series of R.

Proof. (i)=⇒(ii) It follows from Proposition 3.1.4.
   (ii)=⇒(i) Assume R is semi-primitive and left artinian. We first note the following
two facts.
 (a) Since R is left artinian, every non-zero left ideal contains a minimal left ideal. Note
     that it must be principal and simple as a left R-module.

 (b) Every minimal left ideal I is a direct summand of R. In fact, since ∩m m = rad(R) =
     0, there exists a maximal left ideal m which does not contain I. Hence R = m + I;
     but m ∩ I = 0 since I is minimal. We deduce that R = I ⊕ m.
Now we prove that R is a semi-simple ring. Take a minimal left ideal a1 ⊂ R; if a1 = R,
then we are done. If not, write R = a1 ⊕ b1 . Since b1 ̸= 0, it contains a minimal left ideal
a2 ; if a2 = b1 , then we are done. Recall that a2 has a complementary in R, so it has a
complementary in any submodule of R which contains a2 , see Lemma 2.9.4. In particular,
if a2 ̸= b1 , then b1 = a2 ⊕ b2 for some b2 ̸= 0. Continuing in this way we get

(17)                             R := b0 ⊋ b1 ⊋ b2 ⊃ · · ·

                                               95
and minimal left ideals a1 , a2 , . . . , such that
                                                ai ⊕ bi = bi−1 .
But R is artinian, so the chain (17) is finite and we get a finite direct sum decomposition
    L
R = 1≤i≤n ai . Since each ai is a simple R-module, R is semi-simple.
Remark 3.5.3. R and R/rad(R) have the same simple left modules. Also, a ring is
semi-primitive if and only if it has a faithful semi-simple module. (check these).
Definition 3.5.4. A ring is called semi-primary if rad(R) is a nilpotent ideal and R/rad(R)
is semi-simple.
    If R is semi-primary, then every left or right nil ideal is nilpotent, since it is contained
in rad(R). So rad(R) is the largest nilpotent left ideal; it is also the largest nilpotent right
ideal.
Proposition 3.5.5. Left artinian rings are semi-primary.
Proof. Denote J = rad(R) in the proof. According to Theorem 3.4.4, it is enough to
prove that R/J is semi-simple. But rad(R/J) = J/J = 0, so that R/J is semi-primitive.
Moreover, R/J is artinian since R is, therefore R/J is semi-simple by Theorem 3.5.2.
Theorem 3.5.6 (Hopkins-Levitzki). Let R ba semi-primary ring and M be a left module.
The following statements are equivalent:
  (i) M is left noetherian;

 (ii) M is left artinian;

 (iii) M has a composition series, or equivalently, M has finite length.
Proof. It is obvious that (iii) implies (i) and (ii). We show that if M is either noetherian
or artinian, then M has a composition series. Let J = rad(R) be the Jacobson radical of
R. Consider the series
                               M ⊃ · · · ⊃ J i M ⊃ J i+1 M ⊃ · · ·
This is finite since J n = 0 for n ≫ 0. Study Fi := J i−1 M/J i M , each may be viewed as an
R/J-module. Since R/J is a semi-simple ring, each Fi is a semi-simple R/J-module. If
M is artinian (or noetherian), then Fi has finite length, since it is a direct sum of simple
modules. The additivity of ℓ(·) shows that M has finite length.
Corollary 3.5.7. A left artinian ring is automatically left noetherian.
Proof. Combine Proposition 3.5.5 and Theorem 3.5.6.

   In summary:
                      (artinian)
             simple            +3 semi-simple           +3 left artinian   +3 semi-primary
                                        KS
                             (artinian)
                                                               
                             semi-primitive            left noetherian

                                                      96
3.6    Density theorem for semi-simple modules
Let M be a left R-module. Let R′ = EndR (M ). We can also view M as a left R′ -module:

                              R′ × M → M ; (f, m) 7→ f (m).

Recall that the multiplication in R′ is defined by f · g = f ◦ g.
   We have seen that there is a natural ring homomorphism in (16)

                                 λ : R → R′′ := EndR′ (M ).

We want to know how large the image of λ is. The density theorem, due to Jacobson, says
that any element in EndR′ (M ) can be approximated by an element in the image when M
is semi-simple.

Example 3.6.1. Let’s consider a simple case: R = F is a field, M = F n . Then R′ =
Mn (F ) acts on F n . We have the morphism F → EndR′ (F n ); it is surjective. Indeed, F n
is a simple R′ -module generated by e := (1, 0, . . . , 0)T : any x is of the form Ae, where
A = (x|0 · · · 0) (the matrix whose first column is x and the other columns are 0). So for
φ ∈ EndR′ (F n ),
                                   φ(x) = A · φ(e) = ax
where a is the first coefficient of φ(e). In particular, taking x = e, we see that φ(e) =
ae = (a, 0, . . . , 0)T .

Definition 3.6.2. We say that R → R′′ := EndR′ (M ) has a dense image, or R acts
densely on R′ M , if for every φ ∈ R′′ , any x1 , . . . , xn ∈ M , there exists r ∈ R such that

                                rxi = φ(xi ), ∀i = 1, . . . , n.

Remark 3.6.3. If we equip M with the discrete topology and R′′ with the coarsest topology
such that for every m ∈ M , the morphism f 7→ f (m) ∈ M is continuous. Then Definition
3.6.2 means that the image of R is a dense subset of R′′ .

Theorem 3.6.4 (Jacobson density theorem). Suppose M is a semi-simple left R-module,
and let R′ = EndR (M ). Then R → R′′ = EndR′ (M ) has dense image. Furthermore, if M
is finitely generated over R′ , then the homomorphism R → EndR′ (M ) is surjective.

   We first prove a lemma, which is just the special case of the theorem when n = 1.

Lemma 3.6.5. Let M , R′ be as above. Then for any φ ∈ R′′ and any x1 ∈ M , there
exists r ∈ R such that rx1 = φ(x1 ).

Proof. We need check φ(x1 ) ∈ Rx1 . For this we may assume x1 ̸= 0. If M is simple, this
is trivial. Since M is semi-simple, we can write

                                       M = Rx1 ⊕ M ′

                                              97
for some submodule M ′ (since every submodule is a direct summand). If pr : M → Rx1
denotes the projection, then pr ∈ R′ , so

                                 φ(x1 ) = φ(pr(x1 )) = pr(φ(x1 )).

But {y ∈ M : pr(y) = y} is just Rx1 , thus φ(x1 ) ∈ Rx1 as desired.

Lemma 3.6.6. Given n > 1 and φ ∈ EndR′ (M ), we have φ⊕n ∈ EndRn′ (M ⊕n ), where
Rn′ = EndR (M ⊕n ).

Proof. We know that Rn′ = Mn (R′ ). Given g ∈ Rn′ , we represent g by (gi,j )1≤i,j≤n , with
gi,j ∈ R′ . Given (m1 , . . . , mn ) ∈ M ⊕n , since φ ∈ EndR′ (M ), we have (the check is as in
n = 1 case)

            φ⊕n g(m1 , . . . , mn ) =      φ⊕n ( nj=1 g1,j (mj ), . . . , nj=1 gn,j (mj ))
                                                P                        P

                                           ( nj=1 φg1,j (mj ), . . . , nj=1 φgn,j (mj ))
                                            P                         P
                                    =
                                           ( nj=1 g1,j (φ(mj )), . . . , nj=1 gn,j (φ(mj )))
                                            P                            P
                                    =
                                    =      g(φ(m1 ), . . . , φ(mn ))
                                    =      gφ⊕n (m1 , . . . , mn ).



Proof of Theorem 3.6.4. We want to find some r ∈ R such that φ(xi ) = rxi , i.e.

                              (φ(x1 ), . . . , φ(xn )) = (rx1 , . . . , rxn ).

The trick is to apply Lemma 3.6.5 to the induced endomorphism

                                         φ⊕n : M ⊕n → M ⊕n .

Indeed, by Lemma 3.6.6 we have that φ⊕n is a homomorphism of EndR (M ⊕n )-modules.
Then we can apply Lemma 3.6.5 to φ⊕n and the element (x1 , . . . , xn ) ∈ M ⊕n , and obtain
an r ∈ R such that

                    (rx1 , . . . , rxn ) = r(x1 , . . . , xn ) = (φ(x1 ), . . . , φ(xn )),

giving the desired result.
    The surjectivity assertion is clear under the assumption, since in this case φ is deter-
mined by its values on a finite generating set for M (over R′ ).

Example 3.6.7. Let F be a field and V = F(N) . Let I be the set of all finite rank F -linear
morphisms on V . Let R be the subring of EndF (V ) generated by I and the identity 1. One
can show that: (i) V is a simple R-module; (ii) F = EndR (V ). However, the morphism
R → R′′ = EndF (V ) is just the natural inclusion, which is not surjective.

   Let us now discuss the condition under which M is a finitely generated R′ -module.


                                                     98
Proposition 3.6.8. Assume M is a semi-simple left R-module. Then M is also semi-
simple as a left R′ -module. If moreover R is left artinian, then M is finitely generated
over R′ .

Proof. (i) We claim that: if x ∈ M generates a simple left R-submodule, then R′ x is a
simple R′ -module. Let a′ ∈ R′ and a′ x ̸= 0, we need to show that a′ x generates R′ x, i.e.
x = b′ a′ x for some b′ ∈ R′ . Define a morphism ℓ : Rx → M by sending u to a′ u; this is
an R-module homomorphism since a′ ∈ R′ = EndR (M ). Since Rx is simple, ℓ induces an
                       ∼
isomorphism ℓ : Rx −   → Ra′ x. Take a decomposition of R-modules M = Ra′ x ⊕ N , and
consider
                                      pr     ℓ−1
                                   M → Ra′ x → Rx ,→ M.
This is an R-module homomorphism sending a′ x to x, in other words, it defines an element
b′ ∈ R′ such that b′ a′ x = x.
    The claim implies the result, because M being semi-simple, thus decomposes as ⊕i∈I Ni
                                                                                     P
where Ni are simple. Hence every element x ∈ M can be uniquely written as x = i∈I xi ,
with xi = 0 for almost all i. If xi ̸= 0, then Rxi = Ni (as Ni is simple), so R′ xi is simple
by the claim. Thus x ∈ i∈I R′ xi , a finite sum of simple R′ -submodules of M . This
                             P

shows that, as an R′ -module, M is the sum of its simple submodules, hence is semi-simple
by Proposition 2.9.3.
    (ii) Consider the following set

                 S = {a left ideal of R : a = AnnR (X), finite X ⊂ M }.

Since R is artinian, S contains a minimal element a and let X be the finite set associated
to a. Note that a could be zero, i.e. AnnR (X) = 0 for some finite X. The minimality
implies that, for any y ∈ M ,

                           AnnR (X ∪ {y}) = a ∩ AnnR (y) = a

i.e. a ⊂ AnnR (y). In other words, if r ∈ R annihilates X, then r annihilates M .
     We claim that M = R′ X, i.e. generated by X as an R′ -module. If not, since M is a
semi-simple R′ -module by (i), it decomposes as M = R′ X ⊕ N . Let

                                    pr : M ↠ N ,→ M

denote the projection to N . Then it is an R′ -endomorphism of M , i.e. pr ∈ R′′ . By
Jacobson density, for X ∪ {y} (here y ∈ N is non-zero) there exists r ∈ R such that
pr = r on X ∪ {y}. In particular, rX = 0, which implies r ∈ a; but pr(y) = y, hence
ry = y ̸= 0; this contradicts what we have remarked: a ⊂ AnnR (y).

    As a consequence of Jacobson density theorem, we prove the following double central-
izer theorem for semi-simple modules. As in §3.2 let E := EndZ (M ) and R̄ be the image
of R → E. (This map is injective if and only if M is a faithful R-module.)


                                             99
Theorem 3.6.9. Assume R is left artinian and let M be a semi-simple left R-module.
Then
                               CE (CE (R̄)) = R̄.

       The theorem says that semi-simple modules over a (left) artinian ring are balanced.

Proof. By Proposition 3.6.8, M ′ is a finitely generated semi-simple R′ -module. Then we
apply Jacobson Density theorem.

Burnside’s theorem
We present now some applications of Jaconson density theorem to representation theory.

(iii) Let V1 , . . . , Vr be a set of pairwise non-isomorphic simple R-modules. Then the map
                                      r

Proof. (i) Since F lies in the center of R, D := EndR (V ) (which is a division algebra) is

first assertion follows from Lemma 3.6.11 below and the second follows from Jacobson
density theorem.
    (ii) Let n := dimF V and choose e1 , . . . , en a basis of V over F , say the canonical one.

                        EndF (V ) → V ⊕ · · · ⊕ V, A 7→ (Ae1 , . . . , Aen ).

It is a morphism of R-modules, and is a bijection by identifying EndF (V ) with Mn (F )

                                                 100
Lemma 3.6.11. Let D be a finite dimensional division F -algebra and F be algebraically
closed. Then D = F .

Proof. Let x ∈ D and consider the subring F [x] ⊂ D. This is a (commutative) domain
as D is a division ring. Since dimF F [x] ≤ dimF D < ∞, the natural surjective ring
homomorphism F [X] → F [x] is not an isomorphism. Its kernel is a non-zero prime
(hence maximal) ideal, so F [x] is a finite field extension of F . But F is algebraically
closed, so F [x] = F and x ∈ F .

Example 3.6.12. If R = Mn (F ), it has only one simple module F n ; the relation dimF R =
(dim V )2 holds.


3.7    Primitive rings
Recall that a ring is semiprimitive if and only if its Jacobson radical is zero. Also recall
that a left R-module M is called faithful if AnnR (M ) = 0. This is equivalent to the
injectivity of the natural map R → EndZ (M ) (endomorphism ring as an abelian group).
As a consequence, if M is faithful, the following morphism is also injective:

                         R → R′′ = EndR′ (M ), R′ := EndR (M ).

Proposition 3.7.1. A ring R is semi-primitive if and only if there exists a faithful semi-
simple left R-module.

Proof. Let M be a faithful semi-simple R-module, then AnnR (M ) = 0; but we have
rad(R) ⊂ AnnR (M ), hence rad(R) = 0. Conversely, assume rad(R) = 0 and set
                                         M
                                   M=          N
                                            N simple


where the sum runs over all simple R-modules N . Then M is semi-simple and
                                    \
                       AnnR (M ) =    AnnR (N ) = rad(R) = 0,
                                       N

where the middle equality follows from Lemma 3.3.2. Hence M is faithful.

   Proposition 3.7.1 gives another characterization of semi-primitive rings and motivates
the following definition.

Definition 3.7.2. A ring R is called left (resp. right) primitive if there exists a faithful
simple left (resp. right) R-module.

   Obviously left (or right) primitive rings are semi-primitive.

Remark 3.7.3. The notion of primitivity is not left-right symmetric (although semi-
primitivity is).

                                            101
Lemma 3.7.4. A simple ring R is left (resp. right) primitive.
Proof. By taking a maximal left ideal of R, we obtain a simple left R-module, say M . Since
AnnR (M ) is a two-sided ideal in R and since R is simple, we must have AnnR (M ) = 0.
Example 3.7.5.       (i) For any division ring D, Mn (D) is simple, hence is primitive.

 (ii) Let V = F (N) , then EndF (V ) is a primitive ring because V becomes a faithful simple
      EndF (V )-module. Moreover, the subring defined in Example 3.6.7 is also primitive.
      However, EndR (V ) is not a simple ring because it contains a non-zero proper two-
      sided ideal (cf. Example 3.2.5).
Proposition 3.7.6. Let R be a left artinian ring. Then
  (i) R is semi-simple if and only if R is semi-primitive;

 (ii) R is simple if and only if R is left primitive.
Proof. (i) is proved in Theorem 3.5.2.
    (ii) Assume R is left primitive. Then R is semiprimitive since rad(R) = 0. By (i) it
follows that R is semi-simple. Now use the Wedderburn-Artin theorem to get
                              R∼
                               = Mn1 (D1 ) × · · · × Mnr (Dr )
and that the simple R-modules are Dini . Need to show r = 1. If r ≥ 2, then R can not
act faithfully on any simple module, so R can not be left primitive.
Theorem 3.7.7. Let R be a left primitive ring, V be a faithful simple left R-module. Set
D = EndR (V ), which is a division ring by Schur’s lemma. Then λ : R → EndD (V ) has
dense image. Moreover,
    (i) if R is left artinian, then n := dimD (V ) is finite and R ∼
                                                                   = Mn (D).
    (ii) if R is not left artinian, then dimD (V ) is infinite and for every n ∈ Z≥1 there
exists a subring Rn of R together with a surjective ring homomrophism Rn ↠ Mn (D).
Proof. Note that λ is injective by the faithfulness of V . The density of λ(R) follows from
Jacobson’s theorem.
   • Assume that n := dimD V is finite. Then λ is surjective and thus R ∼
                                                                        = Mn (D), and
     is a simple ring. In particular R is left artinian.

   • Assume that dimD (V ) is infinite. There exists a family {vi }i∈Z≥1 of D-linearly
     independent vectors in V . For every n, let Vn be the linear span of {v1 , . . . , vn } and
     set
                                 Rn := {r ∈ R : r(Vn ) ⊂ Vn }
                                    an := {r ∈ R : r(Vn ) = 0}.
     Then Rn is a subring of R and an is a two-sided ideal of Rn . We have the natural ring
     homomorphism λn : Rn /an ,→ EndD (Vn ). Furthermore, Jacobson density theorem
     implies that λn is actually surjective, hence Rn ↠ Rn /an ∼  = Mn (D).
     Note that an is a left ideal of R and an+1 ⊂ an for all n, thus R is not left artinian.

                                             102
The above shows that dimD (V ) < ∞ if and only if R is left artinian. The finishes the
proof.

3.8       Central simple algebras
From now on let F be a field and consider F -algebras.
Definition 3.8.1. Let A be an F -algebra, the center of A is denoted by Z(A)(37) , i.e.
Z(A) = CA (A). We say that A is central if Z(A) = F .
Definition 3.8.2. Let A be an F -algebra. We say that A is central simple if
  • A is a simple ring,
  • A is a central F -algebra.
Remark 3.8.3. If A is a simple ring, Z(A) is always a field. Indeed, let a ∈ Z(A) be
non-zero, then Aa = aA is a two-sided ideal of A, hence equals to A, i.e. a is invertible.
So Z(A) is a commutative division algebra, namely a field.
    We will only consider central simple F -algebras which are finite dimensional over F
(hence are artinian). By Proposition 3.2.12 they are of the form Mn (D), where D is a
central division F -algebra with Z(D) = F . Recall (by Wedderburn-Artin theorem) that
D and n can be recovered by the decomposition A A = M ⊕n where M is the unique simple
left A-module, and D ∼  = EndA (M ). Thus we are reduced to studying finite dimensional
central division F -algebras.
Example 3.8.4. (i) If F = C, then there is no non-trivial central division algebra by
Lemma 3.6.11.
   (ii) Over R, except R itself, we have Hamilton’s quaternion algebra: H = R ⊕ Ri ⊕
Rj ⊕ Rk, with the identities
                                    i2 = j 2 = k 2 = ijk = −1,
which determine all the possible products of i, j, k. This is the unique non-trivial one by
a theorem of Frobenius.
    (iii) Over a finite field F, a theorem of Wedderburn says that any central division
algebra is a field, hence equals to F itself.
    (iv) Let E/F be a finite cyclic extension of degree n, and let Gal(E/F ) = ⟨s⟩. Let
A = A(E, s, γ) be the subalgebra of Mn (E) generated by all of the diagonal matrices
                                  diag(b, sb, . . . , sn−1 b), b ∈ E
and the matrix                                              
                                         0        1    0 ···
                                        0        0    1 ··· 
                                                            
                                     z=
                                        0        0    0 1 
                                                             
                                         γ        0    0 0
(37)
       For the German “Zentrum”


                                                 103
where γ is a non-zero element of F . Note that b 7→ diag(b, sb, . . . , sn−1 b) allows us to
embed E into A. We check that

                                     zb = s(b)z, z n = γ

and every element of A can be written uniquely as the form

                                 b0 + b1 z + · · · + bn−1 z n−1

where the bi ∈ E. One checks that A is central simple and dimF A = n2 ; moreover,
A∼= Mn (F ) if and only if γ is the norm of an element of E.

   Let A, B be two F -algebras. Then A ⊗F B is also an F -algebra. Moreover,

                                A → A ⊗F B, a 7→ a ⊗ 1

is an injective ring homomorphism so we can view A as a subalgebra of A ⊗F B. Similarly
for B ,→ A ⊗F B. Moreover, inside A ⊗F B, we have AB = BA (commutativity). If we
assume both A, B finite dimensional over F , then dimF A ⊗F B = dimF A · dimF B.

Proposition 3.8.5. If A, B are subalgebras of an F -algebra S, then S ∼
                                                                      = A ⊗F B if and
only if

 (1) ab = ba for all a ∈ A, b ∈ B;

 (2) there exists a basis {aα } of A/F such that every element of S can be written in a
                     P
     unique way as α aα bα , where bα ∈ B are almost all 0.

If S is finite dimensional over F , then condition (2) can be replaced by

(2’) S = AB and dimF S = dimF A · dimF B.

Proof. We construct an algebra homomorphism A ⊗F B → S given by a ⊗ b 7→ ab; this
is well-defined thanks to (1). Condition (2) means that this is bijective. If S is finite
dimensional, then (2’) implies it is bijective.

Lemma 3.8.6. (i) For any F -algebra B, there is an isomorphism of F -algebras

                                 Mn (F ) ⊗F B ∼
                                              = Mn (B).

   (ii) For any division F -algebra D, we have as F -algebras:

                             Mn (F ) ⊗F Mm (D) ∼
                                               = Mnm (D).

Proof. (i) We identify b ∈ B with diag(b, . . . , b) ∈ Mn (B) and write A = Mn (F ). Then
AB = BA. To check condition (2), we let Eij ∈ A be the canonical basis over F (only
(i, j)-entry is non-zero and equals 1). It is clear that that any matrix (bij ) ∈ Mn (B) can
                          P
be written uniquely as i,j bij Eij .
     (ii) Left as an exercise.

                                             104
   If A is an F -algebra, so is Aop .

Definition 3.8.7. Let Ae := A ⊗F Aop , and call it the enveloping algebra of A.

    If A is a subalgebra of some F -algebra B, then we can view B as an Ae -module as
follows:
                                   (a ⊗ a′ ) · b := aba′ .
This is well-defined as B is an F -algebra. In particular, A itself is an Ae -module. We
have:

   • Ae -submodules of A as Ae -module are just two-sided ideals of A. Thus, if A is
     simple ring, then A is a simple Ae -module.

   • EndAe (A) = Z(A). Indeed, let f : A → A be an endomorphism of Ae -modules.
     Then as left A-module, f has the form x 7→ xd for some d ∈ A; as left Aop -module
     (i.e. right A-module), f has the form x 7→ cx for some c ∈ A. Taking x = 1, we
     have c = f (1) = d and cx = xc for any x ∈ A. Therefore c ∈ Z(A). In particular, if
     A is central, then EndAe (A) = F .
     More generally, if A is a subalgebra of some F -algebra B, then

                                        HomAe (A, B) ∼
                                                     = CB (A).

Lemma 3.8.8. If A is a finite dimensional central F -algebra, then A is simple if and
only if the homomorphism

                            λ : Ae = A ⊗F Aop → EndF (A)
                                        a ⊗ b 7→ [x 7→ axb].

is an isomorphism. If this holds, we have Ae ∼
                                             = Mn (F ) where n := dimF A.

Proof. We view A as an Ae -module as above. Assume first A is a simple ring. Then A
is simple as Ae -module and EndAe (A) = F since A is central. Apply Jacobson density
theorem to R = Ae and M = A, R′ = F , we get a surjection λ : Ae ↠ EndF (A)
since A is artinian. Comparing dimensions, it is in fact an isomorphism. We also have
EndF (A) ∼= Mn (F ) if n = dimF A.
    Conversely, assume A is not simple and let I ⊂ A be a proper non-zero two-sided
ideal. The image of λ must send I to I, hence λ can not be surjective.

   Let A be a finite dimensional central simple F -algebra, and C be any F -algebra. Let
B := A ⊗F C.

Proposition 3.8.9. We have CB (A) = C.

                             X              X

                                  α                   α

hence equivalent to aaα = aα a for any a ∈ A. But A is central, meaning that aα ∈ F , so
that b ∈ C.

   Note that we only use the fact that A is central. We have the converse of the above
result.
Theorem 3.8.10. Let B be an F -algebra, not necessarily of finite dimensional over F ,
and A be a finite dimensional F -subalgebra which is central simple. Set C := CB (A), then
  (i) the natural homomorphism A ⊗F C → B given by a ⊗ c 7→ ac between F -algebras is

             {two-sided ideals of C} → {two-sided ideals of B}, I 7→ AI = A ⊗F I

      whose inverse is given by I = AI ∩ C.

(iii) Z(B) = Z(C).

Proof of Theorem 3.8.10. (i) Since HomAe (A, B) = CB (A) = C, the result follows from
Lemma 3.8.11 with M = B.
   (ii) Let I be a two-sided ideal in C, then AI is a two-sided ideal in B = AC. Claim:
AI ∩ C = I. We fix a basis {a1 = 1, a2 , . . . , an } of A/F , then any element b ∈ B can be
written uniquely as ni=1 ai ci , with ci ∈ C (don’t confuse with cα above). Then b ∈ AI if
                     P

and only if ci ∈ I, and b ∈ C if and only if, by unicity, c1 = b and ci = 0 for 2 ≤ i ≤ n.
This shows b ∈ I, and hence the association I 7→ AI is injective.
   Now let I ′ be a two-sided ideal of B, we need to show I ′ = AI for some two-sided

of B as Ae -module, we have an isomorphism by Lemma 3.8.11

                                   A ⊗F HomAe (A, I ′ ) = I ′ .

The inclusion I ′ ⊂ B allows us to identify I := HomAe (A, I ′ ) with a subspace of C, and
one checks that I is a two-sided ideal of C.
    (iii) We must have Z(B) ⊂ C as CB (A) = C. This implies Z(B) ⊂ Z(C). Conversely,
if c ∈ Z(C), c also commutes with A, so it commutes with the whole B = AC, so
c ∈ Z(B).

Corollary 3.8.12. Let A be a finite dimensional central simple algebra. For every F -
algebra C, the F -algebra A ⊗F C is simple if and only if C is; A ⊗F C is central (over
Z(C)) if and only if C is central.

Proof. Theorem 3.8.10(ii) implies the equivalence for simplicity, and (iii) implies the equiv-
alence for centrality.

   In particular, we get the following consequence when C = K is a field extension of F .

Corollary 3.8.13. Let A be a finite dimensional F -algebra and K/F be a field extension,
then A is central simple over F if and only if AK := A ⊗F K is central simple over K.

Example 3.8.14. We have H ⊗R C is still a CSA over C, so is of the form Mn (D) for
some division algebra D over C. We already see that D = C, and looking at dimensions,
we get n = 2, i.e.
                                 H ⊗R C ∼= M2 (C).
(Find an explicit isomorphism!)

Corollary 3.8.15. If A1 , . . . , An are all finite dimensional central simple F -algebra, so
is A1 ⊗F · · · ⊗F An .

   Although the above proof relies on the finiteness of Ai , however, the statememt is also
true for infinite dimensional case.

Proposition 3.8.16. If A1 , . . . , An are all central simple F -algebra, so is A1 ⊗F · · ·⊗F An .


                                               107
Proof. The centrality is easy. Let us show that if A, B are central simple F -algebras, then
A ⊗F B is still simple.
   Choose a F -basis {b1 , b2 , · · · } of B, and assume that I is a two-sided ideal of the tensor
                                                                                        P
product. Then, we choose a non-zero element u, such it can be a finite sum                 ai ⊗ bi ,
ai ∈ A and the number of non-zero terms is minimal. Without loss of generality, we
assume a1 ̸= 0.
   Since Aa1 A = A, one can even choose a such element u in the ideal, such that the first
term a1 = 1.
   Thus, take any element x ∈ A and consider the commutator of u and x, then, [u, x] ∈ I
and it has less number of non-zero terms. Thus, [u, x] = 0. The central property of A
implies that x ∈ B. By assumption, B is simple, so x generates the whole B. Now, the
proposition follows by 1 ⊗ B generates A ⊗F B as a two sided ideal.

Splitting fields
Definition 3.8.17. The matrix algebra Mn (F ) is called a split central simple algebra
over F . If A is finite dimensional central simple over F , then an extension field K of F
is called a splitting field for A if AK := A ⊗F K is split over K. We also say K splits A.

       We have the following simple properties:

       • Any K/F is a splitting field of Mn (F ).

       • If K is a splitting field of A and B, then it is a splitting field of A ⊗F B,(38) and of
         Aop , hence of Ae .

       • If A = Mn (D) for some central division F -algebra D, then K is a splitting field of A
         if and only if K is a splitting field of D. As a consequence, we only need to consider
         splitting fields of a central division F -algebra.

         Proof. In fact, since Mn (D) ∼
                                      = Mn (F ) ⊗F D, we always have

                      = (Mn (F ) ⊗F K) ⊗K (D ⊗F K) ∼
          Mn (D) ⊗F K ∼                            = Mn (K) ⊗K Mm (∆) ∼
                                                                      = Mmn (∆),

         here we used that, since D ⊗F K is a central simple algebra over K (because D is
         over F ), we have D ⊗F K ∼  = Mm (∆) for some central division K-algebra ∆. Now if
         K splits D, then ∆ = K, hence K splits A.
         Conversely, if K splits A, then Mn (D) ⊗F K ∼
                                                     = Mr (K) for some r. The isomorphism
                   ∼
         Mmn (∆) = Mr (K) then shows that ∆ = K and mn = r, so K also splits D. Indeed,
         in Wedderburn-Artin theorem, if A ∼  = Mn (D) is a simple artinian ring (where D is
         a division ring), then D and n are uniquely determined by A.
(38)
                             = (A ⊗F K) ⊗K (K ⊗F B) = Mm (K) ⊗K Mn (K) ∼
       Because (A ⊗F B) ⊗F K ∼                                         = Mmn (K).




                                                108
       • Splitting fields exist. Indeed, if K = F is an algebraical closure of F , then K splits
         D.

The next result is less obvious.

Proposition 3.8.18. Let D be a finite dimensional central division F -algebra, then every
maximal subfield K of D contains F and is a splitting field of D.

Lemma 3.8.19. Let A be a subalgebra of some F -algebra B, and view B as an A ⊗k B op -
module. Then EndA⊗F B op (B) ∼
                             = CB (A).

Proof. We have EndA⊗F B op (B) ,→ EndB op (B) ∼= B, so we can identify EndA⊗F B op (B)
with a subalgebra in B, and it makes sense to compare it with CB (A). For y ∈ B, let
φy : B → B, sending x to yx (which is B op -equivariant). It is A ⊗F B op -equivariant if
and only if
                             φy ((a ⊗ b)x) = (a ⊗ b)φy (x)
i.e.
                                         y(axb) = a(yx)b,
if and only if ya = ay for any a ∈ A, if and only if y ∈ CB (A).

Proof of Proposition 3.8.18. We first prove that CD (K) = K. Let K ′ = CD (K). Then
K ′ ⊃ K and if K ′ ̸= K, we can choose x ∈ K ′ \K. As in the proof of Lemma 3.6.11,
the subalgebra generated by K and x is a subfield of D strictly containing K, which
contradicts the maximality of K. Thus CD (K) = K. In particular, K contains F (as
F = Z(D) ⊆ CD (K)).
    Write R := K ⊗F Dop . We make D into a left R-module by (k ⊗ d)x := kxd. Then
D is a simple faithful R-module, because R is also a simple ring. Lemma 3.8.19 implies
that EndR (D) ∼= CD (K), thus EndR (D) = K by the last paragraph. By Jacobson density
theorem we deduce that R ∼  = EndK (D) = Mn (K) where n = dimK D. In other words, K
        op
splits D , hence also splits D.

       The proof gives the following result.

Corollary 3.8.20. Let K be the maximal subfield of a finite dimensional central division
F -algebra D, then CD (K) = K. Moreover, (dimF K)2 = dimF D.

Proof. By the isomorphism K ⊗F Dop ∼= EndK (D) ∼
                                               = Mn (K), where n = dimK D, we
obtain
                         dimF D = dimK (K ⊗F Dop ) = n2 .
Since dimF D = dimF K · dimK D, we also get dimF K = n.

Remark 3.8.21. It can be shown that every central division F -algebra D contains a
maximal subfield which is separable over F . Thus any central simple F -algebra splits over
a finite separable extension of F .

                                               109
Example 3.8.22. In general, maximal subfields of D are not unique. If H = R ⊕ Ri ⊕
Rj ⊕ Rk, with i2 = j 2 = k 2 = ijk = −1, denotes the usual Hamilton 4-dimensional
division algebra over R, then H contains C = R[i] as a maximal subfield. Also R[j], R[k]
are maximal subfields, both isomorphic to C. Of course, C is separable over R.

Skolem-Noether theorem
Theorem 3.8.23. Let B be a finite dimensional central simple F -algebra and A be a
simple subalgebra of B. For any homomorphisms f, g : A → B of F -algebras, there exists
y ∈ B × such that
                              g(a) = y −1 f (a)y, ∀a ∈ A.
   In particular, any f : A → B can be extended to an automorphism of B which is
inner, that is, of the form a 7→ y −1 ay for some y ∈ B × .

   Note that since A is simple, any F -algebra homomorphism is non-zero, hence injective.

Proof. Set R := A ⊗F B op . It is a finite-dimensional simple F -algebra by Theorem 3.8.12.
Any module for R is semi-simple and any two simple R-modules are isomorphic, by
combining Proposition 3.1.5 and Proposition 3.2.12. Hence any two R-modules of the
same finite dimension over F are isomorphic.
    There are two left R-modules structures on B. One is defined using the usual left and
right multiplication by A and B, i.e. (a⊗b)x = f (a)xb. The other one is: (a⊗b)x = g(a)xb.
These two R-module structures must be intertwined by some ℓ ∈ AutR (B), namely

(18)                              ℓ(f (a)xb) = g(a)ℓ(x)b

for all a ∈ A, x, b ∈ B. On the other hand, ℓ is a right B-module endomorphism of B, it
has the form x 7→ yx with y = ℓ(1). Note that y ∈ B × , because ℓ is an automorphism
and ℓ−1 also has the form x 7→ y ′ x with y ′ = ℓ−1 (1), thus yy ′ = y ′ y = 1.
    Taking x = 1 and b = 1 in (18), we obtain

                                     yf (a) = g(a)y,

i.e. g(a) = yf (a)y −1 for a ∈ A.
     For the last statement, take g to be the natural embedding A ,→ B. Then f can be
extended to the inner automorphism a 7→ y −1 ay.

Corollary 3.8.24 (Skolem-Noether). Any automorphism of a finite dimensional central
simple algebra is inner.

Corollary 3.8.25. Let A be a finite dimensional central simple F -algebra and K splits
A. If f : A ⊗F K ∼= Mn (K) and g : A ⊗F K → Mn (K) are two K-algebra isomorphisms.
Then there exists P ∈ GLn (K) such that for all a ∈ A:

                                g(a ⊗ 1) = P −1 f (a ⊗ 1)P.

                                           110
Proof. Apply Theorem 3.8.23 to B = Mn (K) and A := A ⊗F K here. Then there exists
P ∈ GLn (K) such that g = P −1 f P on A.

                                                          = K2 as F -algebras, then K1
                                             ×
and K2 are conjugate, i.e. there exists y ∈ A such that K2 = y −1 K1 y.

Theorem 3.8.23.

Theorem 3.8.27 (Wedderburn). Any finite division ring is a field.

Proof. Assume D is a finite division simple F -algebra (this implies that F is a finite
field). Then any maximal subfield of D is an extension of F with degree n (determined
by D). Such a finite field is unique up to isomorphism ! Therefore we can apply Corollary
3.8.26 to deduce that two maximal subfields are conjugate. Now consider D× which is a
multiplicative group, and K × (where K is a fixed maximal subfield). Every element of
D× is contained in some maximal subfield. Hence
                                            [
                                    D× =       a−1 K × a.
                                           a∈D×

  We have the following result from finite group theory. From which we deduce that
K = D and D is commutative.
Lemma 3.8.28. Let H be a proper subgroup of a finite group G, then G ̸= g∈G g −1 Hg.
                                                                       S

Proof. If g ′ = hg for h ∈ H, then g ′−1 Hg ′ = g −1 Hg, so there are at most [G : H] distinct
sets g −1 Hg. But each one of them has the same cardinality as H, and they have non-trivial
intersection (as they all contain the identity element), the result easily follows.

Theorem 3.8.29 (Frobenius). The only finite dimensional division R-algebras are R, C
and H.

Proof. Let D be such an R-algebra. If the center of D is C, then D ∼     = C by Lemma
3.6.11. So we assume Z(D) = R and D ̸= R from now on. Let K be a maximal subfield
of D. Since [D : R] > 1, so [K : F ] > 1 and K must be isomorphic to C. As [K : R] = 2,
this implies [D : R] = 4 by Corollary 3.8.20. We will construct a subalgebra of D which
is isomorphic to H, hence D ∼  = H comparing their dimensions.
              ∼
     Since K = C, we can find i ∈ K such that i2 = −1. By Theorem 3.8.23 the R-
embedding K ,→ D, sending i to −i, is given by a conjugation: there exists j ∈ D× such
that j −1 ij = −i. We see that j ∈  / R = Z(D) and we want to prove that j 2 ∈ −R≥0 ,
hence j 2 = −1 up to multiplication by an element of R. The equality j −1 ij = −i is just
i−1 ji = −j. Let K ′ be the field generated by R and j; then K ′ ∼
                                                                 = C and

                                   K ′ → K ′ , x 7→ i−1 xi

                                             111
is a well-defined field isomorphism which fixes R. This corresponds to the unique non-
trivial element σ in Gal(C/R), hence is just the complex conjugation. Since in C, σ(x) =
                                                                                   √
−x if and only if x2 ∈ −R≥0 , we get j 2 = −r with r ∈ R≥0 . Replacing j by j/ r we
obtain j 2 = −1, and the equality j −1 ij = −i still holds true. Let k := ij, then clearly
k 2 = −1 and ijk = −1. It is easy to check that {1, i, j, k} are linearly independent
over R,(39) hence the subalgebra generated by i, j, k is isomorphic to H, and the proof is
finished.

Double centralizer theorem for central simple algebras
Theorem 3.8.30. Let B be a central simple F -algebra.

  (i) For every semi-simple F -subalgebra A of B, we have CB (CB (A)) = A.

 (ii) If A is simple, then CB (A) is simple. Furthermore, dimF B = (dimF A)·(dimF CB (A)).

Remark 3.8.31. (1) If A is central simple, then this is obvious from Theorem 3.8.10.
Indeed, it implies that B ∼ = A⊗F CB (A), and since B is central simple, so is CB (A). Apply
the theorem again we get A = CB (CB (A)), giving (i). But it is crucial to not restrict to
central subalgebras, since maximal subfields of a division algebra are typically not central.
    (2) If we apply Theorem 3.8.30 to B = D is division central F -algebra, and A = K
a maximal subfield of D, we get CD (K) = K, thus dimF D = (dimF K)2 which recovers
Corollary 3.8.20.
    (3) Although B is central simple, it may contain a semi-simple F -subalgebra: if B =
Mn (F ), then {diag(b1 , . . . , bn )} is isomorphic to F × F × · · · × F which is a semi-simple
subalgebra. We check that CB (A) = A, hence CB (CB (A)) = A. We could also consider
another semi-simple sub-algebra: A′ = {diag(b1 , . . . , bn−2 , b, b)}, which is isomorphic to
F × · · · × F (n − 1 copies). Then CB (A′ ) consists of matrices of the form
                                                            
                                          b1 0 0         0
                                       0 b2 0           0
                                                            
                                                             .
                                                             
                                       0 0 ...          0
                                      
                                                             
                                          0 0 0 M2 (F )

Taking CB (·) again, we get back A′ .

Proof. (i) Set R := A ⊗F B op ; it is a semi-simple F -algebra. This is because A is semi-
simple, hence is a direct product of simple rings by Wedderburn-Artin theorem, and we
apply Corollary 3.8.12. As consequence, any module over R is semi-simple; in particular
B, viewed as R-module, is semi-simple.
   Lemma 3.8.19 implies that R′ := EndR (B) ∼   = CB (A). By Jacobson density theorem,
the morphism R → EndR′ (B) is surjective. Given x ∈ CB (CB (A)), the endomorphism
(39)
       Indeed, this follows from the equality (a + bi + cj + dk)(a − bi − cj − dk) = a2 + b2 + c2 + d2 .

    We need to show this implies x ∈ A. Since B is central simple, the homomorphism

A ⊗F B op → EndF (B) is injective. Since

                             x ⊗ 1 ∈ (A ⊗ B op ) ∩ (B ⊗ 1) = A ⊗ 1

we obtain x ∈ A.
   (ii) Assume A is simple. Then R is also simple. Write R as Ms (D) by Wedderburn-

Then M ∼ = D⊕s and Dop := EndR (M ). Write R B = M ⊕r viewed as R-modules. It follows

3.9     Brauer groups
Definition 3.9.1. Let A and B be central simple F -algebras. We say that A is similar
to B, written as A ∼ B, if there exists n, m ≥ 1 such that Mn (A) ∼
                                                                  = Mm (B).

Lemma 3.9.2. This defines an equivalence relation. Moreover, if A ∼ A′ and B ∼ B ′ ,
then A ⊗F A′ ∼ B ⊗F B ′ .

Proof. Exercise.

    Trivially, if A = Mn (D) for some central division F -algebra D, then A ∼ D. If D, D′
are central division F -algebras, then D ∼ D′ if and only if D ∼
                                                               = D′ by Wedderburn-Artin
theorem. For any A we write [A] for its equivalent class. Let Br(F ) be the monoid formed
by the equivalence classes of central simple F -algebras, the multiplication being given by
⊗ and the unit being the class of Mn (F ), or F .
(40)
    Remark: it is in fact an isomorphism, since R′ ,→ B ⊗F B op ∼
                                                                = EndF (B), while EndR′ (B) also embeds
in EndF (B).


                                                 113
Proposition 3.9.3. Br(F ) is an abelian group, called the Brauer group of F .

Proof. Since A is central simple, we have A ⊗F Aop ∼
                                                   = Mn (F ) ∼ F , so [A] has an inverse
and Br(F ) becomes a group. Commutativity follows from the fact A ⊗F B ∼  = B ⊗F A

Example 3.9.4. 1. Br(R) ∼     = Z/2Z, the nontrivial element being represented by Hamil-
ton’s quaternion algebra. As a consequence, Hop ∼   = H.
    2. If F is a finite field, then any finite division algebra is a field (Wedderburn’s
theorem).
    3. If F is separably closed, then Br(F ) is trivial.
    4. Br(F ) is always torsion as a Z-module. (This uses a cohomological interpretation
of Br(F ).)


4     Representation theory
4.1    Representations
We fix a group G (usually will be a finite group) and a field F .

Definition 4.1.1. By a linear representation (V, ρ) of a group G, we mean a homomor-
phism of groups G → AutF (V ) (bijective linear transformations). The degree of ρ is the
dimension of V over F .

    This is equivalent to giving an F -linear action of G on V : G × V → V .
    A morphism between two representations (V1 , ρ1 ) and (V2 , ρ2 ) is an F -linear map
f : V1 → V2 such that the diagram
                                                   f
                                          V1            / V2

                                     ρ1 (g)                   ρ2 (g)
                                                         
                                          V1            / V2
                                                   f


commutes for every g ∈ G. We write HomG (V1 , V2 ) for the set of morphisms from V1 to
V2 . It is an F -vector space.
     Two representations are said to be isomorphic or equivalent if there exists a bijective
morphism between them.
     In this course, we will only consider finite dimensional representations. Let RepF (G)
denote the category of finite dimensional representations of G over F .
     Matrix representation: Let B = {e1 , ..., en } be a basis for V /F . If a ∈ EndF (V ),
we write                                 n
                                       X
                                 aei =      αij uj , 1 ≤ i ≤ n
                                      j=1

and obtain the matrix (αij ). In this way we get a group homomorphism ρB : G → GLn (F ).
Such a homomorphism is called a matrix representation of G of degree n. A change of

                                                  114
basis from B to B ′ = {e′1 , · · · , e′n } replaces ρB by ρB ′ = P ρB P −1 , where P is the transition
matrix.

 (1) Trivial representation: V = F , ρ(g)v = v for any g ∈ G and v ∈ V . The matrix
     representation is g 7→ Id.

 (2) A representation of degree 1 is a homomorphism G → GL1 (F ) = F × , also called
     character. When G is finite, ρ(g) are roots of unity. For example, for ρ : G =
     Z/4Z → C× , its image must lie in {1, −1, i, −i}.

 (3) Permutation representation. Let X be a finite set on which G acts. Define a vector
     space V of dimension n := |X|, with basis {ex }x∈X . Let G act on ex via g ·ex := eg.x .
     This gives a linear representation of G on V . In particular,
      • If X = {1, . . . , n}, G = Sn , we get a representation of Sn to GLn (F ), each σ
      with value being a permutation matrix. More generally, if there exists a group
      homomorphism G → Sn , we get a representation of G.
      • Let X = G and G acts on it via g.x = gx. The resulting representation of G on V is
      called the left regular representation. A given linear representation ρ : G → AutF (V )
      is isomorphic to the left regular representation, if and only if there exists v ∈ V, such
      that (ρ(g)v)g∈G is a basis of V .

 (4) Let V be the F -vector space spanned by homogenous polynomials in x, y of degree
     n, so that dimF V = n + 1 . Let G = SL2 (F ) act on V by:
                             a b xi y n−i = (ax + cy)i (bx + dy)n−i
                                 
                             c d

      One checks that this defines a representation of G.


4.2     Group algebras
Definition 4.2.1. The group algebra F [G] is the F -algebra having the set G as a basis:
                                           P
every a ∈ F [G] is written uniquely as a = g∈G ag g (finite sum); the multiplication is

Remark 4.2.2. In general, F [G]× contains G but is larger than G. F [G] is commutative
if and only if G is abelian.

Example 4.2.3. Let G = C4 be the cyclic group of order 4 and let s be a generator.
Then G = {1, s, s2 , s3 }, and F [G] = F ⊕ F s ⊕ F s2 ⊕ F s3 as a vector space over F . As
an algebra, we have an isomorphism
                                                  ∼
                                 F [X]/(X 4 − 1) −

  (i) If F = C, then this implies F [G] ∼

 (ii) If F = R, then F [G] ∼

(iii) If F = F2 , then F [G] ∼

Remark 4.2.4. It can happen that G1 ≇ G1 but F [G1 ] ∼   = F [G2 ]. For example, if G is
any abelian group of order n, then C[G] is semi-simple (see Theorem 4.2.9 below) and

                           C[G] ∼

However, Perlis and Walker proved that if G1 , G2 are finite abelian groups such that
