   Universal property of F [G]: for any F -algebra R, we have a natural isomorphism

                          HomF -Alg (F [G], R) ∼
                                               = HomGr (G, R× ).

Proposition 4.2.5. There is an equivalence between the categories RepF (G) and (left)
F [G]-Mod, given as follows. Let (V, ρ) be a representation of G over k, we make V into
an F [G]-module by             X               X
                                   ag g · v :=    ag ρ(g)v.
                                 g∈G           g∈G

Proof. We use
                    HomF -Alg (F [G], Endk (V )) ∼
                                                 = HomG (G, AutF (V )).


   We can define many concepts in representation theory using the corresponding ones
in module theory. Examples are as follows. Let (V, ρ) be a representation of G over F .

   • A submodule U of V is the same thing as a subspace U which is stable by G. We
     call it a sub-representation. Its quotient V /U is still an F [G]-module, called quotient
     representation. This gives a short exact sequence

                                   0 → U → V → U/V → 0.

   • V is said irreducible if it is simple as an F [G]-module; absolutely irreducible if it is
     absolutely simple as an F [G]-module, i.e. V ⊗F E is still simple as an E[G]-module
     for any field extension E/F ; reducible if not irreducible. V is called completely
     reducible (also called semi-simple) if it is semi-simple as an F [G]-module. V is
     called indecomposable if it is indecomposable as an F [G]-module.




                                            116
Lemma 4.2.6. Let M be simple left R-module, where R is a finite dimensional F -algebra.
The following are equivalent:
   (1) M is absolutely simple;
   (2) M ⊗F E is simple for every finite extension E of F ;
   (3) EndR (M ) = F ;
   (4) the homomorphism R → EndF (M ) is surjective.

Proof. (1)=⇒(2) is trivial.
    (2)=⇒(3) Let φ ∈ EndR (M ). In particular, it is an element in EndF (M ). Choose E/F
large enough to contain an eigenvalue of φ, say λ ∈ E. Set φE := E⊗F φ ∈ EndE (M ⊗F E).
Then φE − λ is not a bijection, i.e. not invertible in EndE (M ⊗F E). But M ⊗F E is
simple by assumption, so φE = λ, hence φ = λ too (and λ ∈ F ).
    (3)=⇒(4) follows from Jacobson density theorem.
    (4)=⇒(1) For every field extension E/F , the homomorphism R⊗F E → EndE (M ⊗F E)
is again surjective. By linear algebra, one sees that M ⊗F E is a simple R ⊗F E-module.
Indeed, if N ̸= 0 is a proper R ⊗F E-submodule of M ⊗F E, we may choose v ∈ N and
w∈ / N , and define an E-linear map M ⊗F E → M ⊗F E sending v to w; clearly such a
map can not lie in the image of R ⊗F E.

Definition 4.2.7. A field F is called a splitting field of G if any simple F [G]-module is
absolutely simple. We also say that G splits over F .

Corollary 4.2.8. Given F and G, there exists a finite extension E/F over which G splits.

   Basic operations on representations:

     Change of fields Let (ρ, V ) be a representation of G over F . Let E/F be a field
     extension. Then (ρ ⊗ 1, V ⊗F E) is a representation of G over E.

     Direct sum if V = V1 ⊕ V2 with ρ = (ρ1 , ρ2 ).

     External tensor product Let (Vi , ρi ) be a representation of Gi over F , for i = 1, 2.
     Then ρ1 ⊠ ρ2 becomes a representation of G1 × G2 .

     Tensor product Let (V1 , ρ1 ), (V2 , ρ2 ) be representations of G over F , then V1 ⊗F V2
     becomes a representation by letting

                                  (ρ1 ⊗ ρ2 )(g) = ρ1 (g) ⊗ ρ2 (g).

     As F [G]-modules, we get V1 ⊗F V2 , via g(v1 ⊗ v2 ) = (gv1 ) ⊗ (gv2 ).

     Hom-spaces Let (V1 , ρ1 ), (V2 , ρ2 ) be representations of G over F . The space
     HomF (V1 , V2 ) is equipped with a representation of G:

     (19)                 g · f := ρ2 (g) ◦ f ◦ ρ1 (g)−1 ∈ HomF (V1 , V2 ).

     We have HomG (V1 , V2 ) is just HomF (V1 , V2 )G .

                                             117
         Contragredient representation Given (V1 , ρ1 ), take V2 = F the trivial repre-
         sentation, we get the contragredient representation HomF (V1 , F ), denoted by V1∗ .
         Choose B = {ei } a base of V1 , and B ∗ = {e∗i } the dual base of V1∗ .

         Pull-back/restriction/inflation Let φ : H → G be a group homomorphism.
         then we deduce a representation of H from a representation of G. In fact, φ induces
         an F -algebra homomorphism F [H] → F [G]. When H → G is an inclusion, the
         pull-back is called the restriction; when it is a quotient, it is called the inflation
         from G to H.

         Reduction Let H be a normal subgroup of G and (V, ρ) be a representation of G.
         Set V N := {v ∈ V : hv = v}. Then V N becomes a representation of G/H.

         Regular representation: F [G] viewed as a left F [G]-module, corresponds to the
         left regular representation of G.

Theorem 4.2.9. (Maschke’s theorem) Assume G is finite. Then F [G] is a semi-simple
ring iff char(F ) does not divide |G|. In particular, if char(F ) = 0, F [G] is semi-simple.

such that V = W ⊕ W ′ as vector spaces, and let π : V ↠ W be the projection map.

Corollary 4.2.13. With the notation in Theorem 4.2.11, F is a splitting field of G if
and only if Di = F for any 1 ≤ i ≤ r. If this is the case, we have s = r, i.e. the
number of isomorphism classes of irreducible representations of G is equal to the number
of conjugacy classes in G.

Proof. This is a combination of Proposition 3.1.5 and Lemma 4.2.6.

Example 4.2.14. We saw that R[C4 ] ∼    = R ⊕ R ⊕ C. This example shows that, when F is
not algebraically closed, Di need not be central over F .

Example 4.2.15. We have C[S3 ] ∼
                               = C ⊕ C ⊕ M2 (C). This is because S3 has 3 conjugacy
classes, hence r = 3 and
                                n21 + n22 + n23 = 6,
which forces (up to renumeration) n1 = n2 = 1, n3 = 2.

Example 4.2.16. Let Q8 = {±1, ±i, ±j, ±k} be the quaternion group, a subgroup of H.
It has 5 conjugacy classes:

                          {1}, {−1}, {i, −i}, {j, −j}, {k, −k}.

The irreducible C-representations of Q8 have dimension 1, 1, 1, 1, 2 respectively. Precisely,
the quotient group G/{±1} is abelian of order 4, isomorphic to Z/2Z × Z/2Z, inducing
4 1-dim representations. Fixing an isomorphism H ⊗R C ∼    = M2 (C), we obtain one 2-dim
representation of Q8 via Q8 ,→H ⊗R C ∼   = M2 (C). However, this last representation can
not be defined over R.
    For irreducible R-representations of H, again 4 of them are 1-dimensional and one of
them is 4-dimensional which is induced by the natural action of Q8 on H. This corresponds
to the fact that
                              R[Q8 ] = R ⊕ R ⊕ R ⊕ R ⊕ H.

Corollary 4.2.17. If G is abelian, then any absolutely irreducible representation of G is
of degree 1.

                                             120
Proof. We may assume F is a splitting field of G. Hence there are d = |G| conjugacy
classes and di=1 n2i = |G| = d, showing ni = 1.
             P

    This is true even without the assumption char(F ) ∤ |G|. Indeed, rad(F [G]) annihilates
any simple module, so we may assume rad(F [G]) = 0, thus F [G] is semi-simple (as it is
artinian). Therefore F [G] decomposes as a direct sum of simple matrix rings Mni (Di ).
Since G is abelian, F [G] is commutative. But Mni (Di ) is commutative only when ni =
1.

Remark 4.2.18. If the field F is algebraically closed and characteristic 0, then the con-
verse statement is still true. However, without the algebraically closed condition, the above
example shows that the converse statement is wrong: all absolutely irreducible represen-
tations of G are of degree 1 cannot imply that G is abelian.


4.3    Character of representations
From now on, we consider linear representations over F , such that char(F ) ∤ |G| and G
splits over F . This is equivalent to saying that every irreducible representation of G is
absolutely irreducible.
    Let V be a finite dimensional F -vector space. For a ∈ EndF (V ) we let Tr(a) denote
the trace of a. It is also the sum of all eigenvalues of a.

Definition 4.3.1. Let (V, ρ) be a linear representation of G. We define the map χρ =
χV : G → F by
                                   χρ (g) = Tr(ρ(g)).
The F -valued function χρ is called the character of the representation ρ. We extend χ to
a function on F [G] by linearity.
    If ρ is irreducible, then χρ is called irreducible. If F = C, χρ is called a complex
character. The degree of ρ is called the degree of χρ .

   Basic properties:

   • Equivalent representations have the same characters.

   • If E/F is a field extension, then χρ⊗E = χρ . Indeed, choose {ei } a basis of V /F ,
     then {ei ⊗ 1} is a basis of V ⊗F E over E.

   • Any character is a class function, i.e. it is constant on every conjugacy class:
     χρ (h−1 gh) = χρ (g).

   • deg ρ = χρ (1).

   • If U ⊂ V is a sub-representation, then χV = χU + χV /U . Indeed, choose a basis of
     U over F , and extend it to a basis of V , then with respect to this basis the matrix
                                                ρ   ∗ 
     of ρ(g) is upper triangular, of the form 0U ρV /U .


                                            121
   • If ρ1 , ρ2 are representations of G, then χρ1 ⊕ρ2 = χρ1 + χρ2 , χρ1 ⊗ρ2 = χρ1 χρ2 .

   • χρ∗ (g) = χρ (g −1 ): this is because ρ∗B ∗ = (t ρB )−1 where B ∗ is the dual basis of B.

   • If F ⊂ C, then χρ (g −1 ) = χρ (g). To see this, we may assume F = C. Any eigenvalue
     of χρ (g) is a root of unity, and eigenvalues of ρ(g −1 ) are just the inverse of the ones
     of ρ(g). As ϵ−1 = ϵ for any root of unity ϵ, the result follows.

   Denote by cf(G) the F -vector space of class functions on G. We define an inner

Theorem 4.3.6. χi : 1 ≤ i ≤ r is an orthonormal basis for cf(G).

Proof. We already see that χi are orthonormal; so they must be linearly independent. In
             P
fact, if χ := i αi χi = 0, then take ⟨χ, χi ⟩ for any i, we get αi = 0.
    It forms a basis because the number of conjugacy classes in G is equal to the number
of irreducible representations of G.

Corollary 4.3.7. The space cf(G) admits two bases: the irreducible characters χi and
the characteristic functions 1C of the conjugacy classes C ⊂ G.

Corollary 4.3.8. Furthermore, let us assume char(F ) = 0.

  (i) Let W be a representation of G and V be an irreducible representation of G. Then
      the multiplicity of V in W is equal to ⟨χW , χV ⟩.

 (ii) Two representations of G with the same characters are isomorphic.

(iii) Let W be a representation of G. Then W is irreducible if and only if ⟨χW , χW ⟩ = 1.

Proof. Clear.

4.4    Examples
We always take F = C in this subsection.



                                                    124
4.4.1   Cyclic groups
Let G = ⟨g⟩ of order n, i.e. G ∼    = Z/nZ. It is abelian, so there are n isomorphism
classes of irreducible representations. Let ζn ∈ C be a primitive n-th root of unity. For
0 ≤ i ≤ n − 1, define ρi to be
                                     ρi (g k ) = ζnik ∈ C×
which is a 1-dimensional representation of G.

4.4.2   Character table of S3
Let G = S3 . It has order 6, and three conjugacy classes:

                C1 = {id}, C2 = {(12), (13), (23)}, C3 = {(123), (132)}.

By the relation n21 + n22 + n23 = 6, we must have n1 = 1, n2 = 1, n3 = 2, i.e. there are two
irreducible representations of degree 1, and one of degree 2. It is easy to construct the
irreducible representations of degree 1: the trivial one and the character sgn. We know
already
                                          C1 C2 C3
                                       ♯ 1     3    2
                                      χ1 1     1    1
                                      χ2 1 −1 1
                                      χ3 2 α β
The orthogonal relations ⟨χ3 , χ1 ⟩ = ⟨χ3 , χ2 ⟩ = 0 imply that
                                     1
                                       (2 + 3α + 2β) = 0
                                     6
                                   1
                                     (2 − 3α + 2β) = 0
                                   6
we get β = −1 and α = 0. In fact, since χ2 χ3 is also an irreducible character of G, it
must be equal to χ3 , hence α = 0.
   Let us construct the representation ρ3 . Since S3 acts naturally on X = {1, 2, 3}, we
obtain a permutation representation of degree 3, say π. Its character is given by

                          χπ (C1 ) = 3, χπ (C2 ) = 1, χπ (C3 ) = 0.

Such a representation is not irreducible (always), since F (e1 + e2 + e3 ) is stable under S3 .
The quotient is then isomorphic to ρ3 , because the character is χπ − χ1 , which is equal to
χ3 .
     As a consequence, we have a decomposition C[S3 ] ∼ = C ⊕ C ⊕ M2 (C).




                                             125
4.4.3   Character table of A4
Let G = A4 . It has order 12. Its conjugacy classes are

                     C1 = {id}, C2 = {(12)(34), (13)(24), (14)(23)}

           C3 = {(123), (142), (134), (243)}, C4 = {(132), (124), (143), (234)}.
So there are in total 4 non-isomorphic irreducible representations. On the other hand, A4
contains a normal subgroup of order 4, i.e. V4 = C1 ∪ C2 . The quotient A4 /V4 is abelian
of order 3. There are three non-isomorphic irreducible representations of A4 /V4 , all of
degree 1. Inflation to A4 gives us three non-isomorphic representations of A4 of degree 1.
This implies that the last irreducible representation has degree 3.
    Let ω = e2πi/3 be a primitive 3-th root of unity. Note that the class of (123) in A4 /V4
is a generator, and (123)2 = (132). We obtain the character table as follows:

                                           C1 C2 C3 C4
                                     ♯     1 3 4 4
                                    χ1     1 1 1 1
                                    χ2     1 1 ω ω2
                                    χ3     1 1 ω2 ω
                                    χ4     3 α β γ
We must have β = γ = 0 as χ2 χ4 is still an irreducible character of G of degree 3, hence
must be equal to χ4 . To determine α, we use the orthogonal relation for rows ⟨χ4 , χ1 ⟩ = 0,
i.e. 3 + 3α + 0 + 0 = 0, hence α = −1.
     Let us construct the representation ρ4 . Since A4 acts on X = {1, 2, 3, 4}, we get as
above a permutation of degree 4, say π. Its character is given by

                  χπ (C1 ) = 4, χπ (C2 ) = 0, χπ (C3 ) = 1, χπ (C4 ) = 1.

We have ρ1 ⊂ π as sub-representation, whose quotient has character χπ − χ1 , which is χ4 .
    Next, we consider the tensor product ρ4 ⊗ ρ4 , which has degree 9. It has character χ24 .
If we write ρ4 ⊗ ρ4 = 4i=1 ρdi i , then
                      L

                                           1
                      d1 = ⟨χ1 , χ24 ⟩ =      (1 × 1 × 9 + 3 × 1 × 1) = 1
                                           12
                       d2 = ⟨χ2 , χ24 ⟩ = 1, d3 = ⟨χ3 , χ24 ⟩ = 1
                                             1
                          d4 = ⟨χ4 , χ24 ⟩ = (27 − 3) = 2.
                                            12
   We have a decomposition

                               C[A4 ] ∼
                                      = C ⊕ C ⊕ C ⊕ M3 (C).

However, as χ2 , χ3 are not defined over R, we have

                                  R[A4 ] ∼
                                         = R ⊕ C ⊕ M3 (R).

4.4.4     Character table of Dn
Recall that Dn = ⟨r, s : rn = 1, s2 = 1, srs = r−1 ⟩. It has order 2n. We first determine its
conjugacy classes. Every element of Dn has the form rk or srk , where 0 ≤ k ≤ n − 1. We
have the following:
   • since srk s = r−k , we get (srk )2 = 1, and {rk , r−k } are in the same conjugacy class. If
     n is even, {r0 = 1} and {rn/2 } form single classes, so {rk } splits into n2 +1 conjugacy
     classes. When n is odd, they split into n+1  2
                                                      conjugacy classes.
                   ′
   • rk and srk can not be in one conjugacy class.

   • r−i sri = sr2i , so that s ∼ sr2 ∼ sr4 ∼ · · · . So when n is even {srk ; 0 ≤ k ≤ n − 1}
     splits into two conjugacy classes; when n is odd, it forms a single conjugacy class.
In summary, if n is even, there are n2 + 1 + 2 conjugacy classes; if n is odd, there are
n+1

4.4.5   Character table of Q8
Let Q = Q8 be the quarternion group. By definition, Q is of order 8 defined by

                Q = {1, i, j, k, −1, −i, −j, −k}, i2 = j 2 = k 2 = ijk = −1

or equivalently (setting a = i, b = j)

               Q = {1, a, a2 , a3 , b, ab, a2 b, a3 b|a4 = 1, b2 = a2 , bab−1 = a−1 }.

It has 5 conjugacy classes:

                           C1 = {1}, C2 = {−1}, C3 = {i, −i},

                                C4 = {j, −j}, C5 = {k, −k}.
Since i n2i = 8, we know the degree of any irreducible representation is ≤ 2. Moreover,
        P

we see that four out of the five irreducible representations have degree 1 and only one has
degree 2: 1 + 1 + 1 + 1 + 22 = 8. As H = {1, −1} is a normal subgroup of Q, we first
study the quotient group Q/H, which is isomorphic to Z2 × Z2 = {1, ī, j̄, k̄}. Since this
is an abelian group, it has 4 irreducible representations, all of degree 1. Inflation gives us
all the irreducible representations of degree 1 of Q. In all we obtain

                                       C 1 C 2 C3 C4 C5
                                   ♯   1 1     2  2  2
                                  χ1   1 1     1  1  1
                                  χ2   1 1     1 −1 −1
                                  χ3   1 1 −1 1 −1
                                  χ4   1 1 −1 −1 1
                                  χ5   2 α 0      0  0

                                                128
Then we use the orthogonal relation to show α = −2 as in the case of D4 . In particular,
we see that D4 and Q8 have the same character table.
   We want to construct the representation ρ5 . Recall that Q is a subgroup of H× , and H
                                                                                       
                                                   0
can be embedded in GL2 (C) by sending a = i 7→ 0i −i   , b = j 7→ −10 1              0 i
                                                                      0 , ab = k 7→ i 0 .
So ρ5 is simply the embedding ρ5 : Q ,→ GL2 (C).
Corollary 4.4.2. The representation ρ5 is a faithful representation of Q.
   We deduce that
                            C[Q8 ] ∼
                                   = C ⊕ C ⊕ C ⊕ C ⊕ M2 (C).
Note that χi for 1 ≤ i ≤ 4 are defined over R, but ρ5 is not. The natural action of Q8 on
H gives an irreducible R-representation of degree 4. As a consequence,
                               R[Q8 ] ∼
                                      = R ⊕ R ⊕ R ⊕ R ⊕ H.

4.5    Induced representations and Frobenius reciprocity
In this subsection, G is a finite group and F can be a field of arbitrary characteristic, and
not necessarily a splitting field of G.

Corollary 4.5.6. If V is irreducible, and if ResG
                                                H V contains a sub-representation isomor-
                                       G
phic to W , then V is a quotient of IndH W .
Proof. The injection W ,→ ResG  H V (in particular non-zero) induces a non-zero map

IndH W → V . Since V is irreducible, this map must be surjective.
Corollary 4.5.7. Assume H is an abelian subgroup of G, let d = [G : H]. Assume F is
algebraically closed. Then every irreducible representation V of G has degree ≤ d.
Proof. Consider ResGH V . It contains an irreducible sub-representation, since it is of finite
length as F [H]-module. Since H is abelian and F is algebraically closed, it must be of
dimension 1, say χ by Corollary 4.2.17. Therefore V is a quotient of IndG  H χ.

4.6        Clifford’s theorem
Theorem 4.6.1. Let N be a normal subgroup of G. Let V be an irreducible representation
of G.
   (i) ResGN V is semisimple and is a direct sum of irreducible representations of N of
equal dimension.
   (ii) Fix W ,→ ResG                                            ∼       (45)
                       N V irreducible. Let H := {g ∈ G : gW = W }            which is a
subgroup of G containing N , then V is induced from some irreducible representation of
H.

Remark 4.6.2. In Jacobson’s book, H is denoted by T (W ) and called inertia subgroup
of W . It is not normal in general, and depends on W : in fact different choice of W yields
conjugate of H.

Proof. (i) First, we show that ResG
                                  N V always has an irreducible sub-representation, say
W . Indeed, as an F [N ]-module, V is artinian, hence contains a minimal sub-module,
which must be simple.
(45)

for g1 , g2 such that g1 H ̸= g2 H, g1 W ′ and g2 W ′ have no common Jordan-Hölder factors
(as F [N ]-modules). We conclude by Lemma 4.6.3 below.
Lemma 4.6.3. Let R be a ring and M an R-module of finite length. Assume that
M1 , . . . , Mr ⊂ M are submodules such that Mi and Mj have no common Jordan-Hölder
factor for any 1 ≤ i ̸= j ≤ r. Then ri=1 Mi = ri=1 Mi .
                                       P           L

Proof. Consider the natural surjective morphism f : ri=1 Mi ↠ ri=1 Mi . Suppose f is
                                                        L            P

not injective, and let 0 ̸= S ⊂ ker(f ) be a simple submodule. For 1 ≤ i ≤ r, let Si ⊂ Mi be
the projection of S to Mi . As S is simple, we have either Si ∼
                                                              = S or Si = 0. By assumption,
Mi and Mj have no common Jordan-Hölder factor, hence Si = 0 except for one i. This
means that S ⊂ Mi for some i. But f |Mi is injective, so S = 0, contradiction.
Corollary 4.6.4. Let N be a normal subgroup of G and V be a semi-simple G-representation.
Then ResG
        N V is still semi-simple.

4.7        Arithmetic considerations
4.7.1      Algebraic integers
If R is a commutative ring and x ∈ R. We say x is integral over Z if there exists n ≥ 1
and a0 , ..., an−1 ∈ Z such that
                                     xn + an−1 xn−1 + · · · a0 = 0.
When R = C, such x is called an algebraic integer.
(46)
       Proof : if U ⊊ s ⊗ W were a proper submodule, then s−1 U ⊊ W would a proper submodule of W .


                                                  136
Example 4.7.1. Consider R = Q. Then r ∈ Q is integral over Z if and only if r ∈ Z.
Proposition 4.7.2. Let x ∈ R. The following properties are equivalent:
  (i) x is integral over Z.

 (ii) The submodule Z[x] ⊂ R is a finitely generated Z-module.

(iii) There exists a finitely generated sub-Z-module of R which contains Z[x].
In particular, if R itself is finitely generated Z-module, then any element of R is integral
over Z.
Proof. (i)=⇒(ii) The equation implies that Z[x] is spanned over Z by {1, x, . . . , xn−1 }.
   (ii)=⇒(iii) trivial.
   (iii)=⇒ (i) Since Z is noetherian, if Z[x] is contained in a finitely generated Z-module,
then Z[x] is a noetherian module. Consider the ascending chain

                           Z ⊂ Z + Zx ⊂ Z + Zx + Zx2 ⊂ · · ·

which must stabilize. So for n large, xn ∈ i<n Zxi .
                                          P

Corollary 4.7.3. The set of integral elements in R form a subring of R.
Proof. Easy. If x, y are integral, then Z[x], Z[y] are finitely generated Z-modules, so is
Z[x, y]. Since x + y, xy ∈ Z[x, y], we get the result.

    From now on, we consider R := Z(C[G]), the center of C[G], which is a commutative
ring.
                                  P
Proposition 4.7.4. Let u =          g∈G u(g)g ∈ R such that u(g) ∈ C are all algebraic
integers, then u is integral over Z.
   Here, we may view the coefficient u(g) as a function G → C, which is moreover a class
function. This explains the notation.

Proof. Let C1 , . . . , Cr be the list of conjugacy classes in G and 1Ci be the characteristic
function. Then the 1Ci form a basis of R over C. We set

                                   R′ := Z1C1 ⊕ · · · ⊕ Z1Cr .

Claim: this is a subring. In fact, we have

with αijk equals the number of (g, h) such that l = gh for a fixed l ∈ Ck . In particular,
the coefficients belong to N. By Proposition 4.7.2, the 1Ci are integral over Z.
   Now, for u ∈ R we can write u = i αi 1Ci , with αi = u(gi ) for any gi ∈ Ci . Hence
                                       P

the αi are algebraic integers. By Corollary 4.7.3, u is integral over Z.

Theorem 4.7.8. Let Z(G) be the center of G. For any irreducible representation V of
G, (dim V )|[G : Z(G)].

Proof. Let m ∈ N and let Gm := G×G×· · ·×G. Let (V, ρ) be an irreducible representation
of G. We form the representation (V ⊗m , ρ⊗m ) of Gm :

Remark 4.7.9. Theorem 4.7.8 can be strengthened as follows: If A is an abelian normal
subgroup of G, then (dim V )|[G : A] for any irreducible C-representation V of G.
   As an example, the group S4 contains a normal abelian subgroup V4 . It has 5 irreducible
characters, whose degrees are respectively 1, 1, 2, 3, 3, all dividing 6 = [S4 : V4 ].

4.7.3   Characters of dimension ≥ 2
Theorem 4.7.10. Let V be an irreducible C-representation of G and assume dim(V ) ≥ 2.
Then there exists g ∈ G with χV (g) = 0.
Proof. Write χ = χV . There exists a finite Galois extension L/Q such that χ(g) ∈ L for
all g ∈ G. Consider                     Y     Y
                           B(χ) :=                |σ(χ(g))|2 .
                                        σ∈Gal(L/Q) g∈G

It is clear that B(χ) is fixed by Gal(L/Q), hence belongs to Q. However, all the values
σ(χ(g)) are sums of roots of unity, hence are integral elements. This implies that B(χ) is
also an algebraic integer, thus B(χ) ∈ Z≥0 . Below we will prove B(χ) = 0, which gives
the result.
    Since V is irreducible, one has
                            1 X                1 X
                                   χ(g)χ(g) =         |χ(g)|2 = 1.
                           |G| g∈G            |G| g∈G

Moreover, this holds true for σ ◦ χ (any σ) because it remains irreducible. Thus
                            X X
                                       |σ(χ(g))|2 = |G| · [L : Q].
                          σ∈Gal(L/Q) g∈G

By the arithmetic-geometric inequality, we obtain (setting n = |G| · [L : Q])

                                           B(χ)1/n ≤ 1

with equality if and only if |χ(g)|2 = |χ(g ′ )|2 for any g, g ′ ∈ G (the action of σ does not
change the value of |χ(g)|). Since χ(1) = dim(V ) > 1 by assumption and g∈G |χ(g)|2 =
                                                                               P

|G|, there must exist g ∈ G such that |χ(g)|2 < 1. Hence B(χ) < 1. However, it is an
integer, so B(χ) = 0.

Example 4.7.11. Below is the character table of G = S4 :
                                     C1 C 2 C3 C4 C5
                                χ1   1  1   1  1  1
                                χ2   1 −1 1    1 −1
                                χ3   2  0   2 −1 0
                                χ4   3  1 −1 0 −1
                                χ5   3 −1 −1 0    1
from which we see that 0 occurs in the last three rows.

4.7.4   p-groups
We use representation theory to prove the following classical result in group theory.
Proposition 4.7.12. Let p be a prime number. A group of order p2 is abelian.
Proof. We will prove that C[G] is commutative, this is equivalent to proving that all
irreducible C-representations of G are 1-dimensional. By Theorem 4.7.8, an irreducible
representation can have dimension 1, p, or p2 . But Theorem 4.2.11 implies that it can not
be p2 or p (because there always exists the 1-dimensional trivial representation).

   When G has order p3 , we can predict the degrees of its irreducible representations.
Proposition 4.7.13. Let G be a non-abelian group of order p3 . Among the irreducible
C-representations of G, p2 of them have dimension 1 and p − 1 of them have dimension
p.
Proof. We know the following facts:
   • Z(G) is non-trivial; moreover, G/Z(G) can not be cyclic, otherwise G would be
     abelian. Thus |Z(G)| = p and G/Z(G) ∼  = Z/pZ × Z/pZ (note that it is always
     abelian by Proposition 4.7.12).

   • if g ∈ G, sending h to h−1 gh induces a bijection between G/ZG (g) and Cg , so |Cg |
                                                          / Z(G), then |Cg | = p or p2 . But
     divides |G| = p3 . If g ∈ Z(G), then |Cg | = 1. If g ∈
     ZG (g) always contains Z(G) ∪ {g}, so |ZG (g)| ≥ p + 1, consequently |Cg | = p. In
     all, there are p2 + p − 1 conjugacy classes: p of them have size 1 and p2 − 1 of them
     have size p.
On the other hand, if ni denotes the dimension of irreducible representations, then ni ∈
{1, p} by Theorem 4.7.8 together with Theorem 4.2.11(iii). Since G/Z(G) is abelian of
order p2 , we obtain p2 1-dimensional representations of G/Z(G), hence of G by inflation.
Using Theorem 4.2.11(iii), we easily deduce the result.
Example 4.7.14. Proposition 4.7.13 implies that a (non-abelian) group of order 8, like
D4 or Q8 , has 4 irreducible representations of dimension 1 and 1 irreducible representation
of dimension 2.

                                            140
4.8       Supersolvable groups
Definition 4.8.1. A finite group is called supersolvable if there exists an ascending chain

                                  {1} = G0 ⊊ G1 ⊊ · · · ⊊ Gn = G

of normal subgroups Gi ◁ G, such that Gi /Gi−1 is cyclic for all i.

Example 4.8.2.           • Abelian groups are supersolvable;

       • Dn is supersolvable;

       • subgroups and quotients of a supersolvable group are still supersolvable.

Remark 4.8.3. For a solvable group, we only require Gi−1 to be normal in Gi and Gi /Gi−1
to be abelian. As an example: A4 is solvable but not supersolvable. Indeed, A4 contains an
abelian normal subgroup V4 which is of order 4, isomorphic to Z/2Z × Z/2Z (in particular
not cyclic). But A4 has no (proper) cyclic normal subgroups.

Lemma 4.8.4. Let G be a non-abelian supersolvable group. There exists a normal abelian
subgroup which is not contained in the center Z(G) of G.

Proof. The group Ḡ := G/Z(G) is again supersolvable. Take Ḡ1 to be the first subgroup
in the ascending chain, which is a cyclic group. Let A be the inverse image in G of Ḡ1 .
Then A contains (properly) Z(G), and is normal abelian (since Z(G) commutes with a
lifting of the generator of Ḡ1 ).

Theorem 4.8.5. Assume F is algebraically closed. Let G be a supersolvable group. Then
every irreducible representation of G over F is induced from a 1-dimensional representa-
tion of some subgroup.

Proof. Let (V, ρ) be an irreducible representation of G over F . If G is abelian, the result
is obvious by Corollary 4.2.17. So we assume G is non-abelian in the rest of the proof.
    First, we may assume ker(ρ) = {1}, i.e. ρ is faithful.(47) Indeed, the result is true for
G/ ker(ρ), then there exists H/ ker(ρ) ⊂ G/ ker(ρ) and χ : H/ ker(ρ) → F × such that

Remark 4.8.6. A finite group is called monomial, if its complex irreducible representa-
tions are all monomial, i.e. induced from characters of degree 1. It is a theorem of Taketa
(1930) that a monomial group is solvable; and supersolvable groups are monomial. But
the converse is not true: S4 is monomial but not supersolvable.
    Since An is not solvable for n ≥ 5, so An , Sn are not monomial for n ≥ 5 by Taketa’s
theorem.
Example 4.8.7. Take G = A5 . It has 5 irreducible representations, and the character
table is as follows:
                             C1 C2 C3 C4            C5
                        χ1 1      1    1     1√      1√
                                           1+ 5    1− 5
                        χ2 3 −1 0            2√      2√
                        χ3 3 −1 0 1−2 5 1+2 5
                        χ4 4      0    1    −1      −1
                        χ5 5      1 −1       0       0
where C1 = {Id}, C2 = {(12)(34), ...}, C3 = {(123), ...}, C4 = {(12345), ...}, C5 =
{(21345), ...}.
    Any proper subgroup of A5 has order ≤ 12 (exercise!). Hence, χ2 , χ3 , χ4 are not
monomial representations. On the other hand, A4 < A5 is a subgroup of order 12, and
χ5 is induced from a 1-dimensional representation of A4 . In fact, take ψ : A4 → C× be a
non-trivial character, and we claim that IndA A4 ψ is irreducible, hence is isomorphic to ρ5
                                               5


(the unique irreducible representation of A5 of degree 5). Indeed, by Frobenius reciprocity,
ρ1 = 1 does not occur in the decomposition of IndA                                      A5
                                                     A4 ψ (as ψ is non-trivial). As IndA4 ψ
                                                       5


has dimension 5, it must be isomorphic to ρ5 for dimension reasons.


4.9    Induction theorems
We assume F = C in this section. Let V1 , . . . , Vr be the complete list of irreducible
representations of G, with χi = χVi the characters. We may view χi as class functions on
G. Let W be any representation of G, we know that W = ri=1 Vimi with mi ∈ N so that
                                                         L

                                            r
                                            X
                                     χW =         mi χi .
                                            i=1


                                            142
Recall that W ⊗ W ′ is also a representation of G, with character χW χW ′ .

Definition 4.9.1. We let R(G) be the ring

                                 R(G) := Zχ1 ⊕ · · · ⊕ Zχr ,

with natural addition and multiplication, viewing R(G) as a subring of cf(G). To see that
it is a unital ring, just note that χi χj is the character corresponding to Vi ⊗ Vj , hence can
be written as an integral linear combination of χi ; the trivial character χ1 is the unit of
R(G).
     An element in R(G) is called a generalized character (or virtual character) of G.

   For H ⊂ G, we have natural maps

   • the restriction R(G) → R(H);

   • the induction R(H) → R(G): φ 7→ IndG                    G                           −1
                                                                     P
                                                H φ, here IndH φ :=    t̄∈G/H,g t̄=t̄ φ(t gt)
     (this generalizes the formula for characters of induced representations).

Moreover, the equality Ind(φResψ) = Ind(φ)ψ (see Proposition 4.5.12) for φ ∈ R(H) and
ψ ∈ R(G) shows that the image of Ind : R(H) → R(G) is an ideal of R(G).

Definition 4.9.2. Let H be a class of subgroups in G. Let RH (G) be the ideal of R(G)
generated by the image of IndG
                             H for H ∈ H.

   We will consider two classes of subgroups of G: elementary subgroups and quasi-
elementary subgroups.

4.9.1   Elementary groups and quasi-elementary groups
Definition 4.9.3. Let p be a prime number. We call G a p-elementary group if it is of
the form G = C × P where C is a cyclic group of order prime to p and P is a p-group
(i.e. |P | = pn for some n).
    We call G elementary if it is p-elementary for some prime p.

Definition 4.9.4. A group G is called p-quasi-elementary if it is a semi-direct product
C ⋊ P with C and P as above.
   We call G quasi-elementary if it is p-quasi-elementary for some prime p.

   Of course, p-elementary groups are p-quasi-elementary groups.

Lemma 4.9.5. In a p-quasi-elementary group G, the subgroup C is unique. Precisely,
C = Gp′ := {x ∈ G : o(x) is prime to p}.
   Conversely, if Gp′ is a cyclic subgroup of G, then G is p-quasi-elementary.

   Note that Gp′ need not be a subgroup in general.


                                             143
Proof. We choose a presentation G = C ⋊ P . We claim that Gp′ = C: C ⊂ Gp′ is clear;
for the conserve, if x ∈ Gp′ , the image of x in G/C ∼
                                                     = P is still an element of order prime
to p, but P is a p-group, so x̄ = 1.
    Conversely, if Gp′ is a subgroup, then it must be normal as any element in gxg −1 also
has order prime to p. Assume Gp′ is moreover cyclic, and let P be any Sylow p-subgroup
of G, then G = Gp′ ⋊ P is quasi-elementary.

Proposition 4.9.6. (i) If G is p-elementary (resp. p-quasi-elementary), then so is any
subgroup of G.
   (ii) If G ⊂ G′ with G p-elementary (resp. p-quasi-elementary), then so is any conju-
gate of H.

Proof. (i) Let H be a subgroup of G. We have Hp′ = H ∩ Gp′ . If G is p-quasi-elementary,
then Hp′ is also cyclic normal in H and H/Hp′ < G/Gp′ is a p-group, thus H is p-quasi-
elementary.
   If G is p-elementary, then P is its unique Sylow p-subgroup. For any Sylow p-subgroup
of H, it is contained in P , therefore HP := H ∩ P is a Sylow p-subgroup of H. Since Hp′
commutes with HP , one gets H = Hp′ × HP is p-elementary.
   (ii) If G = C ⋊ P , then both xCx−1 and xP x−1 are subgroups of xGx−1 . We have
xCx−1 is the set of elements in xGx−1 of order prime to p and it is cyclic since C is.
This allows us to show xGx−1 is also p-quasi-elementary. If moreover G = C × P , then
xGx−1 = (xCx−1 ) × (xP x−1 ).

Remark 4.9.7. Quasi-elementary groups are supersolvable. This is because a finite p-
group is supersolvable. In fact, any finite p-group has a non-trivial center, by counting its
orbits. Considering successively G/Z(G), we get the result. Since C is normal cyclic, the
pullback of a chain Pi ⊊ Pi+1 ⊊ · · · of P (with Pi ◁ P ) provides one for G.

Example 4.9.8. Given a p-quasi-elementary group G = C ⋊ P , we set Z := {g ∈
H | gy = yg, ∀y ∈ P }, then Z × P is a p-elementary subgroup of G.
   For example, for G = S3 , C = A3 , P = ⟨(12)⟩, we have G = C ⋊ P is 2-quasi-
elementary, and Z = {1} so that H = P .

Lemma 4.9.9 (Isaacs). Let G = C ⋊ P be a p-quasi-elementary group. Define Z as
in Example 4.9.8, so that H := Z × P is a p-elementary subgroup of G. Suppose that
λ : G → F × is a character which is trivial on H (i.e. H ⊂ ker λ). Then λ itself is trivial.

Example 4.9.10. As an example, if λ : S3 → F × is a character trivial on P = ⟨(12)⟩,
then it is also trivial on any conjugate of P , hence trivial on the whole S3 .

Proof. It suffices to show C ⊂ ker λ. Let K = C ∩ ker λ which is a normal subgroup of
G. Then λ induces an injective homomorphism C/K ,→ F × . If y ∈ P , then

                          λ(ycy −1 ) = λ(y) · λ(c) · λ(y)−1 = λ(c),

so y(ck)y −1 lies in the coset cK. In particular, P acts on each coset cK.

                                            144
    Since P is a p-group and |cK| = |K| is prime to p, by Lemma 4.9.11 below we have
a fixed point ck ∈ cK under this action, i.e. ck ∈ Z for some k ∈ K. Since λ|Z = 1 and
λ(k) = 1, we get λ(c) = 1. The choice of c being arbitrary, the result follows.

Lemma 4.9.11. If P is a finite p-group acting on is a finite set X. Then

                              |X| ≡ ♯{fixed points} (mod p).

In particular, if |X| is prime to p, then there is a fixed point under this action.
                                                                                   `
Proof. For each x ∈ X, let Ox be the orbit of x under P -action. Then X = orbit Ox .
Each Ox has a p-power order: let Hx be the stablizer of x, then |Ox | = [P : Hx ]. Moreover,
x is a fixed point iff Ox = {x} iff Hx = P . We get

                              |X| ≡ ♯{fixed points}    mod p.

Since |X| is prime to p, there exists at least one fixed point.

Theorem 4.9.12. Let G be a quasi-elementary group. Any irreducible character of G is
an integral linear combination of characters induced from linear characters of elementary
subgroups of G.

     Here, by linear character we mean the character of a one-dimensional representation;
it is just a group homomorphism G → F × .

Proof. We do induction on |G|. Recall that G is then supersolvable, hence any irre-
ducible representation V of G is induced from a one-dimensional representation W of
some subgroup, say H. Moreover, dim V > 1 if and only if H ⊊ G. Since H is also quasi-
elementary, we may apply the inductive hypothesis to (H, W ) to express χW as an integral
linear combination of characters induced linear characters of elementary subgroups of H.
Since taking induction is transitive, we get an expression for χV .
    We assume dim V = 1 in the rest, i.e. χ = χV is itself a linear character. Write
G = C ⋊ P , and let Z ⊂ C be the centralizer of P in C; i.e.

                             Z := {g ∈ C | gy = yg, ∀ y ∈ P }.

Then Z × P =: H < G is elementary. If G = H, then G is itself elementary, and there is
nothing to prove. Assume H ⊊ G and consider IndG
                                               H χ (here we view χ as a representation
of H). Claim:
                               IndG
                                  H χ = χ ⊕ (⊕i Wi )

with dim Wi > 1. If not, let χ′ be another one-dimensional sub-representation of IndG
                                                                                    H χ.
                                             ′                                ′
Then Frobenius reciprocity shows HomH (χ |H , χ|H ) ̸= 0; but both χ and χ are one-
dimensional, hence χ′ |H = χ|H . Then χ′ χ−1 is a character of G which is trivial on H.
Lemma 4.9.9 implies that χ′ χ−1 is trivial on the whole G, i.e. χ′ = χ.


                                            145

subgroups of G. Since                          X

4.9.2   Solomon’s induction theorem
Theorem 4.9.14 (Solomon). The trivial representation is an integral linear combina-
tion of characters induced from the trivial characters on quasi-elementary subgroups. In
particular, RH′ (G) = R(G), where H′ is the set of quasi-elementary subgroups of G.

   We start with several lemmas.

intersections. Then the Z-linear combinations of IndG

Lemma 4.9.16 (Banaschewski). Let A be a (possibly non-unital) subring of the ring of
Z-valued functions on a finite set X under pointwise addition and multiplication. If 1 ∈
                                                                                       / A,
then for some x ∈ X and prime p, we have

                                f (x) ≡ 0 (mod p) ∀f ∈ A.

Remark 4.9.17. The general form of this lemma says: if R is a commutative ring, and
A is a possibly non-unital subring. Assume that A is not contained in any of a finite list
of ideals I1 , ..., In , at most two of which are not prime, then A is not contained in their
union.



                                             146
Proof. For each x ∈ X we get a map evx : A → Z; the image evx (A) is a subgroup of
Z, hence is of the form nx Z for some nx ∈ Z≥1 . Suppose on the contrary that for every
x ∈ X we have nx = 1. Let fx ∈ A be an element so that fx (x) = 1; consider the product

                                         (1X − fx ).
                                     Y

                                       x∈X

This vanishes at every x ∈ X, so vanishes identically. But expanding the product we see
1X ∈ A.
Proof of Solomon’s theorem. We apply Lemma 4.9.16 to the ring A generated by the
character of IndGH 1 for H running over quasi-elementary subgroups, viewed as functions
on X = G. Assume 1G ∈    / A, we get some g ∈ G and some prime p such that χIndGH 1 (g) ≡ 0
mod p for every H ∈ H. Hence, to show the result, it is enough to show that for each
g ∈ G and each prime p, there is a quasi-elementary subgroup Hg,p such that χIndGH 1 (g) ̸=
                                                                                  g,p
0 (mod p).
    We decompose ⟨g⟩ = C × ⟨g⟩p with |C| prime to p. Let N = NG (C) be the normalizer
of C in G and let P ⊂ N be a Sylow p-subgroup. Then H := C ⋊P is a p-quasi-elementary
subgroup.
    Recall that

          χIndGH 1 (g) = #{t̄ ∈ G/H : t−1 gt ∈ H} = #{t̄ ∈ G/H : g(tH) = tH}.

If t−1 gt ∈ H, then also t−1 Ct ⊂ H as elements of C are all powers of g. But inside H,
t−1 Ct = Hp′ = C, so one gets t ∈ N by definition. That is χIndGH 1 (g) = χIndNH 1 (g), and
more importantly, the values are equal to the number of fixed points for the natural action
of ⟨g⟩ on N/H. Since N = NG (C), for any t ∈ N we have t−1 Ct = C ⊂ H, so C acts
trivially on N/H, so the action factors through ⟨g⟩/C which is a p-group. By Lemma
4.9.11,
                               |N/H| ≡ χIndNH 1 (g) (mod p).
Since N/H has order prime to p, we get χIndNH 1 (g) ̸= 0 mod p.

4.9.3   Brauer’s induction theorem
Theorem 4.9.18 (Brauer). Let H be the set of elementary subgroups of G. Then R(G) =
RH (G).

Proof. First, since RH (G) is an ideal, it suffices to show 1G ∈ RH (G). By Solomon’s
theorem, 1G = H ′ ∈H′ cH ′ χIndG ′ 1 . Then using Theorem 4.9.12, we can write each 1H ′ as
                P

an integral linear combination of characters induced from linear characters of elementary
subgroups of H ′ . By transitivity of induction, we get an expression for 1G .

Example 4.9.20. In general, even for irreducible representation V of G, the coefficients
cξ could be negative. To see this, just take a non-solvable group and an irreducible repre-

4.10     Rationality
Let F ⊂ C be a subfield. If V is a linear F -representation of G, we denote by VC the
C-representation V ⊗F C, obtained by extending scalars from F to C. In general, even
when V is irreducible, VC can be reducible.
     A linear C-representation of G is said to be realizable over F (or rational over F ) if
it is isomorphic to V ⊗F C, where V is a representation of G defined over F .
     Let F be a subfield of C. If ρ is a C-representation of G, a necessary condition for ρ
to be realizable over F is that χρ (g) ∈ F for all g ∈ G. In general, this is not sufficient.

Example 4.10.1. Take G = Q8 and let ρ5 be the unique irreducible 2-dimensional C-
representation of G, see §4.4.5. Then its character is given by

                              {1} {−1} {i, −i} {j, −j} {k, −k}
                        χ5     2   −2      0       0       0

In particular, χ5 (g) ∈ Q for all g ∈ G, but ρ5 is not realizable over Q (or over R).

   We denote by RF (G) the group generated by the characters of the F -representations
of G. It is a subring of R(G) in Definition 4.9.1 (for F = C). Moreover, if V1 , . . . , Vs are
the irreducible representations of G over F , then the χi := χVi form a Z-basis of RF (G)
and they are mutually orthogonal. However, in general, ⟨χi , χi ⟩ need not be equal to 1.

Lemma 4.10.2. Let V be a C-representation of G. In order that V be realizable over F ,
it is necessary and sufficient that χV ∈ RF (G).

Proof. The condition is clearly necessary. Conversely, we can write χV = si=1 ni χi with
                                                                        P

ni ∈ Z. We obtain:
                                    ⟨χV , χi ⟩ = ni ⟨χi , χi ⟩.
Note that ⟨χV , χi ⟩ ≥ 0 (this can be seen by taking base change to C), so ni ≥ 0. Thus
V = ⊕si=1 Vi⊕ni is realizable over F .

                                             148
Theorem 4.10.3 (Brauer). If F contains the m-th roots of unity, then any C-representation
of G can be realized over F .
                                                    P
Proof. By Brauer induction theorem, any χV = i ni χIndGH ξi where Hi are elementary
                                                              i
subgroups, and ξi is a linear character of Hi . Since the exponent of Hi is a divisor of m,
ξi : Hi → C× takes values in F × as F contains all m-th roots of unity. Thus, IndG   Hi ξi is
realizable over F and χIndGH ξi ∈ RF (G). We deduce χV ∈ RF (G) and the result follows
                            i
from Lemma 4.10.2.


4.11      Representations of symmetric groups
4.11.1    Review on symmetric groups
Let Sn be the symmetric group acting on X = {1, . . . , n}. Any σ ∈ Sn can be represented
as a product of disjoint cycles. A cycle is written in the form

                                            (k1 · · · km )

where ki ∈ X (unique up to cyclic permutations). The integer m is called the length of
the cycle. Recall some useful properties.

   • Disjoint cycles commute in Sn .

   • Every element σ ∈ Sn can be written as a product σ = τ1 · · · τr , with τi disjoint
     cycles; if mi denotes the length of τi , {m1 , . . . , mr } is called the cycle shape of σ.

   • if σ ∈ Sn , τ = (k1 · · · km ) is a cycle, then

                                       στ σ −1 = (σ(k1 ) · · · σ(km ))

       is again a cycle of length m.

   • As a consequence, if σ ∼ σ ′ (conjugate) then they have the same cycle shape. The
     converse is also true. Hence the conjugacy classes in Sn are in bijection with the
     cycle shapes, also in bijection with the partitions of n. Here a partition λ of n is

                        λ = (λ ≥ λ2 ≥ · · · ≥ λr ) = (λ1 , λ2 , · · · , λr ), λi ≥ 1

       such that λ1 + · · · + λr = n. We writ λ ⊢ n to mean that λ is a partition of n.

   Take F = Q. For each λ ⊢ n, we will construct an irreducible representation of Sn
over Q, called Specht module S λ .




                                                149
4.11.2   Young diagrams
Definition 4.11.1. A Young diagram is an array of boxes with nonincreasing row length.

Example 4.11.2.
                                      3 ⊣ (2, 1) =


                              11 ⊣ (5, 4, 2) =



Definition 4.11.3. Let λ ⊢ n be a partition. A Young tableau of shape λ consists of

   • the Young diagram corresponding to λ and

   • a way to fill the integers 1, . . . , n in the n boxes.

   The group Sn acts on the set of Young tableaux of shape λ pointwisely: action denoted
by ⋆. The action is transitive: for any t, t′ , there exists g ∈ Sn such that t′ = g ⋆ t.

Example 4.11.4.
                                    (13) ⋆ 2     1 = 2         3
                                           3         1

                                2    3   5 10 7            2   3   8 10 7
                     (1589) ⋆                          =                       .
                                1    8   4   6             5   9   4   6
                                9 11                       1 11

Definition 4.11.5. A tabloid is a (row) equivalence class of Young tableaux, where t ∼ t′ if
for each row, the set of values coincide. Denote by {t} the equivalence class corresponding
to t.

Example 4.11.6.

                    1   2   3 ∼ 3        1   2 ∼ 1         3   2 ∼ 3       2   1
                    4   5       5        4       4         5       5       4

In fact, there are 3!2! = 12 tableaux in this equivalence class.

Lemma 4.11.7. Given a shape λ = (λ1 ≥ λ2 · · · ≥ λr ) ⊢ n.
    (i) let t be a Young tableau and Rt := {σ ∈ Sn : σ ⋆ t ∼ t} (row stabilizer). Then Rt
is isomorphic to Sλ1 × · · · × Sλr .
                                                                    Q
    (ii) The number of Young tableaux equivalent to a given one is i λi !.

Proof. Clear.

    Now we also consider the column stabilizer, denoted by Ct (column stabilizer), which

                             4              5

                               Cσ⋆t = σCt σ −1 ,    Rσ⋆t = σRt σ −1 .

Proof. Clear.

   Since the action of S4 permutes the {ti }, and is isomorphic to the natural permutation
representation of S4 acting on {1, 2, 3, 4} (looking at the second row). So

                                 f := {t1 } + {t2 } + {t3 } + {t4 }

is an F [S4 ]-submodule of M (3,1) and its quotient (or complement) is just the Standard
representation of S4 . We denote it by S (3,1) . It is easy to see that S (3,1) is characterized
by the three-dimensional subspace of vectors 4i=1 ci {ti } with 4i=1 ci = 0. It has a basis
                                                P                 P


                             {t1 } − {t2 }, {t2 } − {t3 }, {t3 } − {t4 }.

4.11.3    Specht modules
Definition 4.11.13. Let t be a tableau, the polytabloid associated to t is
                                     X
(25)                            et =     sgn(σ)σ ⋆ {t}.
                                            σ∈Ct


The Specht module S λ associated to a cycle shape λ is the module spanned by polytabloids
et where t is taken over all tableaux of shape λ ( but not direct sum), i.e.

                      S λ = F et1 + F et2 + · · · , ti tableaux of shape λ.

Example 4.11.14. We continue the example with S4 . Even for t ∼ t′ , et , et′ need not be
                                      1 2 3        2 1 3            3 2 1
equal (as Ct ̸= Ct′ !). Take t = t1 =       , t′ =        , t′′ =                . Then
                                      4            4                4


                             et = {t1 } − { 4         2     3 } = {t } − {t }
                                                                    1        2
                                            1

as Ct = {1, (14)}, and similarly et′ = {t1 } − {t3 }, et′′ = {t1 } − {t4 }. This suggests that
S (3,1) has dimension 3 and is the standard representation of S4 .

   For convenience, we write
                                           X
                                   κt :=          sgn(σ)σ ∈ F [Sn ].
                                           σ∈Ct

so that et = κt ⋆ {t}. Note that κt only depends on Ct . It has some nice properties.

Lemma 4.11.15. (i) For g ∈ Sn , gκt = κg⋆t g, equivalently κg⋆t = gκt g −1 . As a
   consequence, eg⋆t = g ⋆ et .

                                                      152
 (ii) If g ∈ Ct , then gκt = κt g = sgn(g −1 )κt .

(iii) κ2t = |Ct |κt . As a consequence, et = |C1t | κt ⋆ et (recall that char(F ) = 0).

Proof. (i) It is a direct computation:

                           sgn(σ)σ · g −1 =              sgn(σ)gσg −1
                     P                           P
               g·
                    σ∈Ct                        σ∈Ct
                                                            sgn(σ ′ )σ ′   with σ ′ = gσg −1
                                                  P
                                            =
                                                σ ′ ∈Cg⋆t
                                            = κg⋆t · g.

The last statement follows by definition: eg⋆t = κg⋆t (g ⋆ t) = (gκt ) ⋆ t = g ⋆ et .
  (ii) If g ∈ Ct , then

                            sgn(σ)σ = sgn(g −1 )
                     P                                    P
                g·                                              sgn(gσ)gσ
                     σ∈Ct                                σ∈Ct
                                      = sgn(g −1 )                 sgn(σ ′ )σ ′ with σ ′ = gσ

The other assertion is proved in a similar way.
  (iii) It is a consequence of (ii):
                                X                 X
                          κ2t =      sgn(g)κt g =   κt = |Ct |κt .
                                     g∈Ct                    g∈Ct


Lemma 4.11.16. The Specht module S λ is cyclic. In fact, for any polytabloid et , we have
S λ = F [Sn ] · et .

Proof. By definition S λ is generated by all eti for ti of shape λ. But each ti is of the form
g ⋆ t for some g ∈ Sn , we conclude by Lemma 4.11.15(i).

4.11.4    The dominance order
Let λ, µ ⊢ n. Let t, s be tableaux of shape λ and µ respectively.

Lemma 4.11.17. If Ct contains a transposition τ , then κt ∈ F [Sn ]·(1−τ ). If τ ∈ Ct ∩Rs ,
then κt ⋆ {s} = 0.
                                              `
Proof. Consider the coset decomposition Ct = i σi H where H := {1, τ } is viewed as a
subgroup of Ct . Then
            X              X               X                X
       κt =      sgn(σ)σ =    sgn(σi )σi −   sgn(σi )σi τ =     sgn(σi )σi · (1 − τ ).
            σ∈Ct                 i                   i                         i


The condition τ ∈ Rs means that (1 − τ ) ⋆ {s} = 0, hence the result.




                                                   153
Definition 4.11.18. Given λ = (λ1 ≥ · · · ) and µ = (µ1 ≥ · · · ) such that λ, µ ⊢ n, we
say λ ▷ µ (called dominance order) if
                              X        X
                                  λi ≥     µi , ∀k ≥ 1.
                                   i≤k       i≤k

Here we set λi = µi = 0 for i sufficiently larege.

   This defines a partial order on the set of partitions of n (not every pair (λ, µ) is
comparable with respect to ▷). If λ ▷ µ and µ ▷ λ then λ = µ.

Example 4.11.19. Take n = 7, λ = (3, 1, 1, 1, 1) and µ = (2, 2, 2, 1). Then λ and µ are
not comparable.

Lemma 4.11.20. Let λ, µ ⊢ n. Let t, s be tableaux of shape λ and µ respectively. If
κt ⋆ {s} ̸= 0, then λ ▷ µ.

Proof. Since κt ⋆ {s} ̸= 0, for any b, c in the same row of s, they cannot lie in the same
column of t. We claim that this implies λ ▷ µ. Prove for example λ1 ≥ µ1 . Write
µ = b1 · · · bµ1 , then b must lie in different column, so that λ , the number of columns
 1                          i                                        1
of t, is bigger than µ1 .

     In the next lemma, we specialize to the case λ = µ.

Lemma 4.11.21. Let s, t be two tableaux of shape λ. Assume κt ⋆ {s} ̸= 0. Then
  (i) there exists g ∈ Ct such that {s} = g ⋆ {t};
  (ii) κt ⋆ {s} = ±et . In particular, κt defines an F -linear projection from M λ to F et .

Proof. (i) Since κt ⋆ {s} ̸= 0, Ct ∩ Rs does not contain any transposition (bc) by Lemma
4.11.17. So for any b, c in the same row of s, they can not lie in the same column of
t. Consider the first row of s, say b1 b2 . . . , then the b lie in different columns of t
                                                                 i
(although not necessarily the first row of t), we can then find an element g ∈ Ct such that
it sends bi to the first row of t, i.e. the set of values appearing in the first row of s and
of g ⋆ t coincide (here we use that λ, µ are of the same shape). Note that Cg⋆t = Ct (as
g ∈ Ct ), and κg⋆t ⋆ {s} = κt ⋆ {s} ̸= 0 (as κg⋆t = κt ), so we can continue the procedure
row by row to get an element in Ct which sends {t} to {s}.
    (ii) Using (i), we may write {s} = g ⋆ {t} for some g ∈ Ct . By Lemma 4.11.15 we have

                      κt ⋆ {s} = (κt · g) ⋆ {t} = sgn(g −1 )κt ⋆ {t} = ±et .

Recall that {s : shape λ} form a basis of M λ . For this basis, we have either κt ⋆ {s} = 0
or κt ⋆ {s} = ±et . The result follows from this.




                                               154
4.11.5    Irreducibility of S λ
Theorem 4.11.22. (i) The Specht module S λ is absolutely irreducible for any λ ⊢ n.
    (ii) HomSn (S λ , S µ ) ̸= 0 if and only if λ = µ.
    (iii) The Specht modules S λ (for λ ⊢ n) form a complete set of isomorphism classes
of irreducible representations of Sn over F .

Proof. (i) Since char(F ) = 0, S λ is absolutely irreducible if and only if EndSn (S λ ) = F .
Let φ ∈ EndSn (S λ ) and t be a tableau of shape λ. By Lemma 4.11.21 and Lemma 4.11.15,
                                       1              1
                         φ(et ) = φ         κt ⋆ et =       κt ⋆ φ(et ) ∈ F et ,
                                      |Ct |           |Ct |

i.e. φ(et ) = cet for some c ∈ F . Since S λ is a cyclic F [Sn ]-module generated by et , we get
φ = c.
     (ii) Let φ ∈ HomSn (S λ , S µ ) be non-zero. If t is a tableau of shape λ, then φ(et ) ̸= 0
since et generates S λ . We then have
                                                     1
                                    0 ̸= φ(et ) =         κt ⋆ φ(et ).
                                                    |Ct |
                 P
Write φ(et ) = i ci {si } for si of shape µ. Then κt ⋆ {si } ̸= 0 for at least one si . This
already implies λ ▷ µ. On the other hand, since both S λ and S µ are irreducible, φ induces
an isomorphism between S λ and S µ . Apply the same argument to φ−1 we obtain µ ▷ λ,
so λ = µ.
    (iii) Since the number of irreducible representations of Sn is equal to the number of
conjugacy classes in Sn , which is again equal to the number of partitions of n. So we have
constructed all absolutely irreducible representations, up to isomorphism.

Theorem 4.11.23. For µ ⊢ n, M µ = λ▷µ (S λ )⊕κλ,µ , with κµ,µ = 1.
                                         L

Proof. Given µ ⊢ n, we can decompose M µ as a direct sum of S λ , with multiplicities
denoted by κλ,µ . If S λ appears in the decomposition, then

                                       HomSn (S λ , M µ ) ̸= 0.

The same argument as in Theorem 4.11.22(ii) shows that λ ▷ µ. To prove κµ,µ = 1, it
suffices to prove
                           dimF HomSn (S µ , M µ ) = 1.
The proof is similar to that of Theorem 4.11.22(i).

   Now we explain, without proof, how to determine the numbers κλ,µ , which are called
Kostka numbers. Write µ = {µ1 , . . . , µs }. A Young tableau of shape λ and weight µ
consists of a Young diagram and a way to fill {1, . . . , 1, 2, . . . , 2, . . . , s, . . . , s} (with j
appearing µj times). It is called semi-standard, if the values are non-decreasing for each
row and strictly increasing for each column.

                                                  155
Theorem 4.11.24. For λ ▷ µ, κλ,µ is equal to the number of semi-standard tableaux of
shape λ and weight µ.

Example 4.11.25. If n = 5 and λ = (3, 2) ▷ µ = (2, 1, 1, 1), there are three semi-
standard ways to fill (1, 1, 2, 3, 4) in the Young diagram of shape λ:

                            1   1   2       1     1    3         1   1   4
                                        ,                    ,
                            3   4           2     4              2   3

hence κλ,µ = 3.

Remark 4.11.26. In general, there are no nice formulas known for the Kostka numbers.
However, some special cases are known. For example, if µ = (1, 1, . . . , 1) is the partition
whose parts are all 1 then a semistandard Young tableau of weight µ is a standard Young
tableau; the number of standard Young tableaux of a given shape λ is given by the hook-
length formula. See Theorem 4.11.31 below.

4.11.6   Dimension of S λ
We fix a partition λ ⊢ n.

Definition 4.11.27. A Young tableau is called standard if for each row and each column,
the values are ordered increasingly.

   Example of standard tableau:
                                            1    3     5 .
                                            2    4
Example of non-standard tableau:
                                            1    4     5 .
                                            2    3

Theorem 4.11.28. The set {et : t standard} form a basis for S λ . In particular, the
dimension of S λ is equal to the number of standard Young tableaux.

Definition 4.11.29. The hook-length of a given entry (i, j) in a Young diagram of shape
λ, denoted hi,j , is the number of entries (i′ , j) with i′ ≥ i, or (i, j ′ ) with j ′ ≥ j.

Example 4.11.30. Let 9 ⊣ λ ↔                            , then h1,1 = 6, h1,2 = 5.



Theorem 4.11.31 (Hook-length formula). The dimension of S λ is given by

                                                 n!
                                            Q            .
                                                i,j hi,j

   • λ=             : dim M (2,2) = 6, dim S (2,2) = 2.



   • λ=             : dim M (2,1,1) = 12, dim S (2,1,1) = 3.




   • λ=        : dim M (1,1,1,1) = 24, dim S (1,1,1,1) = 1.




Using the description of Kostka numbers, one checks that

                               M (4) = S (4) ,    M (3,1) ∼
                                                          = S (3,1) ⊕ S (4) ,

       M (2,2) = S (2,2) ⊕ S (4) ⊕ S (3,1) , M (2,1,1) ∼
                                                       = S (2,1,1) ⊕ S (4) ⊕ (S (3,1) )⊕2 ⊕ S (2,2) .
Finally, M (1,1,1,1) is just the regular representation of S4 , hence
